# Desktop GUI developer preview

The desktop GUI under `desktop/` is an opt-in Tauri preview for PRD #176. It is a second local client of the existing daemon, not a replacement for the TUI, and it is not included in the default release artifacts. The current M0/M1 spike deliberately depends on the root `dot-agent-deck` library before the protocol crate is extracted so that terminal transport and the control-deck interaction model can be tested first.

## Prerequisites

- Enter `devbox shell` for the repository's pinned toolchain: Rust 1.97.1, `cargo-nextest`, Clippy, rustfmt, Node.js 24.12.0, and pnpm 11.22.0. Provide equivalent versions yourself if you do not use Devbox — the frontend needs Node.js 20.19 or newer, and a pnpm that reads `desktop/pnpm-lock.yaml`'s `lockfileVersion: '9.0'` without rewriting it. Note that **nothing tests that Node floor**: CI's `desktop-web` job pins Node 24 and pnpm 11, matching the Devbox pins rather than the stated minimum. (Corrected 2026-09-04: this line claimed pnpm 10.34.5 and a `desktop-web` job deliberately running Node 20 so the floor "stays tested rather than merely claimed" — both were false, and the second inverted what the job actually establishes.) Devbox supplies Node with its **plugin disabled** (`disable_plugin: true` on the `nodejs` entry); that removes a corepack shim this repository never used and nothing else — see [devbox and concurrent starts](devbox-concurrency.md).
- Install the [Tauri 2 system prerequisites](https://v2.tauri.app/start/prerequisites/) for your platform. On macOS this includes the Xcode command-line tools. On Linux a `devbox shell` already carries them — see [Linux system libraries](#linux-system-libraries) below — and only a non-Devbox Linux setup installs WebKitGTK and the related `-dev` packages by hand.
- Install the desktop JavaScript dependencies once:

```sh
cd desktop
pnpm install
```

Agent CLIs and their credentials are needed only for agents you deliberately start through the daemon. The fixture preview does not call an LLM, execute an agent command, or modify project files.

### Linux system libraries

This is not optional reading on Linux, because `desktop/src-tauri` is a **workspace member**: both gates CLAUDE.md mandates carry `--workspace`, so `cargo clippy --workspace --all-targets --features e2e -- -D warnings` and `cargo test-fast` build this crate whether or not you are working on the GUI. Without GTK 3, WebKitGTK and glib they fail — the first at build time (`The system library 'glib-2.0' required by crate 'glib-sys' was not found`), the second at run time (`libgdk-3.so.0: cannot open shared object file`). Issue #771.

**In a `devbox shell` there is nothing to install.** `devbox.json` carries a `path:tauri-deps#tauri-deps` entry, and `tauri-deps/flake.nix` builds the transitive pkg-config closure of the same libraries `ci.yml`'s `build` job installs with apt. `pkg-config@0.29.2` is pinned there too, so resolution does not depend on the host having one. Nothing else is exported — in particular `LD_LIBRARY_PATH` stays unset.

**What actually makes pkg-config find the store, corrected by issue #815.** It is the *package entry*, not `devbox.json`'s `env` block. `pkg-config@0.29.2` puts Nix's pkg-config **wrapper** on PATH, and that wrapper discards the caller's `PKG_CONFIG_PATH` outright — it re-execs the real binary with `PKG_CONFIG_PATH` replaced by a role-mangled variable its own setup hook builds from the profile (`PKG_CONFIG_PATH_FOR_TARGET` here). Measured: with `PKG_CONFIG_PATH` pointed at a directory holding a hand-written `.pc`, `pkg-config --modversion` for that module fails, while the same value in `PKG_CONFIG_PATH_FOR_TARGET` resolves. So the `env` block is a fallback for a *non-wrapper* pkg-config and a no-op the rest of the time; do not reach for it when diagnosing a resolution problem, and do not read its value as evidence of anything.

**Outside Devbox, on a distribution toolchain, plain apt is enough.** The Debian/Ubuntu set is the one CI installs:

```sh
sudo apt-get install -y libwebkit2gtk-4.1-dev libgtk-3-dev \
  libayatana-appindicator3-dev librsvg2-dev libxdo-dev libasound2-dev
```

The last one is not Tauri's. PRD #802 M7 declares `cpal` in this crate for microphone capture, and on Linux its `alsa-sys` build script resolves `alsa` through pkg-config — so without `libasound2-dev` the crate does not build at all, which is issue #771's failure arriving through a different dependency. macOS needs nothing for CoreAudio and Windows nothing for WASAPI, so the edit is Linux-shaped. Four places declare this set and they are meant to stay in step: `tauri-deps/flake.nix` (the devbox shell), both `apt-get` blocks in `.github/workflows/ci.yml` (`build` and `e2e-deterministic`), and the bundle job's block in `.github/workflows/release.yml`.

**Do not mix the two.** `apt-get install` inside a `devbox shell` looks like it should work and does not, which is what made issue #771 expensive rather than merely annoying:

- A devbox shell runs under **Nix glibc**, whose loader cache does not exist — `ldconfig -p` returns *zero* entries. Libraries under `/usr/lib` are therefore invisible to the dynamic linker no matter what apt put on disk, so `pkg-config` can report `glib-2.0` present, the build can succeed, and the test binary still dies at `libgdk-3.so.0: cannot open shared object file`.
- The obvious second workaround breaks something else. `export LD_LIBRARY_PATH=/usr/lib/x86_64-linux-gnu` does make both Rust gates pass, and then Nix's own `node` fails with `undefined symbol: uv_tcp_keepalive_ex` because it picks up the system `libuv` ahead of its own. `pnpm install`, `pnpm build` and `pnpm test` are all unusable while that variable is set.

The fix avoids `LD_LIBRARY_PATH` entirely rather than pointing it somewhere safer, and the reason is worth knowing if you touch `tauri-deps/flake.nix`: each `.pc` file names absolute `/nix/store` paths, so the linker is invoked with `-L/nix/store/…-gtk+3-3.24.52/lib`, and Nix's `ld` wrapper turns every in-store `-L` into a matching `-rpath`. The test binary finds its libraries through its own RUNPATH, which is per-binary and cannot leak into `node`. `LD_LIBRARY_PATH` takes precedence over `DT_RUNPATH` for *every* process in the shell, which is the same class of problem in the other direction.

### The stale-environment trap, and the check that catches it

Issue #815 is worth reading before you debug a GTK failure here, because its symptom is indistinguishable from a `devbox.json` defect and its cause is neither.

`devbox run` **reuses an already-computed devbox environment whenever one is inherited** — `DEVBOX_PATH_STACK` is the trigger, measured by stripping the `DEVBOX_*` variables one at a time — and it then neither re-installs the profile nor re-applies the `env` block. A shell entered before `devbox.json` last changed therefore keeps a profile with no `tauri-deps` and no Nix `pkg-config`, and *every nested `devbox run` inside it inherits that staleness silently*. That matters more here than it looks: this repository's orchestration runs each dispatched agent inside a devbox shell, so nested `devbox run` is the normal case rather than an exotic one.

What makes it expensive is that the degraded state reports success everywhere. The host's `pkgconf` answers instead, `pkg-config --exists gtk+-3.0` passes, `--modversion` prints a real version, the compile links clean and `cargo clippy --workspace --all-targets` goes green. Only the loader notices, tens of minutes later, because Nix's `ld` wrapper mints an `-rpath` for `-L` paths under `/nix/store` and for no others — so a `/usr/lib/x86_64-linux-gnu` answer is the exact moment the binary loses its RUNPATH, and `cargo test-fast` then dies before its first test with `libgdk-3.so.0: cannot open shared object file`.

The remedy is to leave every nested devbox shell until `DEVBOX_PATH_STACK` is unset, then `devbox install` and re-enter.

`scripts/devbox-check-gtk.sh` catches it at the point the wrong answer is given: for each of the 14 modules it asserts the resolved **`libdir` is under `/nix/store`**, not merely that the module exists. `libdir` rather than `prefix` because every one of the 14 defines it, `libxdo` reports an empty `prefix`, and `libdir` is the value that becomes the linker's `-L` flag. `scripts/devbox-smoke.sh` invokes it and CI's `devbox` job runs that — the only job that can see this regress, since every other one installs the compile set with apt and would stay green with `devbox.json` empty of GTK. `xtask/linkage-check/src/devbox_gtk_origin.rs` drives the script under a stubbed `pkg-config` in the fast tier, so weakening the assertion back to a presence check goes red on the per-task gate; that matters because a presence check is precisely what was there when #815 shipped.

Bundling a `.deb` locally needs more than the compile set — `patchelf`, `fakeroot`, `file` and `desktop-file-utils` — which nothing in this repository's gates exercises, so they are deliberately not in `devbox.json`. Install them yourself before `pnpm tauri build`.

## Fixture preview

Run the web frontend without Tauri for a deterministic, safe UI fixture:

```sh
cd desktop
pnpm dev
```

Open `http://localhost:1420/`. A normal browser defaults to fixture transport; `http://localhost:1420/?fixture=1` selects it explicitly. `?state=` picks the scenario, and the six it accepts are `connected` (the default four-agent deck), `crowded`, `empty`, `disconnected`, `error` and `fleet` — for example `http://localhost:1420/?fixture=1&state=disconnected`. An unrecognised value falls back to `connected` rather than failing. See [The connection states](#the-connection-states) for what each one is for. (It said *five* until PRD #742 M4 added `fleet`, which is the only one of the six that renders more than one deck; every other scenario is one deck, byte for byte what it was before that milestone. `fleet` began as three decks — two answering and one not — and M14 added a fourth that has not reported yet, so it now carries every degraded state the overview can draw at once.)

The fixture's **Advance fixture** control walks a fixed review → test → human-approval sequence. Terminal input, pause/resume, retry, approval, workflow ordering, and agent-profile editing affect fixture or browser-local state only.

## Live Tauri preview

The live preview requires a daemon built from the same checkout. The exact build match is intentional: the desktop refuses to attach across a protocol or build mismatch and never recycles a daemon that may own valuable running agents.

Build the matching CLI once so the desktop can resolve a sibling executable:

```sh
devbox shell
cargo build --locked --bin dot-agent-deck
```

Then start the desktop window:

```sh
devbox shell
cd desktop
pnpm tauri dev
```

Tauri selects live transport automatically and initially performs a connect-only probe. If no daemon owns the socket, use the visible **Start deck** action; daemon creation never happens merely because the window opened. The Rust core resolves only a pre-launch `DOT_AGENT_DECK_BINARY`, a sibling `dot-agent-deck` binary, or one already on `PATH`—the webview cannot supply an executable path. You may instead run `cargo run --locked --bin dot-agent-deck -- daemon serve` in another terminal and leave it running.

If another build already owns the default socket, do not stop it until you have confirmed that terminating its agents is safe; use `dot-agent-deck daemon stop` only as a deliberate lifecycle action.

To keep the preview away from your everyday deck altogether, `task run-desktop` runs it against an isolated sandbox daemon with its own `desktop.toml`, and `task run-all` runs the TUI beside it against that same sandbox daemon. [local-run.md](local-run.md) has what they isolate, what they do not, and how to stop them.

### Attaching across a contract difference (development only)

**Since [#801](https://github.com/vfarcic/dot-agent-deck/issues/801) the everyday reason to reach for this is gone**, and that is the first thing to know about it. This section used to open by saying the **build stamp** (`git describe`) had to match — which made the daemon you actually use the one daemon the preview refused to open, since every commit restamps the desktop on its next rebuild and a released daemon never matches a branch build at all. The stamp no longer classifies anything. Two builds that agree about the contract connect however far apart their stamps are, with no switch and no confirmation.

What is left refusing, above the `PROTOCOL_VERSION` floor, is a **declared contract break** between the two builds — which means the deck genuinely may read a field with a meaning this app does not intend. Set `DOT_AGENT_DECK_DESKTOP_ALLOW_BUILD_MISMATCH=1` before launching to downgrade *that* from a refusal to a warning:

```sh
DOT_AGENT_DECK_DESKTOP_ALLOW_BUILD_MISMATCH=1 pnpm tauri dev
```

The variable keeps its name for continuity with the shells and scripts that already set it; what it bypasses is the contract comparison, not a stamp comparison, and it no longer has a stamp comparison to bypass. The protocol check is unaffected and still refuses an incompatible daemon, and the mismatch is not swallowed — the connection banner keeps naming the break and both builds for the whole session. Only `1` and `true` arm it.

This is a **development** switch, not a compatibility guarantee, and it is now a sharper thing to arm than it used to be: it no longer waves through a cosmetic stamp difference — it waves through a break somebody wrote a `changelog.d/*.breaking.md` fragment for, which is the semantic break `CLAUDE.md` rule 12 describes. The contract check is what protects a daemon that owns live agents from a client that reads its state differently. Use it to inspect a daemon, not to run work you care about, and never in a packaged build.

Users get the same escape hatch without a shell, through **Connect anyway** (issue #801). It appears in the connection banner — and on the agent overview's incompatible note — only when the desktop crate reports the refusal as overridable, meaning the protocol check already passed. (The DTO field is still spelled `buildStampMismatchOnly`; it is named for what used to set it, and since #801 it is set by a declared contract break.) It is confirmation-gated, it is **session-scoped** (a process-global flag, never written to disk, so quitting the app restores the refusal), and it is offered whatever the live-agent count, which is the case a packaged `.app` could not otherwise recover from: **Replace deck** is correctly refused while agents are live, and an env var is not something a `.app` launched from Finder ever receives. Accepting it does not clear the caveat — the connection banner stays up while connected, naming both builds, for the rest of the session.

A **protocol** mismatch offers nothing. The version check runs first in `classify_handshake`, so an allowance cannot reach it, and the crate never sets the overridable flag on that path — so no screen puts a button on it either. `protocol_mismatch_never_advertises_an_override` in `desktop/src-tauri/src/daemon_bridge.rs` pins both halves.

### Compatibility is classified from the CONTRACT, not from a git tag ([#801](https://github.com/vfarcic/dot-agent-deck/issues/801))

The handshake asks three separate questions and they used to collapse into one. They are now kept apart, and the least meaningful of them no longer decides anything:

| question | mechanism | verdict |
|---|---|---|
| can we decode each other's frames at all? | `PROTOCOL_VERSION` | exact equality, checked first, the hard floor for **every** deck kind — not bypassable by an allowance or an endpoint kind |
| do we agree what the fields *mean*? | the two builds' declared contract breaks | refuses, overridably |
| may I use *this* verb? | the `Hello` reply's advertised capability set | degrades the surface, never refuses |
| are we the same build? | the `git describe` stamp | **reported, never classified** |

**Why the stamp had to stop deciding.** `git describe` names the nearest tag *reachable from HEAD*, and the release workflow applies that tag *after* the content it names has landed — so a branch cut in between describes as the previous release while being functionally the new one. Measured on #801: a branch whose `git diff v0.39.0 HEAD -- src/` was **empty** was refused with `build mismatch: desktop is 0.38.0-gfa02054-dirty, daemon is 0.39.0-g1ea0fe7`. Two identical trees, classified incompatible. Reading the release digits off that same stamp — which is what this crate did until #801, via a `0.MINOR` compatibility key — narrows the false positive to a minor boundary rather than removing it, and a minor boundary is exactly where the interesting case lives.

**What replaces it.** `daemon_protocol::CONTRACT_BREAKS` is an append-only list of the TUI↔daemon contract breaks a build declares — one entry per `changelog.d/<issue>.breaking.md` fragment, appended in the same commit. It rides every `Hello` reply as `contract_breaks`, and `classify_handshake` compares the two lists as sets: equal is compatible, and any difference is a refusal naming the breaks and which side is behind. It cannot suffer the tag-timing error, because it is content in the contract's own source rather than a tag in the commit graph, and it sees one thing a digit cannot: a break declared on an **unreleased** branch, which has no minor to move yet.

Nothing is lost by retiring the digit comparison. While `0.x`, only a *declared* break bumps the minor ([versioning.md](versioning.md)), so the digits could only ever refuse across a break somebody had already written a fragment for — which is exactly what the list holds.

**A deck that declares no list at all connects, and that is an assumption about a population rather than a deduction.** The list does not ship empty, so "a build that omits the field is at our contract by construction" would be false. What holds is narrower: `PROTOCOL_VERSION` must match for the comparison to be reached, so a deck landing here is a protocol-9 build older than this change — and every *released* build in that set carries #617 (`v0.40.0` onward), so it is at this build's contract and merely cannot say so. Refusing the set would refuse every released daemon up to `v0.40.2` for staying silent, which relocates #801's false positive rather than removing it. The residual is the unreleased case, stated in `ContractComparison::Undeclared`: a dev build from the 73-minute window between PRD #882's bump and #617 genuinely predates a declared break and is connected anyway. The population that can land there is fixed and ageing, since every build from this change onward declares.

**The deck's KIND no longer changes the answer, which supersedes PRD #741 M8.** M8 demoted the stamp for a **remote** deck only, and its reasoning was about the *remedy*: the refusal's own sentence named **Replace deck**, which against a deck on another host means terminating a daemon somebody else may be using, and M2 had made that structurally impossible anyway — so the sentence named an action whose button is disabled on the same screen that printed it. That argument was right about the stamp, and the stamp now refuses nobody of either kind. What is left refusing is a *declared* break, which is not noise for a deck of either kind, so it refuses both — and **Connect anyway** is the escape hatch it leaves, for both.

What a screen acts on when the deck is older but compatible is still the **capability set**. `connection.projectActionsReason` is derived from what the daemon advertised in its `Hello` reply through `DaemonCapabilities::supports`, so it answers *"does this deck do what I am about to ask it to"* rather than *"is this deck the same build as me"*. A daemon that advertises none of `list-projects`, `resolve-project`, `prepare-workflow`, `start-prepared-agent` is an older daemon; the workflow panel says which are missing and disables **Launch live loop**, and the agents already running on that deck stay visible and usable.

**What this still does not catch, stated rather than implied.** An **undeclared** semantic break. If the author writes neither the `.breaking.md` fragment nor the `CONTRACT_BREAKS` entry, no mechanism here sees it: the verb is still advertised and still answers, the wire shape is unchanged, and the stamp did not see it either (an undeclared break bumps only the patch, which the digit comparison passed). So this is a residual carried over, not one introduced. What stands behind it is `CLAUDE.md` rule 12's cross-version manual test, run against the previous release before such a change merges, and the fragment its outcome demands — which `xtask/linkage-check`'s `contract_breaks` rule then pairs with the entry the handshake reads. The same argument is on `CONTRACT_BREAKS` in `src/daemon_protocol.rs`, which is where a reader changing this code will meet it.

In live mode the preview lists daemon-owned agents, attaches xterm.js to each PTY stream, forwards terminal input and resize requests, refreshes status, and exposes a confirmed stop action. Open **Projects**, choose the project this launch runs in, then **Configure workflow** to pick one of that project's orchestrations and choose **Launch live loop**, which starts its configured role set with the enabled profile commands — [The launch flow](#the-launch-flow-and-why-its-order-does-not-commute) below is why that order does not commute. **Start deck** and **Launch live loop** both require a confirmation. The project configuration must contain exactly the submitted roles and one start role; the current bundled `dot-agent-deck` profile uses `orchestrator` as that start role and requires every listed profile to remain enabled for a live launch. Before spawning, the **daemon** materializes the same canonical coordinator context the TUI uses, on its own filesystem: since #819 M6 the desktop asks for it through the `prepare-workflow` verb, which loads one validated config snapshot, resolves, composes and publishes before answering, and starts no roles when any of that fails. Non-Pi coordinators use a readiness-gated, identity-bound, idempotent submission with bounded retry. Pi cannot be the desktop workflow coordinator in this preview: its native seed path has no delivery acknowledgement, so the bridge rejects that launch before spawning any role. Use a non-Pi coordinator or launch that orchestration from the TUI until acknowledged native seed delivery is available. A partially-created workflow—or one whose coordinator context cannot be delivered—is rolled back in reverse role order.

Desktop bundles include `dot-agent-deck` as a Tauri sidecar built from the same checkout. Run `pnpm bundle:app` to prepare the matching sidecar and produce the native app; the separate bundle config keeps ordinary workspace `cargo test` runs independent of generated binaries. A contract/protocol mismatch exposes **Replace deck** only when the deck now running reports zero live agents; replacement uses the bundled binary and never force-stops live agents. A contract mismatch — one the protocol check already passed — additionally exposes **Connect anyway**, described above, which starts and stops nothing.

The **Projects** rail opens the project picker, and since #819 M6 everything in it comes from the daemon of the **selected** deck — `desktop_list_projects` resolves its daemon through `trusted_daemon()`, so a fleet on the overview changes nothing here and no non-selected deck is ever asked: it lists the projects that one daemon can see on its own machine, and a project it has nothing live in is named by pasting an absolute path, which the daemon resolves on that same machine. Nothing is saved — choosing a project is a step in assembling one launch rather than an entry in a library — and the picker neither moves nor deletes a repository. Projects still need a matching `.dot-agent-deck.toml`, and the launch verb remains the authoritative validation boundary.

Live workflow launch from the desktop preview is currently supported on macOS and Linux only. On Windows, the workflow sheet detects the platform, explains the limitation, and disables **Launch live loop**; the Rust bridge independently rejects a crafted `StartWorkflow` IPC request before validating or spawning roles. This guard is intentional because generated profile commands use POSIX shell quoting and must not be passed to `cmd.exe`. The fixture, daemon connection, and existing-agent terminal views remain available on Windows; use the TUI or launch commands manually until native Windows command construction is implemented.

Whole-run pause, fixture advancement, approval, and retry are not sent to the daemon. Workflow ordering remains a local preview and the live launch follows the role order in `.dot-agent-deck.toml`; command overrides apply to that launch only and do not rewrite project configuration.

### The launch flow, and why its order does not commute

The order is **daemon → project → workflow**. It is not a preference: the workflow list comes out of the chosen project's own `.dot-agent-deck.toml`, which the daemon reads on its own machine, so there is nothing to offer until a project resolves. The **Workflows** sheet reflects that — its workflow field is a `<select>` over the resolved project's orchestrations rather than a name you type, and its project directory is read-only, holding the daemon's own canonical spelling of the project you picked. The way to change it is to pick a different project. That spelling is load-bearing rather than cosmetic: canonicalising a symlinked path changes its basename, and an orchestration the config leaves unnamed is named after that basename, so a listing built from one spelling and a spawn built from another disagree about the name — PRD [#220](https://github.com/vfarcic/dot-agent-deck/issues/220)'s bug verbatim.

**Nothing persists a project client-side, and nothing on the daemon does either.** `desktop_project_cwd()` — the desktop crate's own guess at a project directory — is deleted, and so is the `dot-agent-deck.desktop.projects.v1` `localStorage` list that was the source of truth for the launch cwd. The resolved selection lives in React state and dies with the launch. Enumeration comes from what the daemon already holds and is derived from **live** state: its own startup cwd when that directory is a project, the cwd of every agent it owns, every orchestration's cwd, and every scheduled task's working directory — and the scheduled one is the seed that outlives a restart, since a daemon's agents are its own children and go with it. Every candidate is revalidated through the same bounded reader before it enters the list, because a seed is a candidate directory rather than a proven project. The design self-seeds through the action it exists to support: launching in a project puts an agent there, so the daemon enumerates it for as long as anything runs there. Any future convenience must be prefill for a field the daemon still resolves, never an authority (PRD #819, *Nothing remembers a project*).

The desktop may send back only a path the **daemon** supplied or the **user** typed. It may never derive one from its own environment — that is the whole invariant, and [the tripwire](#the-tripwire-on-that-invariant-and-what-it-cannot-do) is what notices a regression against it.

**Three states, and each is an ordinary outcome rather than an error:**

1. **The daemon knows nothing live.** Its startup cwd is not a project and it owns no agents, so it has none to offer. The picker says so and offers the paste-a-path field. This is the first-run behaviour, and it is the only one — there is no remembered list sitting behind it.
2. **A project left the set between listing and launch.** Enumeration is live-derived, so a project stops being known once the last thing running there exits, possibly seconds after it was drawn. The picker presents that like the empty state — pick another, or paste the path again — and re-lists, which is what turns it back into an honest picture of what is live. It is expected rather than a fault, and the refusal it is recognised by is the daemon's stable `unresolved` code and not its prose.
3. **An older daemon.** It never reaches a project verb at all. The desktop's handshake check is exact equality on `PROTOCOL_VERSION`, which #819 moved 8 → 9, so a v9 desktop refuses a v8 daemon at the handshake with `protocol mismatch: desktop expects 9, deck reports 8`, naming both versions; the build-stamp allowance runs after that check and does not bypass it. There is deliberately no local fallback — resolving client-side "when the daemon is local" would reinstate the silently-wrong-filesystem behaviour on the least-tested path.

**Refusals disclose by trust rather than uniformly.** A path the daemon **already knows** — meaning it has something live there — gets the detailed parse diagnostic, which is where "your config is broken, not empty" earns its keep. An arbitrary pasted path gets the same stable code and one fixed sentence, so the wire response does not directly distinguish "no such directory" from "no config there", and the detail is logged daemon-locally instead. The reason is concrete rather than precautionary: `ProjectConfigError`'s `Display` renders the offending TOML source line verbatim, so returning it for a path the caller merely typed would disclose file *content* and not merely existence. No timing property is claimed alongside it — canonicalisation, traversal, `open(2)` and TOML parsing do observably different amounts of work, and `src/project_resolve.rs`'s module doc says so rather than leaving it to be assumed.

## Decks: which ones the app talks to, and how it reaches another machine

PRD [#741](https://github.com/vfarcic/dot-agent-deck/issues/741) turns the daemon the app connects to from a constant into a **choice**, and PRD [#742](https://github.com/vfarcic/dot-agent-deck/issues/742) turns that choice into a **set**. Before #741, a daemon *was* a `PathBuf` — this machine's attach socket, resolved from the platform paths — and every operation the app performs against one took that path. This section is what replaced that: the type, where the configured decks are stored, what the app trusts about each kind, how a remote one is reached, how to set one up by hand, and — since #742 — what it means to watch several at once.

**The kind is in the type, and that is the whole mechanism.** `Endpoint` in [`src/daemon_client.rs`](../../src/daemon_client.rs) has two variants — `Local(LocalEndpoint)` and `Remote(RemoteEndpoint)` — and `Endpoint::as_local()` returns `None` for the remote arm. Three operations act on a **local process or a local inode** and would act on the wrong thing while reporting success if handed a remote deck's address: `peer_pid`-based termination (`run_daemon_stop`), the stale-inode `remove_file`, and lazy-spawn. All three take a `LocalEndpoint`, and `as_local()` is the only way to obtain one, so a remote deck cannot *reach* them — a compile error rather than a runtime `if`. `Endpoint::require_local(operation)` is the same thing with a refusal that names the operation and the deck, which is what the desktop's disabled **Stop deck** and **Replace deck** buttons render.

**Three *operations*, but four `as_local()` gates.** The fourth is a check rather than an operation, it does not refuse, and it is described in [Trust is a property of the deck's kind](#trust-is-a-property-of-the-decks-kind) — read that before assuming the count of three is the whole list.

`Endpoint::connect_address()` is deliberately a bare `&Path` and errors for the remote arm. Under [the transport decision](#why-ssh--l-and-what-was-rejected) a remote deck is reached through a forwarded socket that exists on *this* filesystem, and spelling that value `LocalEndpoint` would hand it straight back to `run_daemon_stop`, whose `SO_PEERCRED` lookup would then name the local `ssh` client. The address comes from a **lease on a live tunnel** instead, which is why `establish()` in `daemon_bridge.rs` asks `EndpointTunnels` for one rather than asking the endpoint.

### The `[endpoints]` section

The decks live in the same per-installation `desktop.toml` as everything else in [Application settings](#application-settings), under `[endpoints]`, and the section holds exactly two things: `remote`, a list of rows, and `selection`, a token naming the deck the app is talking to.

```toml
[endpoints]
selection = "k3n9wq2mf4ph7x1a"

[[endpoints.remote]]
host = "build-box.example.com"
id = "k3n9wq2mf4ph7x1a"
port = 22
socket = "/run/user/1000/dot-agent-deck-attach.sock"
user = "vfarcic"
```

**The local deck is not a row, and that is the point.** It needs no configuration — `Endpoint::local()` resolves it from the platform paths the way every caller did before endpoints existed — so a fresh install has no `[endpoints]` section at all and still works, and a user who deletes the section gets the local deck back rather than nothing. `selection = "local"` is the reserved token that names it, which is why `local` is the one word an endpoint id may not be.

**Every field is a validating newtype, and the type is doing two jobs at once.** `String` is not available here: `ALLOWED_FIELD_TYPES` in [`xtask/linkage-check/src/desktop_settings_secrets.rs`](../../xtask/linkage-check/src/desktop_settings_secrets.rs) is a closed list, `String` is deliberately absent from it, and the check runs in the **required** `build` job — so `host: String` reddens a required check on the first push. Satisfying that guard with newtypes buys the credential bound the guard exists for *and* the ssh-argument validation nothing in this tree performed before: `SshTarget::parse` (`src/remote.rs`) splits on the first `@` and stores both halves verbatim, with no length bound, no charset check, and no rejection of a leading `-`, whitespace or NUL.

| field | type | required | bound and charset, and what the shape rule adds |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| `host` | `Hostname` | yes | 253 bytes (RFC 1035's presentation limit, which also clears every IP literal form); ASCII alphanumerics plus `. - _ [ ] : %`. Outside brackets `:` and `%` are refused, because `:` would be read as a port separator in a `-L` forward spec and `%` as an IPv6 zone id; a bracketed value must be closed and non-empty. |
| `id` | `EndpointId` | yes | 64 bytes; ASCII alphanumerics, `-` and `_`; `local` and `all` refused case-insensitively. An **opaque minted token** rather than the address — editing a host must not silently re-point a selection — and rather than a list index, so removing a row does not re-point every selection after it. `mint()` produces 16 bytes; the charset is wider so a token a *newer* build wrote still parses. |
| `port` | `SshPort` | defaults to 22 | the type *is* the bound: a newtype over `NonZeroU16`, so `1..=65535` and nothing else is representable. It was a bare `u16` until PRD #741's Greptile review of [#1035](https://github.com/vfarcic/dot-agent-deck/pull/1035), which admits `0` — the one value the webview's own predicate refuses, and not an inert disagreement: a hand-edited `port = 0` loaded and was handed to OpenSSH as `-p 0`. The default comes from `SshPort::DEFAULT` rather than a literal `22`, so a row stored without a port and a row built by `RemoteEndpoint::new` cannot describe different decks. |
| `user` | `Option<SshUser>` | no | 64 bytes (past POSIX's 32-character `LOGIN_NAME_MAX`, which UPN logins routinely exceed); ASCII alphanumerics plus `. - _ @`. Every `@`-separated part must be non-empty, so `user@realm` works while `@realm`, `user@` and `user@@realm` are refused. |
| `identity` | `Option<KeyPath>` | no | 4096 bytes (`PATH_MAX`); ASCII alphanumerics plus `. - _ / ~`; must start `/` or `~/`. A **path**, never key material and never a passphrase. |
| `jump` | `Option<HostAlias>` | no | 253 bytes; ASCII alphanumerics plus `. - _`. A `Host` block *name* from the user's own `~/.ssh/config`, so the bastion's address, port, user and key stay there. No `*` or `?`: a jump target is one host. |
| `socket` | `Option<RemoteSocketPath>` | no | 104 bytes — the smaller of Linux's 108-byte and macOS/BSD's 104-byte `sun_path`, applied to both ends because the far host's OS is not knowable from here; ASCII alphanumerics plus `. - _ /`; must start `/`. No `:`, because `-L` is parsed by splitting on `:`. |

Each newtype's `Deserialize` runs the same check its constructor does, so a hand-edited `desktop.toml` cannot smuggle past what the settings form applies. They live in [`src/remote_tunnel.rs`](../../src/remote_tunnel.rs) rather than in the desktop crate because the CLI's `remotes.toml` has the same problem.

**There is no display name, deliberately.** A user-chosen label is exactly the arbitrary `String` the field-type guard refuses, and it would need its own bidi and control-character handling before anything rendered it. `RemoteEndpoint::describe()` derives the label from the address instead, and every byte of that came through a validated ASCII charset.

**`identity` is named that rather than `key`, and the cost is written down.** `key` is what `remotes.toml` calls it and it trips the naming tripwire in `settings.rs`, whose path allowlist is empty. The two available answers were an exemption pinned to `KeyPath` or a name that is not credential-shaped; this is the second, because `IdentityFile` is OpenSSH's own name for the option and because an empty allowlist is worth keeping — the first entry is the one that makes the second easy. What it costs is that the tripwire is now silent about the one field in this schema that sits next to credential material, which is why that is said out loud in the field's doc comment. The control that matters is unaffected: `KeyPath` is on `ALLOWED_FIELD_TYPES` with a written reason, bounded at `PATH_MAX`, restricted to a charset that cannot represent a multi-line PEM block, and required to start `/` or `~/`.

**`socket` is optional, and a row without one is *storable* and *not connectable*.** It cannot be derived: OpenSSH expands neither `~` nor an environment variable on the remote side of `-L`, and the far host's `XDG_RUNTIME_DIR` and uid are not knowable from here without a second ssh round trip. Requiring it would mean typing `/run/user/1000/dot-agent-deck-attach.sock` correctly *before* anything could test whether it was right, so instead `EndpointSettings::resolve()` returns the local deck plus a named `SelectionFallback::NoRemoteSocket`, the panel turns that into "not configured yet — press Test connection", and **Test connection** discovers the path over the round trip it is already making and writes it back into the row. The write-back is the webview's, deliberately: `useDesktopSettings` already serialises the document's read-modify-write, and a second writer in Rust would be [#828](https://github.com/vfarcic/dot-agent-deck/issues/828) created on purpose.

#### `Option<EndpointSettings>` is merge protection, and it puts an obligation on every future panel

`DesktopSettings::endpoints` is an `Option`, and `None` means **unspecified** rather than **empty**. That distinction is load-bearing and it is not a style choice.

`desktop_set_settings` takes the **whole document** from the webview, and the webview builds that document with `normalizeDesktopSettings`, which constructs a fresh object with a fixed key set — that fixed set is itself a credential guard, so it is not going away. A section the frontend does not declare is therefore already gone before any panel spreads it. If the field were a plain `EndpointSettings`, `#[serde(default)]` would turn "the webview said nothing" into "the webview said empty", and `merged_document` would then write that empty list over a hand-edited `[endpoints]` table: every remote deck deleted by somebody changing the theme. With an `Option`, TOML omits a `None` field entirely and the merge preserves what is on disk — the same protection the merge gives *across builds*, now available *across clients*. `a_client_that_cannot_render_endpoints_cannot_delete_them` pins it.

**The obligation that comes with it:** the moment a panel can render a section, the frontend must **round-trip** it — read it in `normalizeDesktopSettings`, keep it, and send it back — and must never fabricate a default for it. A default `{ remote: [], selection: "local" }` on the frontend side is exactly the deletion the `Option` exists to prevent, arriving from a client that *does* claim to know about endpoints. This generalises: any future section whose frontend half has not landed yet is declared `Option<…>` on `DesktopSettings`, and `endpoints` is the worked example. [Adding a setting](#adding-a-setting) step 2 carries the same rule at the point where it is needed.

**Since PRD #742 M6 that obligation is a mechanism rather than an assertion.** `endpointSectionToSave()` in [`desktop/src/lib/endpoints.ts`](../../desktop/src/lib/endpoints.ts) takes the section the panel was handed and the section it wants to write, and returns `undefined` — *write nothing at all* — when the two say the same thing. Both `EndpointsPanel` and `DeckSelector` route every write through it: the chooser, the row editor, the remove button, the **Test connection** write-back and the selector's own menu. The write-back was the exception until PRD #742 M8 — it called `onSave` directly because it writes against `latest.current` rather than the document its handler closed over, and `saveSection` closed over that document. It was inert (its `row && !row.socket` find-guard fires only on a genuine change, and on an unreadable document the fabricated `remote: []` makes the `find` miss, so nothing is written), but inert by a property of that one write-back rather than by the shared rule these three sentences claimed for it. M8 gave `saveSection` a document parameter so both kinds of write use one gate. So a click that selects the deck already in force, or that abandons a draft without moving the selection, cannot reach the merge and cannot delete a row it never saw. It is shared rather than a guard inside one handler because every one of those sites carried the same hazard; `EndpointsPanel::chooseDeck` had written a `{ remote: [], selection }` section on every click since #741 with no same-selection guard at all.

**What it does not close, deliberately, because this is the kind of thing that gets rediscovered expensively.** An *intentional* selection change against an **unreadable** document still writes `remote: []`. The webview cannot tell "there is no `[endpoints]` section" from "`load_from` could not parse the file and handed you defaults" — both arrive as `endpoints: undefined` — and refusing to create a section when none was declared would make All Decks unstorable on a fresh install, which is the majority case. Closing it needs either `remote` optional on the wire, so the webview can say "I am changing the selection and asserting nothing about the rows", or `load_from` to stop handing the webview a document that is not the file. Both are schema-or-load-path changes, and both are recorded rather than attempted.

**And the wider fact M6 established is the more useful half, because it reframes the whole protection: after a parse failure every field this build's schema owns is already lost on the next save, not just endpoints.** `load_from` logs and returns `DesktopSettings::default()`, the webview is handed those defaults, and `merge_tables` overwrites every key the struct owns — so appearance and zoom are reset by the next save whatever the endpoints section does. The `Option` was never protecting the user's data against *this* client; it protects a section against a client that does **not** render it, which is exactly the claim `a_client_that_cannot_render_endpoints_cannot_delete_them` makes and no more. Worth knowing alongside it: the preservation depends on the document still parsing as a **TOML table**. `merged_document` treats a document it cannot parse as a table as empty and replaces it outright, so a file that is not TOML at all loses its rows whether or not the client declared a section — the `Option` only reaches the case where the bytes are valid TOML and the *schema* is what rejected them.

#### The selection is a growable value, not an id

`Selection` in `settings.rs` is an enum with room for a variant, not an `Option<EndpointId>`, and `DeckSelection` in [`desktop/src/lib/endpoints.ts`](../../desktop/src/lib/endpoints.ts) mirrors it as a union. The stored form is one token — `local`, `all`, or a row's id — and `Selection` is the authority on it. That shape is what made [#742](https://github.com/vfarcic/dot-agent-deck/issues/742)'s **All Decks** additive, and the prediction held when M1 spent it: one variant plus one arm in each of `parseSelection`, `selectionToken` and `deckChoices`, against a change to every prop that carries a selection and a `""`-means-all convention in each of them. `sameSelection` needed none, because it compares stored tokens rather than variants. **No component below `endpoints.ts` handles a raw endpoint id.**

`all` is reserved on both sides, like `local`: `EndpointId::parse` refuses it case-insensitively, so a hand-written `id = "all"` row fails to load rather than making the one stored string ambiguous between the fleet and that row. PRD #742 accepted that refusal as a clean trade and it was not one at the time — "fails to load" then meant the whole document read as defaults and the next save replaced it; that is [#1072](https://github.com/vfarcic/dot-agent-deck/issues/1072), and under the refusal described in [When the document cannot be read](#when-the-document-cannot-be-read) the trade is now what it was assumed to be. `EndpointId::mint` cannot produce it — sixteen hex characters — so only a hand-edited document can reach that refusal. `EndpointSettings::resolve` deliberately has no `All` arm: it answers which *single* deck the deck screen and its terminals talk to, a fleet has no single answer, and `Selection::All` therefore travels through the same `let … else` as `Selection::Local` and gets the local deck with **no** fallback — nothing failed to be honoured. `EndpointSettings::observed_endpoints` is where the fleet's set lives instead, and [All Decks](#all-decks-the-fleet-and-the-one-screen-that-merges-it) is what that set does once it exists.

An unknown token — including one a newer build wrote, as `all` itself was before #742 — parses as `One`, finds no row, and resolves to the local deck with `SelectionFallback::UnknownDeck` reported alongside, which the app renders rather than swallowing. Nothing throws: the document is hand-editable and may have been written by a build with more variants than this one. The token is also **written back unchanged**, so an older build degrades rather than destroying a newer build's selection. That is what let `All` ship without damaging any older build's document, and it is pinned by `a_selection_token_this_build_does_not_know_degrades_without_being_lost` against a word this build still does not know.

### All Decks: the fleet, and the one screen that merges it

PRD [#742](https://github.com/vfarcic/dot-agent-deck/issues/742) adds **All Decks** to the Deck selector — the `all` token, the second reserved word beside `local`. Choosing it puts the app on a *set* of decks rather than one: `EndpointSettings::observed_endpoints` returns the local deck plus every configured row that has an address to connect to, the crate keeps a tunnel and a watcher alive for each, and the **agent overview** renders one group per deck with that deck's agents and that deck's connection state. **A row with no `socket` path is omitted from that set** — it has no address to connect to and a watcher for it would have nothing to watch. That is the CONNECTABLE set; the set the screen draws is wider. PRD #742 M12 separated the two after a half-configured deck turned out to be absent from the fleet entirely — not in the numerator, not in the denominator, no group anywhere, so three configured decks read as `2/2`. A row with no address now gets a group of its own carrying the settings panel's own "press Test connection" words, and M14 did the same for the other way a configured deck can be missing: one whose watcher has not reported yet. Both are in the denominator and neither is ever in the numerator. The **deck screen stays single-deck** — a tile's terminal is always the selected deck's, and All Decks resolves that single answer to the local deck.

**That split is a decision, not an oversight, and the reason is the one cost in this app that is already linear.** Attach streams are deliberately **not** multiplexed: the frame header is five bytes, one kind byte plus a four-byte big-endian length, with **no stream id** (`read_frame` in `src/daemon_protocol.rs`), so muxing would be a wire change and a `PROTOCOL_VERSION` bump rather than a refactor. A live terminal therefore costs one connection and one daemon-side task **per visible tile**. Putting N decks of terminals on one screen multiplies that; putting N decks of *rows* on one screen does not — the overview has no terminal anywhere on it. Cross-deck attach is the follow-up PRD's subject, not a gap in this one.

Two consequences of the split are worth knowing before touching either screen. The attach model below is keyed by **bare agent id** (`setShownTerminals(agentIds)`, `desktop_terminal_attach(agent_id)`), and agent ids are per-daemon monotonic integers, so two decks both mint `"1"` — that holds only because `terminal::attach` resolves its daemon through `trusted_daemon()`, which is `selected_endpoint()`, so every terminal the app opens belongs to whichever deck is selected at the time. And `groupAgents` was untouched by the fleet: it buckets *within* a deck by tab membership, which is a different question from which deck an agent is on, and calling it over a merged list would put two machines' roles in one orchestration card.

#### Per-deck connection state, and the one field that is still the selection's

`ConnectionStatus` and the four fields #741 added beside it stopped describing "the selected deck" and describe **a** deck, one per observed endpoint. `deckKind` and `localOnlyReason` are properties of a deck — whether *this* deck runs on this machine, and therefore whether Stop and Replace can act on it — and reading them off the process-global selection would have disabled the lifecycle controls for the whole view whenever the *selected* deck happened to be remote, and enabled them for a remote group whenever it happened to be local. `projectActionsReason` is likewise the capability set *that* daemon advertised in its own `Hello` reply, so a fleet spanning builds carries N capability sets rather than one.

**`selectionFallback` is the deliberate exception: it is still the selection's, not a deck's.** It describes the selection rather than any deck — "the deck you chose is gone, so you are on local" — so attaching it to every group would be a claim about each of them. The narrow fact that makes reading it off the process-global correct is worth stating rather than assuming, because it is the whole justification: `EndpointSettings::observed_endpoints` returns `[resolve().endpoint]` for every selection but `All`, so every other selection observes exactly the deck it resolved to and the fallback is that deck's by construction; and `All` — the only selection that can observe more than one — resolves to the local deck with **no** fallback at all, so a fleet never has one to misattribute.

#### The aggregate is honest about what it could not see

The overview's header prints `DECKS 2/3` beside the agent and group counts, and those counts are computed over the decks that **answered**. Summing over every deck is the failure the instrument exists to prevent: a deck that is not answering has an unknown number of agents, and adding zero for it under-counts the fleet by however many that deck is running — a wrong number that looks exactly like a right one. `DECKS` renders at one deck too, where `1/1` is the honest reading, because a caveat that appears only once something is already wrong is a caveat nobody learns to read; its hover carries the sentence a ratio cannot, naming that every count beside it is over the decks that answered.

A deck that is not answering renders as a **degraded group** — its own header, its own lamp, its own note, and an em dash where its neighbours' status pips are — and never as a group with zero agents. "This deck runs nothing" and "we cannot see what this deck runs" are different statements and only the first is a number. That is the same rule the header instruments have always followed for a single deck, restated one level out: the instruments read `—` rather than `0` while no deck is answering.

#### `socketPath` is a label; `deckId` is the key

`connection.deckId` is `EndpointIdentity::wire_id()` — FNV-1a over the newtype's *derived* `Hash`, rendered `deck-<16 hex>` — and it is what anything keying on a deck must use. `connection.socketPath` is `Endpoint::describe()`, which is what a reader sees, and the distinction is enforced rather than advisory because the collision it prevents is the most instructive defect in this PRD.

**The defect.** The webview derived its per-deck `daemonId` from `socketPath`, and `describe()` renders a remote deck as `user@host[:port]` — omitting the remote socket path, the identity file and the jump host. So two `[[endpoints.remote]]` rows pointing at one host with different `socket` values — **two daemons on one machine, which is exactly what that field exists for** — described identically, folded into one group, and their agents shared a `daemonId`. The composite `(daemonId, agentId)` key the overview has used since day one does not save it, because the key *component* is the collision. It fails silently and plausibly: the two decks alternate inside one group, last writer wins per emit, and the screen looks like one healthy deck. `EndpointIdentity` was introduced on PR [#1035](https://github.com/vfarcic/dot-agent-deck/pull/1035) specifically for the N-deck case and then stopped at the Rust boundary; `wire_id` is it crossing.

**Derived through `Hash`, hashed rather than rendered, and FNV rather than `DefaultHasher`** — each for its own reason. Hashing the newtype inherits the key: a field added to `RemoteEndpoint` tomorrow joins the token with nothing here to update, where a hand-picked tuple of fields would have been a second copy to keep in step. A hash keeps the value **opaque**, which is a misuse control rather than a disclosure one — the desktop's IPC is same-process and `socketPath` carries the host beside it — so the `deck-…` string cannot become a second hand-assembled identity the way `describe()` did. And the algorithm is written out in `src/daemon_client.rs` because `std` documents `DefaultHasher`'s output as unspecified and free to move between Rust releases, so the token names the same deck across a restart. **That last clause used to end "while the webview keys `localStorage`-backed state on this token", and nothing does** — M8 enumerated the five keys in [The five `localStorage` keys](#the-five-localstorage-keys-and-the-one-that-is-gone) and every one is scoped by runtime mode rather than by a deck. The stable hash is still the right choice, for the forward-looking reason rather than the stated one: this is the identity a per-deck preference would be keyed on the first time anyone stores one, and a token that moved under a toolchain upgrade would break that store in a way nobody would trace back to the upgrade.

**Two things `wire_id` does not promise**, stated because the honest scope is narrower than "stable": identity across a future change to `std`'s own `Hash` impl for a component type, and identity across architectures, since the default `Hasher::write_*` methods feed integers in native-endian order. Either would reset a per-deck UI preference on one machine. Neither can confuse two decks, because both sides of every comparison are minted by one process from one build. The same token is also what stamps `desktop://daemon-event`, and it had to move with the snapshot key rather than after it: the webview's "is this event from the deck the screen is on" filter compares the two, so a stamp left on `describe()` would have gone on answering *yes* for a second daemon on the same host after the snapshot key had stopped doing so.

#### Fleet membership rides on every snapshot

`DesktopSnapshot.fleet` is the observed deck ids in observed order, **selected first** and **never empty**, and every deck's snapshot carries the same list because it is a property of the applied document rather than of the deck that happened to emit. So a webview may prune on any arrival.

**It is on the wire because nothing else says a deck has left.** A deck *joining* announces itself by emitting; a deck leaving produces no event at all — `apply_selection` ends its watcher, and under `All` → `local` the resolved deck does not move, so nothing is emitted on that path either. A webview that only upserts what arrives would keep that deck's agents on screen, frozen and looking live. Selected-first is the second half: `fleet[0]` answers "which deck are the single-deck surfaces bound to", which nothing on the stream could answer before, since under `All` every observed deck emits the same shape. Never-empty is the third: a deck the app cannot reach is still an entry and still emits its own `disconnected` snapshot, so an empty list is a malformed payload rather than an empty fleet — and the webview's prune treats it as such rather than clearing the screen.

One ordering subtlety, because it is cheap to get backwards: **prune after the upsert, not before.** A watcher being torn down can land one last snapshot whose `fleet` no longer names its own deck, and pruning first deletes the entry only for the upsert to reinstate it.

#### What the fleet costs, and what nothing bounds

Per **live remote** deck, in the observe-only scope: one `ssh -N -L` child, one detached OS thread draining its stderr (spawned and never joined), one forwarded Unix socket plus client fds, roughly two held daemon connections, one client-side watcher task and one daemon-side task per held connection. A **local** deck costs the same minus the ssh child, the stderr thread and the forwarded socket.

**Nothing bounds the number of configured or live endpoints.** `MAX_ENDPOINT_ID_BYTES` bounds an id and `MAX_SETTINGS_BYTES` (256 KiB) bounds the file, so the only ceiling is accidental and sits around a thousand rows. And the first thing to hurt is not the one that list suggests: `SNAPSHOT_COALESCE_INTERVAL` is applied **per watcher loop**, so with per-deck watchers the aggregate emit rate into the webview is N × the single-deck ceiling, and the frontend merges every one of them. That is IPC and CPU pressure scaling with **deck count regardless of how few agents each deck has**, which is the opposite of the intuition that a small fleet is cheap. The first hard OS ceiling is separately the ssh child and fd count, reaped only by `retain`/`close_all`.

Coalescing per watcher is the deliberate choice rather than an accident of where the sleep sits. The alternative — one global coalescer over N inbound streams — shares the window across decks and reintroduces exactly the cross-deck stall the per-deck design exists to remove.

#### One unreachable deck must not degrade the others, and that needed two locks split

`DaemonLinks.trusted` and `EndpointTunnels::acquire` each held **one async mutex over the entire map, across establishment**. With one selected deck that serialisation is a feature: concurrent first-uses of the same deck collapse into one handshake. With N decks it inverts — an unreachable deck's connect attempt queues every *other* deck's handshake behind its connect timeout — and it is invisible at every call site, which is why it is written down here. Both are now a per-deck gate keyed like the map and held across establishment, with the map's own lock held only for the lookup and the insert around it, so concurrent first-uses of one deck still collapse into one handshake while two decks never wait for each other.

**Both had to be split, for asymmetric reasons.** `establish()` holds `DaemonLinks`' gate *across* `acquire()`, so fixing only `DaemonLinks` would have left every `acquire` serialised behind one deck's ssh authentication — the property unmet for exactly the remote decks it is about. Fixing only `EndpointTunnels` would have left the `DaemonLinks` test red. And the coverage is asymmetric too: **`EndpointTunnels::acquire` has no test** and was fixed from the code deliberately. The risk test injects its stall at `hello()`, which sits *after* `acquire` inside `establish()`, and for a local endpoint `acquire` returns promptly — so that test passes whether or not this lock was ever split. Stalling `acquire` deterministically means stalling `EndpointConnection::open`, whose slow path is spawning `ssh`, which needs either a real network timeout or a production test seam; neither was judged worth it.

#### Splitting that lock gave up a second property, and putting it back is one counter

The whole-map lock was doing **two** jobs. The first — collapsing concurrent first-uses of one deck — is what the per-deck gate replaced. The second was that, being held *across establishment*, it also excluded **teardown**: `EndpointTunnels::retain` and `DaemonLinks::invalidate_all` blocked until the establishment finished and then removed the entry. The gate does not do that. `retain` and `invalidate_all` take only the map lock, and a deck being established has **no entry in that map yet**, so a teardown landing mid-establishment removes nothing for it and the establishment publishes it afterwards.

The user-visible shape: a refresh is inside `acquire(D)` for up to `FORWARD_READY_TIMEOUT` (30 s) while the user picks a different deck; `retarget_selection` runs `invalidate_all()` and `retain({the new set})`, neither of which can see `D`; and `D`'s lease and trusted link land in maps nothing will consult again until the next settings save. One `ssh -N -L` child, its stderr thread, a forwarded socket and a held daemon connection, to a host the user believes they have disconnected from. Bounded and eventually reaped — but *"I removed that deck" stops meaning "that ssh session is gone"*, which is the part worth a mechanism.

**The mechanism is `desktop/src-tauri/src/generation.rs`: a monotonic counter read before the await and compared after.** A value that moved means somebody removed something, so the publish is refused. **Not publishing is the correct fallback rather than an error** — the lease or the link is still returned, the caller's own `Arc` is then the last one, and what it holds dies with the request instead of outliving it. The compare and the insert happen under **one** hold of the map lock, and each teardown bumps under that same lock, so the two are serialised rather than merely likely to interleave correctly.

**One counter serves both maps**, because they describe one live set and are torn down together — `retarget_selection` calls `invalidate_all` and `retain` in consecutive statements. It lives on `EndpointTunnels` and `DaemonLinks` reads it through `tunnels().generation()`. What that buys precisely: `DaemonLinks::trusted`'s compare is *exact* against `invalidate_all` (same lock), and conservative against the tunnel map's own three teardowns; it does not need to be exact against those, because a link outliving a tunnel `release` is the designed behaviour — `TrustedDaemon` holds its own lease so a classified link cannot describe a deck whose transport has gone.

**What it costs, stated because the cheaper-looking alternative is worse.** The counter does not say *which* deck departed, so a settings save that keeps the deck being established still costs that establishment its cache entry and the next call establishes again. Testing `dto::deck_is_observed` instead would be precise — and would depend on the applied selection having been written before the teardown ran, which is an ordering invariant *between two modules* rather than a property of the one doing the check. One re-establishment on a rare overlap is the cheaper side of that trade.

**The same counter is what names a watcher claim.** `DesktopState::start_watcher_once_for` returns a token rather than a `bool`, and `register_watcher` stores a handle only into the claim that asked for it. Before that the slot was a bare `Option<JoinHandle>` and could answer only "is there a claim here" — so if a deck left and rejoined inside the claim → spawn → register window, watcher A's handle went into watcher B's slot and B's own registration then *replaced* it. Replacing drops a `JoinHandle` rather than aborting it, so A ran untracked and unstoppable for the life of the process, double-folding every broadcast for that deck. A handle arriving for somebody else's claim is now aborted exactly like one arriving for no claim.

#### The applied selection is one value, and the evidence ring follows the selection

Two smaller things the fleet made worth stating.

**`SELECTED_DECK` and `OBSERVED_DECKS` were two `RwLock`s written in sequence**, and the comment on the second said the pair "can never describe different saves" because there is one writer. True of *saves*; false of *reads* — a reader scheduled between the two `write()` calls sees one new value and one old one. They are now one `AppliedSelection` under one lock, with `selected_deck`, `observed_decks`, `deck_is_observed` and `observed_fleet` all deriving from a single read. The direction that cost something was `release_if_not_observed` reading a pre-save observed set while a just-added deck's watcher already held a lease: the probe drops the map's handle, the watcher's lease keeps the child alive, and the next `establish()` opens a **second** `ssh` child. Reported as unreachable-in-practice by the two people who found it; a reconstruction of the two-lock shape failed the regression test 6 times out of 6, so the window is real even though a human-paced settings save makes it rare.

**The evidence drawer and the handoff rail follow the selected deck.** `TauriDeckBridge` keeps one working ring rather than one per deck — the per-deck storage is each `DeckSnapshot`'s own `evidence`/`handoffs`, which the fleet already carries — and `adoptEvidenceDeck` swaps that working copy for the arriving deck's own history whenever `selectedDeckId` moves, in `foldSnapshot` and in `connect()`. It was process-global, handed to whichever deck was currently selected and never cleared on a switch, so a switch to `build-box` mapped its first snapshot carrying every hook event the local deck had produced — one machine's agent ids, roles and pane ids under another machine's name. `isSelectedDeckEvent` stops *live* events crossing and does nothing about a ring that survives the switch, so the filter reads as a stronger guarantee than it was. **Pre-existing rather than introduced by the fleet** (`4a7ae532` passed the same global ring), fixed here because a partial guarantee that reads as a total one is worse than neither. One consequence worth knowing: the sequence counter behind each item's `hook-<n>` id is **never** reset, because those are React keys and two decks restarting the count would collide two distinct items under one key.

### Trust is a property of the deck's kind

This is the conceptual heart of the PRD, and the shortest statement of it is: **`Local` keeps uid + mode `0o600`; `Remote` rests on ssh host-key and user authentication; neither pretends to be the other.**

**#741 left the local check unchanged, and for the first time pinned its refusals.** `verify_endpoint_trusted` (`src/platform/fsperm/unix.rs`) stats the path and requires that it is a socket, that its owner uid is this process's, and that its mode is **exactly** `0o600` — a comparison, not a mask, so `0o644` and `0o666` are both refused and so is `0o700`. It runs **out of band and before the first connect**, which is its position as much as its content: moving it inside `connect` would change the Unix semantics it exists for. What #741 M1 added is the test coverage that did not exist before it — the wrong-mode, foreign-uid, not-a-socket and nothing-at-the-path refusals plus the healthy accept — because nothing else would catch a later refactor silently loosening the predicate. The foreign-uid arm is not testable without a second account, so the comparison is factored out as pure data and *that* is tested, the way the Windows owner-SID check is pinned.

**One clause has moved since, and it is a fourth requirement rather than a change to those three** ([#1020](https://github.com/vfarcic/dot-agent-deck/issues/1020)). The stat is now an `lstat`, and a **symlink** at the endpoint path is refused on its own account. It used to be `std::fs::metadata`, which reads *through* a link — so the uid and mode tested were the target's and the link's own were never consulted, and a link owned by anybody at all was accepted as long as it resolved to a socket of ours at `0o600`. That is reachable only where a foreign uid can create the entry, which means the `/tmp` fallback `attach_socket_path()` takes when `XDG_RUNTIME_DIR` is unset — an ssh session or a container, which is exactly this PRD's case. It is redirection rather than disclosure: the attacker is not the listener, and the payoff is steering our own client at another of our *own* endpoints. #741 M1 had pinned the old behaviour as characterization precisely so this change would go red rather than flip silently. Only the **final** component is `lstat`ed, so a symlinked ancestor resolves as before — which matters on macOS, where `/tmp` is a symlink to `/private/tmp`. The residual is availability and is [#1121](https://github.com/vfarcic/dot-agent-deck/issues/1121): `/tmp`'s sticky bit stops the deck unlinking an entry another uid owns, so a squatter still wedges that path until they or root clear it, and the refusal now names `DOT_AGENT_DECK_ATTACH_SOCKET` as the manual way around it.

**The local check is deliberately NOT run against a forwarded socket, and that is a claim about what it can prove.** A remote deck's connect address is a Unix socket the local `ssh` client created, under a `0700` directory this app owns. A uid + mode test on that inode says something true and useless: it proves the local `ssh` process is ours. It says **nothing** about who is on the far end, which is the only question that matters for a remote deck. Running it anyway would be the disguise PRD #741 exists to remove — a client treating a remote endpoint as local because the address happens to look like one. `establish()` gates it on `endpoint.as_local()` for exactly that reason, and the comment above it says so.

What stands in its place for a remote deck is ssh's own authentication, and #741 M5 made sure it is actually in force rather than merely available:

- **The host-key CHECK is forced on; the trust anchor it consults is not.** `StrictHostKeyChecking=yes`, through the generated config, on both hops. Leaving it unset meant the *user's* config decided, and a `Host * / StrictHostKeyChecking no` line left a long-lived channel the GUI presents as trusted with no host-key check at all — an on-path attacker who answered instead of the real host got a healthy-looking remote deck, and nothing in the app could notice, by construction, because the local inode check is correctly not run against a forwarded socket. Forcing `yes` costs nothing against the default `ask`, since `BatchMode=yes` already makes `ask` fail on an unknown key; against `accept-new` it costs silent trust-on-first-use, which is the point. **What it does not buy** (PRD #741 final audit F2, and this bullet used to imply otherwise): `KnownHostsCommand`, `UserKnownHostsFile` and `GlobalKnownHostsFile` are all inherited from the user's config, measured on both hops, so a `KnownHostsCommand` emitting whatever key the server presents satisfies the forced `yes` against any host. Forcing those three works — `KnownHostsCommand none` from the leading block wins, measured — and is deliberately not done, because it breaks the CA- and inventory-driven fleets `KnownHostsCommand` exists for. This sits inside DECISION 1A's trust boundary, so the answer is the one the inherited forwards got: **Test connection** discloses the resolved host-key source beside them.
- **`StrictHostKeyChecking=no` and `accept-new` are never set by us.** A first-contact decision belongs to a user who can see a fingerprint, and this app cannot show one. The failure surfaces as its own named state with the remedy — run `ssh <target>` once in a terminal — built from the endpoint the tunnel actually uses, bastion and port included.
- **`UserKnownHostsFile` is deliberately not forced**, nor are `GlobalKnownHostsFile` and `KnownHostsCommand`. Pointing any of them anywhere would decide *which* keys count as trusted on the user's behalf. We force that the check happens; the user's config decides what passes it — and **Test connection** shows which source it chose, so the decision is visible rather than merely inherited.
- **The forwarded socket's mode is forced too.** `StreamLocalBindMask=0177` gives a `0600` socket. OpenSSH's default is already `0177`, but it is a config option, and a user config `StreamLocalBindMask 0000` made the socket world-readable and world-writable — with nothing in the app checking, since the trust check is not run there and `await_forward` treats any socket inode as readiness. The only thing keeping a foreign uid off the channel was the `0700` directory the tunnel creates, which does hold but held alone. See [the generated config](#the-generated-ssh-config-and-why-its-layout-is-load-bearing) for why this one option has to be emitted *after* the includes.

**Nothing terminates a process by peer credential on a remote deck.** That is the `as_local()` property above, restated where it matters: `run_daemon_stop` performs an `SO_PEERCRED` lookup and SIGTERMs the pid it finds. Over a tunnel that pid is the local `ssh` client, so the call would kill the tunnel, report success, and leave the remote daemon running. It is unreachable by type, and **Stop deck** / **Replace deck** render as disabled with the reason `require_local` produced rather than silently doing something else.

#### The trust check is the FOURTH `as_local()` gate, and the only one that substitutes rather than refuses

[Decks](#decks-which-ones-the-app-talks-to-and-how-it-reaches-another-machine) above names **three** operations that are local-only because `Endpoint::as_local()` returns `None` for the remote arm: `peer_pid` termination, the stale-inode `remove_file`, and lazy-spawn. #741's own Risks section warned that `peer_pid` was "not the only locality assumption, only the one that was found", and PRD #742's audit enumerated every production call site of `as_local()` and `require_local()` and discharged that warning. **The fourth is the trust check itself**, in `establish()` (`desktop/src-tauri/src/daemon_bridge.rs`), gated by `if let Some(local) = endpoint.as_local()`. For a remote deck `verify_endpoint_trusted` never runs at all.

**The difference in *shape* is the dangerous part, and it is why this belongs beside the three rather than inside their list.** The three that were named **refuse**: a disabled button carrying the sentence `require_local` produced, so the user is told. This one **silently substitutes a different trust basis** — ssh host-key and user authentication in place of the uid-and-mode inode predicate. Nothing in the flow says "this deck was trusted differently"; it simply was. The substitution is correct — a uid and mode test on a socket the local `ssh` client created would prove only that the local `ssh` process is ours — but it is invisible, and an invisible correct answer and an invisible wrong one look identical from the screen.

**With a fleet in one window that matters more than it did with one deck at a time.** Under **All Decks** the local groups and the remote groups sit side by side with the same chrome, the same lamp and the same status pips, so it is newly easy for a reader to assume every group in the view cleared the same gate. The local ones did and the remote ones did not, by design. For the observe-only scope this is not a leak — each remote deck's ssh trust is per-connection and keyed by `EndpointIdentity`, so no deck inherits another's — but a reader reasoning about what the screen proves should reason per group and not per screen.

### Why `ssh -L`, and what was rejected

The app shells out to the system `ssh` and owns a long-lived `ssh -N -L` child per remote deck. Two alternatives were costed first, and both are worth recording because the next person to ask will otherwise re-run the measurement.

**An in-process SSH client (`russh`) was measured out, and the deciding half is `cargo audit`.** `security` is a **required** check on this repository and it is currently clean.

| variant | packages | new to this repo | `cargo audit` | `cargo check` |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| baseline (this tree) | 615 | — | exit 0, zero vulnerabilities, 8 informational | — |
| `russh`, default features | 181 | **81** | **exit 1** — RUSTSEC-2023-0071 | not attempted |
| `default-features = false, ["aws-lc-rs","flate2"]` | 178 | 78 | exit 0 | not attempted |
| `default-features = false, ["ring","flate2"]` | 183 | 78 | exit 0 | exit 0 |
| pure Rust, no crypto backend | 170 | — | — | **exit 101** |

`rsa` is a **default** feature of russh 0.63.3, pinned `=0.10.0-rc.18`. RUSTSEC-2023-0071 (the Marvin timing attack) has `patched = []` — there is no version to bump to — and the advisory's own workaround is "avoid using the `rsa` crate where attackers can observe timing", which describes a network SSH client exactly. Getting past it means either dropping RSA, which silently loses `ssh-rsa`/`rsa-sha2-*` host keys and RSA user keys for precisely the users most likely to have a remote daemon, or writing this repo's **first** `.cargo/audit.toml` ignore — a durable weakening of a clean required gate in exchange for a feature. Of the 81 new crates the overwhelming majority are `0.x` cryptographic primitives, and russh pins two release candidates as hard `=` requirements (`ssh-key = "=0.7.0-rc.11"`, `rsa = "=0.10.0-rc.18"`), so the tree could not take a patch of either without russh cutting a release; this repo's entire crypto exposure today is zero. And **a pure-Rust russh is impossible**, measured rather than assumed: a `compile_error!` demands `ring` or `aws-lc-rs`, both native C, so it would also add a C toolchain requirement to `build`, `build-windows`, `build-macos` and to `flake.nix`.

Against all that, auth is the hard part and the system `ssh` already solves it: `Host` blocks, `Include`, `Match`, `ProxyJump`, `IdentityAgent`, FIDO `sk-` keys, PKCS#11, GSSAPI, certificate auth, `known_hosts` with hashed names and `@cert-authority`. Reimplementing the *config* half of that is a project, not a dependency. Delegating it is `src/remote.rs`'s existing bet and it has held.

**The honest cost of shelling out is that OpenSSH has no stdio-to-remote-*Unix-socket* forwarding** — `-W` is `host:port` only — which leaves two shapes.

- **1A — `ssh -N -L`, app-managed.** What shipped. It forwards the remote attach socket to a local one the app owns, under a `0700` directory it creates.
- **1B — `ssh <host> dot-agent-deck daemon proxy-stdio`, a new deck subcommand.** The remote binary connects to its own attach socket and pipes it to stdio, so the ssh child's stdin/stdout *is* the transport.
