It is genuinely attractive: no local socket anywhere on the client, so no `StreamLocalBindUnlink`, no chosen-path unlink, and no orphaned-tunnel false-healthy state; a plain pipe pair, which the five generic framing helpers already take; and it is the Windows answer at no extra cost, since a Windows client never needs a named pipe to reach a *remote* daemon and Windows 10+ ships OpenSSH.

**1B was not chosen now, and the reason is arithmetic about concurrency.** The desktop holds *concurrent* connections, not one: roughly one attach socket per shown terminal (a nine-tile deck is nine, plus a warm set bounded at 3), plus one long-lived event subscription, plus transient request connections — call it ~11 at once. Under 1B each of those is a separate `ssh` child with its own TCP connection and its own authentication, and each is an `exec` session, so they count against sshd's `MaxSessions`, whose **default is 10** — squarely inside our range. Under 1A a single `ssh` child carries all of them as **forwarding channels** over one authenticated connection, and forwarding channels are not sessions. `UNVERIFIED:` that last clause is recalled OpenSSH semantics rather than a measurement taken here. It is not load-bearing for 1A, which avoids the question entirely, but it **is** the thing that has to hold before 1B ships, so whoever picks up the follow-up measures it first.

1B is filed as the deferred follow-up that removes the local socket and unlocks the Windows desktop. Its own costs are known: a remote daemon whose binary predates the verb fails with `unknown subcommand` — loud, not silent, and the same class as a protocol floor — and it needs the remote binary's path resolved, for which `src/connect.rs`'s `install_path` machinery is the precedent.

**And the local socket stops being the defect it is today, for a reason worth stating precisely.** The disguise was harmful because the client *did not know* it was remote. Under `Endpoint::Remote` it knows: the transport owns that socket, the local trust check is not run against it, its presence is never read as health, and the app owns its lifetime. It is an implementation detail of the remote transport rather than a false claim about the endpoint's nature.

**Four things about the child are not optional**, each a measured cost of shelling out rather than an argument against it. `ssh` is resolved by **absolute path** at startup and never as a bare `"ssh"` from `PATH`, because a GUI launched from Finder or a `.desktop` entry inherits launchd's or the session manager's environment and a user-writable directory earlier in `PATH` can substitute a binary (`src/login_shell.rs` exists because this repo already has this problem). `BatchMode=yes` is forced, because a GUI has no tty: without it ssh tries to read the host-key prompt from a closed stdin and refuses anyway, but the app gets an unclassified failure instead of a classified one — and on a Linux desktop with `SSH_ASKPASS` and `DISPLAY` set, ssh may pop **an unrelated third-party dialog the app did not draw**. Nine of `apply_observation_options`' ten options are inherited, all but `ClearAllForwardings`. And the child's lifetime is owned — process group, reap, and kill on quit including the paths where `Drop` does not run — which was genuinely new work: both pre-existing `Command::new("ssh")` sites block on the child.

**Where a tunnel lives is a design decision, not an implementation detail.** A `TrustedDaemon` is re-established every `HANDSHAKE_REVALIDATE_INTERVAL` (5 s), so a tunnel owned by one would re-authenticate ssh every five seconds: tunnel lifetime is not handshake lifetime. [`desktop/src-tauri/src/endpoint_tunnels.rs`](../../desktop/src-tauri/src/endpoint_tunnels.rs) is the owner — keyed by `EndpointIdentity`, a field of `DesktopState`, shared by `Arc` with `DaemonLinks` because establishment is merely where a transport is *needed*, and **never** a field of `TrustedDaemon`. Its module docs carry the five ownership rules and the four teardown triggers. A live terminal session holds its own `TunnelLease`, because a session's write half outlives the request that opened it by the whole life of the tile.

### The generated ssh config, and why its layout is load-bearing

This is the subtlest part of the transport, and a reader who does not know why will undo it. The tunnel is invoked with `-F <generated file>`, and that file is:

```text
Host *
    <forced_options()>                  first-value-wins: must precede the Includes
Include ~/.ssh/config                   the user's config still decides
Include /etc/ssh/ssh_config             -F suppresses this file, so put it back
Host *
    <forced_last_wins_options()>        last-value-wins: must follow the Includes
```

**Two `Host *` blocks, because OpenSSH resolves different options in different directions.** Almost every option is first-value-wins — `readconf.c` assigns only when the field is still at its sentinel — which is why a leading `Host *` block beats whatever the user's config says later. `StreamLocalBindMask` is parsed with an **unconditional** assignment, so for it the *last* value wins and the usual reasoning inverts. Measured on OpenSSH 10.2p1, all three cases: ours first and theirs second resolves to theirs; theirs first and ours second resolves to ours; and `-o StreamLocalBindMask=…` with theirs in the config resolves to theirs. So the obvious fix — "add `StreamLocalBindMask=0177` to `forced_options()`" — does **not** hold, and a trailing block after the includes is the only channel that forces it.

**Why a generated file at all, when `-o` exists.** `-o` reaches the `ssh` process we spawn and nothing else. When a jump host is set, OpenSSH implements `ProxyJump` by synthesising an implicit `ProxyCommand` that runs a **fresh `ssh`** carrying only `-l`, `-p`, `-J`, `-F` and `-v` — not one `-o` survives. Measured on OpenSSH 10.2p1 with `Host * / ForwardAgent yes` in the user's config and every option on the command line, the *jump* hop resolved `forwardagent yes`, `batchmode no` and `stricthostkeychecking false`: the user's ssh-agent was forwarded to the bastion for the whole life of the tunnel, with the GUI showing nothing. `-F` **is** propagated into that implicit `ProxyCommand`, so the generated config is the one channel that reaches both hops. Re-measured with the file in place, the jump hop resolves `forwardagent no`, `batchmode yes`, `stricthostkeychecking true`, `updatehostkeys false` and `streamlocalbindmask 0177`. The `-o` flags stay as well, because they are what holds if the file is ever read differently than we expect and because the values are identical, so first-value-wins makes the duplication inert.

**`-F` is passed unconditionally, not only when a jump host is configured.** The interpolation is conditional on `-F` being on the command line, not on `-J` being there — measured with `Host * / ProxyJump bastion` in the included file and no `-J` of our own: the implicit `ProxyCommand` still carried `-F <path>`. A jump-only `-F` would therefore leave open the case where the *user's* config adds a jump hop the app never asked for, which has the whole of the problem above.

Four more facts the layout rests on, each measured rather than reasoned from the manual: an `Include` of a **missing** file is not an error, so a user with no `~/.ssh/config` resolves cleanly; a second `Host *` block after the includes still applies, and still applies when the included file ends inside a `Match` block; `-F` **suppresses `/etc/ssh/ssh_config`**, so without the second `Include` a site config would silently stop being read the moment this file existed, and including it after the user's preserves ssh's normal user-over-system precedence; and a *relative* `Include` inside the user's own config still resolves against `~/.ssh` even when reached through ours, so the common `Include conf.d/*` pattern survives being nested one level deeper.

**Writing the file fails the tunnel closed, and that is the design rather than caution.** Without it, the forced options reach only the direct hop — so a tunnel that came up anyway would be exactly the silent agent-forward to the bastion the file exists to prevent. The same rule applies to `Test connection`'s own short-lived `ProbeConfig`: a probe that ran without the file could resolve and *report* a configuration with the user's `ForwardAgent yes` still in force on the jump hop.

**The path the file lives at is itself an ssh input.** `-F`'s value is interpolated **unquoted** into the `ProxyCommand` string OpenSSH hands to `/bin/sh`, and that string is percent-expanded first. Measured: a `%` in the path fails the connection with `vdollar_percent_expand: unknown key`, which no classifier here names; a space makes the jump hop read `-F <first-word>` and treat the rest as a destination. That is why the path check refuses `%`, whitespace and the shell metacharacters. The metacharacter half is worth stating carefully: with an existing config file named `a;touch marker;b.conf`, OpenSSH 10.2 **did** build the string — the implicit `ProxyCommand` carried it verbatim — and the run then ended without creating the marker, for a reason that investigation did not isolate. So the honest claim is that the string *reaches* the `ProxyCommand`, not that 10.2 executes it or that 10.2 refuses it; the app runs against whatever OpenSSH the user has, so refusing the byte here is the defence either way.

### What your own ssh config still decides

DECISION 1A inherits the user's ssh config on purpose — that is most of what shelling out to `ssh` buys — so some of what the tunnel does is not ours to force. These are residuals, not oversights, and **Test connection** is where a user can see the two that matter.

- **Your forwards are inherited for the whole life of the tunnel.** `ClearAllForwardings` is not forced, because it clears command-line forwards too and would therefore clear our own `-L`, and OpenSSH has no per-direction alternative. So a `Host *` block carrying `RemoteForward`, `DynamicForward` or `LocalForward` applies to the direct hop — measured with `Host * { RemoteForward 9999 localhost:22; DynamicForward 1080 }`: both resolve. `remote doctor` refuses to create such a forward at all and calls it a criterion violation; the tunnel makes the same exposure for orders of magnitude longer. `ExitOnForwardFailure=yes` sharpens it in a second, non-obvious way: a config `RemoteForward` that fails to bind now kills the tunnel, because that option applies to **every** requested forward and not only ours. The *jump* hop is exempt, and that is measured rather than assumed — `-J` invokes it with `-W`, which sets `clearallforwardings yes`. **`Test connection` discloses this**: it reuses `remote_doctor::parse_ssh_g` against the tunnel's own `-F` config and forced options, so what it reports is what the tunnel will actually inherit. The panel renders **all three answers in words**, which is PRD #741's final audit F1: *"This deck's tunnel will also carry, from your ssh config: …"* with the list, *"Your ssh config adds no forwards to this deck's tunnel."* when it read the config and found none, and *"This test could not read your resolved ssh config…"* when the resolution did not run or came back truncated. Rendering only the non-empty list made the second and third answers both come out as a blank screen, which is the one claim that block must not make, arrived at from the render rather than from the parse. The parser side of the same finding: a forward line whose value `ssh -G` printed unquoted — `LocalForward "/Users/First Last/db.sock" …`, or any Unix-socket forward, which is the shape this app itself uses — is reported as unreadable rather than dropped, so the list is never quietly shorter than the config.
- **Your config decides where host keys are checked against.** `StrictHostKeyChecking=yes` is forced on both hops, so the check happens; `KnownHostsCommand`, `UserKnownHostsFile` and `GlobalKnownHostsFile` are inherited, so what the check is anchored to is the config's. Measured: all three survive a `Host *` block onto both hops, and a `KnownHostsCommand` that emits whatever key the server presents satisfies the forced `yes` against any host. All three are forceable and deliberately are not forced — `KnownHostsCommand` is how CA- and inventory-driven fleets distribute host keys, and a per-project `UserKnownHostsFile` is ordinary — so **`Test connection` discloses this too**, beside the forwards and from the same `ssh -G` run: *"Host keys for this deck are checked against: …"*. The block is additive, so an empty one asserts nothing (a local deck runs no `ssh` and has no source to name); "could not look" is covered by the forwards sentence above, which is gated on the same fact.
- **Your config can still run code.** `ProxyCommand`, `Match exec` (which runs at config-parse time on *every* invocation, and is not closed by `PermitLocalCommand=no` — measured), `KnownHostsCommand`, and `PKCS11Provider` / `SecurityKeyProvider` `dlopen`s. None is closable without breaking `ProxyJump`, which DECISION 1A exists to inherit.
- **Your config can retarget the connection.** A `Hostname` inside a matching `Host` block silently sends ssh somewhere else, so `describe()` can name a different host than ssh reaches.
- **On macOS, nothing bounds an orphan `ssh` after a force-quit.** `process_cmdline_mentions` is Linux-only, so the orphan reaper unlinks the socket and leaves the process running. It holds an authenticated session until its keepalives notice a dead link, or indefinitely if the far end stays up. `proc_pidpath` / `sysctl KERN_PROCARGS2` would close it.

### Configuring a remote deck by hand

The remote leg needs a **second machine** for the part that is genuinely about two machines — what the client *renders*, which is the half still unticked. It does not need one for the **transport**, and it does not need one for **who resolved a path**; both of those are now covered on one box, and the sentence that used to sit here ("a loopback `ssh` to the same box is not a remote test: locally the client's filesystem *is* the daemon's, so every path assertion passes whichever side resolved it") was one claim too wide in both directions.

- **The transport is covered by `fleet/observe/002`** (`tests/e2e_remote_deck_transport.rs`, lane 1). A loopback tunnel is a *complete* test of the transport: a real `ssh -N -L` child, a real forwarded Unix socket, the real `EndpointConnection::open` arm, the forced host-key check really running, and a real teardown. Sharing a filesystem takes nothing away from any of that. It runs against an `sshd` the test starts itself, so it touches no operator account; see that file's header.
- **"Whichever side resolved it" is covered by `project/resolve/002`** (`tests/e2e_project_verbs.rs`, lane 1). What has to differ to make that falsifiable is not the *filesystem* but the **resolution inputs**, and those can diverge on one machine: the daemon starts in one project directory and the client stands in another, equally valid one, and `ListProjects` must name the daemon's. Measured by inversion — start the daemon where the client stands and the listing carries the client's directory and the test goes red. Note `HOME` is *not* the lever: no project-resolution path reads it (`ResolveProject` refuses a non-absolute path, `read_project_config` does not walk upwards), and the harness has given every daemon its own `HOME` all along.

What a second machine is still needed for is the **display** half — that the project header and agent panes name the daemon's paths and never the client's — which is what [#819](https://github.com/vfarcic/dot-agent-deck/issues/819) left its M10 unticked for.

On the machine that will host the deck:

1. Run `dot-agent-deck daemon serve` (or start the TUI, which lazy-spawns one) and leave it running with at least one agent under it, so there is something to see.
2. Note its attach socket path. The daemon logs it at startup as `Attach protocol listening on <path>`, and it is typically `/run/user/<uid>/dot-agent-deck-attach.sock` on Linux. You do not have to get this now — step 6 discovers it.

On the machine running the desktop app:

3. Make sure plain `ssh <host>` works from a terminal first, non-interactively. The tunnel forces `BatchMode=yes`, so anything that would prompt — an unknown host key, a locked key with no agent — fails instead of asking. If the host key is not yet in your `known_hosts`, this is the step that puts it there.
4. Open **Settings → Decks**, press **Add a deck**, and fill in the host. Leave `User` empty if your `~/.ssh/config` already decides it; leave `Key file` empty if your key is in an agent; set `Jump host` to a `Host` block *name* if you reach the machine through a bastion.
5. Press **Test connection**. Read the state it reports — the twelve of them are below — and fix what it names.
6. On a successful test the **Deck socket** field fills itself in from discovery. Leave it; it is now stored, so the next connection needs no probe.
7. Select the deck in the same panel, or from the **Deck selector** in the top bar of the deck and the agent overview. Selecting a deck detaches every live terminal session, ends the event subscription, and emits a fresh snapshot for the chosen deck — a tile left attached to a deck that is no longer selected is a tile whose keystrokes go to another machine's agent.
8. Or select **All Decks**, which watches this row *and* every other configured deck at once and merges them on the agent overview. The deck screen stays on one deck — All Decks resolves it to the local one — so the terminals do not move; [All Decks](#all-decks-the-fleet-and-the-one-screen-that-merges-it) is what that selection does and why the split is there. **Test connection** tests one deck, so it is disabled while `all` is selected and says why.

**What differs once you are on a remote deck**, none of it silent: **Stop deck** and **Replace deck** are disabled with a reason, and project and workflow actions gate on the daemon's advertised capability set and name what is missing. A build-stamp difference is not one of the differences any more — since [#801](https://github.com/vfarcic/dot-agent-deck/issues/801) it refuses no deck of [either kind](#compatibility-is-classified-from-the-contract-not-from-a-git-tag-801), where PRD #741 M8 had demoted it for remote decks alone.

### The twelve `Test connection` states

**Test connection** exercises the whole transport stack without any screen having to work first, which is what makes it the thing to reach for when a remote deck misbehaves. Every outcome is a **distinct named state** rather than one "failed" — `EndpointTestState` in [`desktop/src-tauri/src/endpoint_test.rs`](../../desktop/src-tauri/src/endpoint_test.rs) — and they are declared in the order the probe reaches them: what stops it before it leaves this machine, then the ssh hop, then the deck at the other end.

| state | what it means, and what to do |
| --- | --- |
| `unknown_deck` | The selection names a row this document no longer holds — removed by hand, or a token a newer build wrote. `SelectionFallback`'s own case, reported as itself. **It carries a second, opposite case since PRD #742 M6**: the selection is `all`, which is every deck at once rather than a deck to test, and the report says so and names no deck. The state is shared rather than split because a thirteenth variant would mean a thirteenth arm in the TypeScript union and a thirteenth row in every table that enumerates them, to distinguish two reports that differ only in one sentence — but note what that costs the reader: `unknown_deck` no longer means "your selection is not in force", since under All Decks it plainly is. |
| `ssh_unavailable` | No `ssh` program at any of the standard absolute locations. Nothing about the deck is wrong; this machine has no client. Install an OpenSSH client. |
| `no_remote_socket` | The row has no socket path and discovery could not produce one. Usually means the deck is not running over there, since discovery asks it. |
| `host_unreachable` | ssh could not reach the host at all: refused, unresolvable, no route, or timed out. |
| `host_key_unverified` | The host's key is not in this machine's `known_hosts`, or it changed. Carries the run-`ssh`-once remedy, built from the endpoint the tunnel actually uses so the bastion and the port survive — an earlier version built it from an unvalidated `SshTarget` and named a different endpoint than the one that failed. |
| `auth_failed` | ssh reached the host and the login was refused: no usable key, wrong user. |
| `transport_failed` | ssh authenticated and something else about the transport failed — a forward that would not bind, a config that would not write, a child that died saying something this build cannot name. |
| `deck_not_answering` | The ssh connection is up and nothing is listening on the remote socket path. Usually "Agent Deck is not running over there". |
| `handshake_refused` | A daemon answered and refused the `Hello` itself. |
| `protocol_refused` | A daemon answered and the wire versions disagree. Both numbers are in the report. **The one state no override can reach**: `PROTOCOL_VERSION` is exact equality, checked first, and is the hard floor for every deck kind. |
| `contract_differs` | The wire agreed and the two builds' **declared contract breaks** did not, so a compatibility break somebody wrote down sits between them ([#801](https://github.com/vfarcic/dot-agent-deck/issues/801)). Not ok, for either deck kind — the protocol agreed, so it is overridable on the connection screen, but an override is a judgement the user makes there and not something a green tick here should pre-empt. The build stamps are reported beside it and are not what was measured. |
| `reachable` | The deck answered and this build can talk to it. |

The report also carries the handshake's own numbers when it got that far — the server's protocol version, the daemon's build stamp, the live agent count — plus the remedy and the forwards disclosure above. In the browser fixture tier the panel renders the states without reaching anything, and says so: *"Open the packaged app to reach a deck."*

## The agent overview

PRD #745 adds the app's second screen: a fleet overview of every agent the app can see, with **no terminal anywhere on it**. It lives in `desktop/src/components/AgentOverview.tsx`, is reached from the **Overview** button in the left rail, and works in both fixture and live mode. It was *"every agent the daemon owns"* until PRD #742 made it every agent of **every observed deck** — one group per deck, merged into one screen; [All Decks](#all-decks-the-fleet-and-the-one-screen-that-merges-it) is what that means and why this is the screen that merges while the deck screen does not. It renders *instead of* the deck rather than as an overlay sheet, so the view state lives in `App` above `ControlDeck` as a discriminated union (`DeckView`) — a union from the start, even at the two values it carried for its first two PRDs, so later destinations arrive as added variants rather than as a refactor of a boolean. That is what happened: PRD #1105 added a third variant for [one agent's pane](#the-agent-pane-overlay), which is the only variant that does *not* replace the mounted screen. The deck is still the screen the app opens on, and the overview is still not the landing screen — it now has one destination rather than none, and #745's other two iteration-3 parts (the group view, and the promotion itself) are untaken (see [Current milestone limits](#current-milestone-limits)).

### What it shows, and why not more

**Four columns by default, nine available, and the operator chooses.** Out of the box: the agent's **name**, its **status**, its **working directory** and its **uptime** — who it is, whether it is healthy, where it is working, and how long it has been at it. The other five — last activity, CLI, active tool, tool count, last prompt — are one click away in the **Columns** picker in the top bar, and the choice is remembered. See [Columns, and who chooses them](#columns-and-who-chooses-them). Every one of the nine is a value the daemon genuinely reports, and there is no `Unavailable` anywhere on the screen. Model, tokens, cost, context window, git branch and attempt count are **absent rather than shown as unavailable**, because none of them exists in daemon state at all — not on the wire, not in `RunningAgent` (`src/agent_pty.rs`), not in `SessionState` (`src/state.rs`). Inventing a source of truth for them is [PRD #633](https://github.com/vfarcic/dot-agent-deck/issues/633)'s discovery work, not this screen's. Session *duration* was on that list too and is no longer: the uptime column ships one, from a source the daemon genuinely holds — see [Uptime](#uptime).

That decision is enforced by the compiler rather than by review. The screen renders from `OverviewAgent`, a `Pick<>` projection of `AgentSession`, and never from `AgentSession` itself — so reaching for `model`, `cost`, `tokens`, `contextPercent`, `worktree`, `attempt` or `duration` on this screen is a **compile error** rather than something to remember. Two fields are widened past the raw `Pick<>` for the same reason a `Pick<>` exists: it closes dishonest field *names* but cannot close a dishonest *sentinel* inside an allowed one. `cwd` is optional here so a directory the daemon did not report travels as absence the whole way, and `writeLease`'s `"unknown"` sentinel is reversed to absent at this boundary. A cell with nothing to say renders blank — not a dash, and not a placeholder.

Sanitisation is a property of the screen rather than of individual cells: every daemon-supplied string passes through `desktop/src/lib/displayText.ts` before React sees it — rendered text, `title` attributes, and the copies behind `data-*`, DOM ids, IDREFs and React keys — while grouping, sorting and the `(daemonId, agentId)` identity keep the raw values. An agent whose reported display name consists entirely of invisible characters renders as `unnamed agent <id>` rather than as a blank cell, on the one screen whose whole job is telling agents apart.

Two fabricated values on the **deck** were withdrawn by the same principle, since a fabricated value is worse than an absent one — it looks like data. Every tile printed `ATT 01` and the top bar printed `ATTEMPT 01`, both read from a hardcoded `1`, and no daemon tracks a retry count anywhere; the branch line printed a literal `Unavailable` where a branch name belongs, and no daemon tracks a per-agent branch either. In live mode the attempt readouts now show the deck's established em dash and the branch chip is simply absent until something reports a branch. The deterministic fixture keeps its own attempt counts and branch, which are legitimately fixture data.

### Columns, and who chooses them

The picker lives in the overview's top bar, so choosing columns never leaves the screen. It offers exactly nine options, and **it cannot offer a tenth by mistake**: the option list is `COLUMN_FIELDS` in `desktop/src/components/AgentOverview.tsx`, whose ids are declared `satisfies readonly (keyof OverviewAgent)[]`, so a column named `model`, `cost`, `tokens`, `contextPercent`, `worktree`, `attempt` or `duration` is a **compile error** rather than something review has to catch. The picker inherits the screen's honesty guarantee from the same `Pick<>` that enforces it everywhere else, instead of restating it.

**There is deliberately no deck column, and it is still nine under a fleet.** Deck is a **grouping** on this screen rather than a cell — one `daemon-group` section per observed deck, which was already the outer unit — so a column repeating the name in every row of a card that is titled with it would say the same thing twice. `daemonId` *is* an `OverviewAgent` field and a tenth entry naming it would be type-legal, so this is a choice rather than a limit; if it is ever wanted it goes through the existing picker and needs no new mechanism.

**The CLI column names a binary, not an identifier, and the daemon names it.** It shows the *command* that launches the agent (`claude`, `opencode`, `pi`, `codex`, `devin`) — not the serialised `AgentType`, which is the wire identity and reads `claude_code` and `open_code`, neither of them a name anybody types. Since issue [#856](https://github.com/vfarcic/dot-agent-deck/issues/856) the resolution happens **daemon-side**: `attach_cli_names` stamps `AgentRecord.cli_name` at the `ListAgents` wire boundary from `AgentSpec::default_command` in `src/agent_registry.rs`, reading the identity the reply itself reports, and the desktop copies it through to `DesktopAgent.cli_name` without consulting its own copy of that table. Which binary a running agent is, is a fact about the daemon's world — it forked the process. Where the **daemon** names no binary (an agent type whose spec has no default command, `AgentType::None` — which carries `#[serde(other)]` and so absorbs a type from a *newer* peer — or a daemon predating the field) the field is absent and **the cell is empty**. No generic word and no local lookup: a word reads as a fact about the agent, an empty cell reads as "the deck did not say", and a fallback to the client's own registry would reinstate exactly the divergence #856 closed. Same disposition as the uptime and activity columns.

**The name column is permanent.** Its checkbox is checked and disabled, and `orderedColumns` puts it back whatever a stored value asks for — a disabled checkbox governs the UI, not a `localStorage` key an older build wrote. A row with no name is not a shorter row, it is an anonymous one, on the screen whose whole job is telling agents apart.

**There is a way back.** A **Restore defaults** entry at the foot of the menu puts the four back; without it the only route out of a set somebody unticked their way into was remembering which four the screen opened on. It persists through exactly the path every other change does — the selection goes up to the screen, whose effect writes it — so there is no second way for a choice to be saved.

**The menu dismisses on an outside click, and three details are the whole thing.** It listens on `pointerdown` rather than `click`: a click fires *after* focus has already moved, so a menu closing on it closes after whatever was clicked took focus, which reads as the menu lagging behind the user. Anything inside the picker's root element is ignored, and the trigger button lives inside that root — were it outside, its own pointer-down would close the menu and its click would toggle it straight back open, so the button would appear not to work at all. And the listener is bound only while the menu is open and removed when it closes, not merely on unmount, because `open` is in the effect's dependency list. `Escape` still works and was previously the only way out.

**Status is ONE column, and it used to be two.** There was a coloured mark in the first track and a textual State column in the third, both rendered from `agent.status` and neither saying anything the other did not. Two picker entries for one field would have been a question with no right answer, so the mark and the label now share one cell: the colour is still what you scan, the word is still what you read, and removing the column removes both. Row-level status signalling is unaffected either way — `data-status` on the `<tr>` is what tints a failed row, and it is not a column.

**The choice is remembered, per mode.** It is written to `dot-agent-deck.desktop.overview-columns.v1.<mode>` through `modeScopedKey`, for the reason every persisted key in this app is scoped that way: a fixture visit must not hand live mode a layout, and vice versa. `readStoredColumns` is where every way a stored value can be unusable turns back into something renderable, and each case is about a value an **older build** wrote rather than about today:

- **Absent, unparseable, or the wrong shape** — a bare string, a number, an object with no `columns` array — takes the defaults. A `SyntaxError` on mount would take the whole screen down.
- **A column that no longer exists is dropped**, not carried through. A retired or renamed id would otherwise render as a `<th>` with no cell under it and one dead grid track down every card — a fault that looks like a layout bug and is really a migration one.
- **Nothing recognisable left** — every stored id unknown, or an empty array — takes the defaults rather than collapsing the screen to the single permanent column. A user cannot reach that state by unticking, because the permanent column has no checkbox to untick.

Whatever survives is written straight back, so the next visit reads a value this build understands.

**No column is hidden by viewport any more.** Two media queries used to drop uptime, CLI and the working directory below 1180px and the active tool, tool count and prompt below 680px, by `nth-child` index. Once the operator picks the columns, hiding one by window width fights them silently — and the index-sensitivity is what made adding a column a five-rule renumbering across two queries plus three hardcoded templates. The narrow case is a **horizontal scroll of the whole table region** instead: the legend and every group card sit inside one `.overview-table-region`, which is the load-bearing part. All the cards share one `grid-template-columns` — generated from the selection and published as the `--overview-grid` custom property on the region's track — and that shared template is what makes the fleet read as a single table across card boundaries. Scrolling cards individually would let two of them sit at different horizontal offsets and the columns would stop lining up, precisely when the chosen set is wide enough to overflow. `min-width: min-content` on the track is what produces a scrollbar at all: every flexible column's track carries a fixed px minimum (`minmax(150px, 1.3fr)`, not `minmax(0, 1.3fr)`), so the grid's min-content width is the sum of those minimums — a fixed number — rather than zero (tracks crushed to nothing, no overflow) or the width of the longest prompt on screen.

### Grouping

The daemon's own `TabMembership` is the grouping key, so the overview groups agents exactly as the deck's tabs already do: one group per orchestration, one per mode name, and one standalone bucket for dashboard panes. **Standalone leads, then orchestrations, then modes.** That order is TUI parity rather than aesthetics — the TUI's dashboard tab is always first, so an overview that buried the same agents at the bottom would describe a different deck than the one running beside it. Orchestrations and modes follow in first-appearance order; roles inside an orchestration are sorted by `roleIndex`, and the start role carries a `COORDINATOR` badge.

The outermost unit is a **daemon group**, not an agent group. With one deck it renders as minimal chrome: a connection lamp, the words `Local deck`, and the per-status pips; under **All Decks** the overview maps over the observed decks and renders one of these per deck, a remote one named by its address rather than by `Local deck`. **No socket filename is on screen.** A shortened label used to sit under the name, and its stated purpose was keeping a uid or a username out of screenshots — but on the default socket that label reads `dot-agent-deck-attach-501.sock`, so it printed the very uid it existed to hide and told the reader nothing they could act on either way. The full path is genuinely diagnostic, so it lives on the name's own `title`, where it costs no layout; `data-daemon-id` on the section carries the sanitised, bounded identity for tests and a future drill-in — since #742 M5 that attribute is `connection.deckId` rather than the label, so a test asserting which section it found cannot be fooled by two decks that describe identically. The two build stamps disclose themselves through that same hover when they differ (issue #801) — they used to hang off the connection message, which for a healthy connection no longer renders, and on the daemon's own name they are more discoverable than they were, because a reader hovers a thing they can see. Agents are keyed by the composite `(daemonId, agentId)` from day one, because agent ids are per-daemon monotonic integers starting at 1 and two daemons both mint `"1"`.

**Both decisions were made to stop [#742](https://github.com/vfarcic/dot-agent-deck/issues/742) being a rewrite, and the prediction held — with one correction that is worth the paragraph.** A second deck did become a sibling group and changed no inner component: M4 lifted `daemon-group` from one hardcoded section to a `.map` over the observed decks and `groupAgents` was untouched. What the composite key did **not** buy is protection against the `daemonId` half itself colliding, which it did, because that half was derived from `socketPath` — see [`socketPath` is a label; `deckId` is the key](#socketpath-is-a-label-deckid-is-the-key). The lesson is narrow and general: a composite key is only as distinguishing as its least distinguishing component.

**The connection message renders only when it says something the lamp does not.** The lamp is `connection-<status>`; the message beside it, for a healthy connection, was literally `Deck responding` — two renderings of one bit. It is suppressed for that case and for no other: the disconnected and incompatible explanations stay, and so does the build-mismatch caveat that must remain visible for the whole session after **Connect anyway**. That last one is why the test is `!connected || buildStampMismatchOnly`, the same flag Connect anyway is gated on, and not a naive "hide when connected" — the bypassed-mismatch state *is* `connected`, and hiding its message would silently undo #801's guarantee.

### Last activity

The **Last activity** column is the one thing on the screen that needed a daemon-side change. `last_activity_ms` was added to `SessionSnapshot` as an additive optional field tagged `#[serde(default, skip_serializing_if = "Option::is_none")]`, which `src/daemon_protocol.rs`'s own policy names as an explicit do-not-bump case, so it moved `PROTOCOL_VERSION` by nothing and older and newer peers interoperate in both directions — a new app against an older daemon simply shows an empty column. (The constant has moved since — to 9 for the unrelated request variants PRD #819 added, and to 10 for issue #1049's `StopDaemon`. It was 8 when this column landed and the field is additive either way.) It crosses the wire as epoch milliseconds rather than a formatted string, so the relative wording stays the webview's decision, and it carries its unit in its name because a bare integer invites the seconds-versus-milliseconds mistake that turns every reading into fifty-seven years.

It is on the screen where a duration built from `SessionState.started_at` is not, and the line between them is honesty rather than taste. `started_at` is invented as `Utc::now()` on hydration, so a duration built on it resets under a restarted daemon and silently lies about long-running work. (The screen *does* carry a duration now — see [Uptime](#uptime) — from a source the daemon genuinely holds. The narrow claim here, about `started_at`, still stands; it is only ever that source this section is rejecting.) `last_activity` is a high-water mark of observed event timestamps, advanced only when a newer frame arrives, so an agent quiet for an hour snapshots as quiet for an hour. And the daemon-restart case resolves to **absence** rather than to a lie, for free: the daemon persists no `AppState`, so a restarted daemon has no sessions at all, `AgentRecord.live` is `None`, the field never reaches the wire, and every cell renders empty. A duration had no absent state to fall back to, because `started_at` is always populated with *something*.

`displayActivity` in `desktop/src/lib/displayText.ts` renders one unit, largest that fits, floored — `just now`, `34m ago`, `2h ago`, `3d ago` — with the exact UTC instant on hover. It renders **nothing at all**, with no hover, for every value it cannot express honestly: absent, non-finite, outside `Date`'s ±100,000,000-day range, or more than a minute in the future. That last one is the clock-skew rule. The instant is stamped by whichever hook process emitted the event rather than by the daemon, so a small positive skew is the ordinary case and one minute of it reads `just now`; beyond that the value is deliberately *not* rewritten into `just now`, because a cheerful `just now` for a stamp ten years out is the same fabrication as a `started_at`-derived duration would be, and a negative `-60m ago` is merely the more obvious bug of the two. Note the desktop is **stricter than the TUI** here, whose `format_elapsed` clamps unconditionally to `0s`; the divergence is intentional and one-directional, since the TUI never renders a negative either.

One consequence is deliberately left alone: the TUI's reconnect hydration does not overlay `last_activity` from the snapshot, so a reconnected TUI card's `Last:` readout still resets. That is a pre-existing inaccuracy which this field has made *fixable* rather than fixed — `last_activity` is the ordering evidence `supersedes_generation` weighs and the key the `ListAgents` newest-wins join selects on, so seeding it belongs in its own change with its own tests.

### Uptime

The **Uptime** column is the second daemon-side change, and it is the duration this screen used to refuse. The refusal was aimed at `SessionState.started_at`, and that aim was right but the reason given for it — that `started_at` is invented as `now` on hydration — understated the problem. `started_at` is **event-derived**: a session exists only once a hook event has arrived, so an agent that has never emitted one has no start instant at all, and that is exactly the agent whose uptime a reader most wants.

The source that works is **when the daemon forked the process**. `AgentPtyRegistry::spawn_agent` stamps `RunningAgent.spawned_at` immediately after `spawn()` returns and nothing ever rewrites it; it reaches the wire as `AgentRecord.spawned_at_ms`, an additive optional field on the same do-not-bump basis as `last_activity_ms`, so it moved `PROTOCOL_VERSION` by nothing. It is an **observation rather than an inference** — signal-independent, present for an agent that has never reported anything, and something the daemon definitionally knows. It is also never invented: `spawn_agent` is the only site that writes a value, so a record that arrives any other way (an older daemon's id-only `ListAgents` reply, the synthetic test seam) carries `None` and every consumer renders nothing. There is no `Utc::now()` fallback anywhere on the path.

**What the number means follows from where it comes from, with no flag needed.** A `clear = true` delegate respawns its worker by removing the old registry record outright and spawning a fresh one, so a restarted worker reports the age of its **current iteration**; a role nobody has restarted — an orchestrator, typically — keeps its original record and reports its **whole lifetime**. And because `agent_records()` filters entries whose child has exited, a spawn instant never outlives the process it describes and ticks up as a phantom uptime.

`displayUptime` in `desktop/src/lib/displayText.ts` renders it: the same buckets `displayActivity` uses, worded as a span rather than as a moment — `<1m`, `34m`, `2h`, `3d`, with the exact UTC spawn instant on hover. No `ago`, because the interval named is still running. The two functions share **one** clock-skew and usability guard (`relativeTo`) and fork only the vocabulary, so the rule a reader learns for one cell holds for the other: absent, non-finite, outside `Date`'s range or more than a minute in the future all render as nothing at all. Sharing the guard is deliberate — the three refusals *are* the policy, and a second copy of them is a second thing to keep true.

### The attach model

This is the least obvious part of the desktop client and the easiest to break, so it is written out in full.

**"Shows no output" and "opens no PTY" are separate properties, and only the first comes free.** Attaching a PTY was never tied to rendering one. `attachAgents` used to fire from `connect()`, from every `desktop://snapshot` event, and from the `start_daemon` action, attaching every agent in the snapshot regardless of what was on screen — filtered on an `attached` set rather than on anything mounted, and `TerminalViewport` triggered nothing at all. Each attach opens a real daemon `AttachStream` socket, and an `AttachStream` replays the full scrollback before it starts streaming, so a nine-agent fleet cost nine sockets and nine replays behind a screen displaying none of them.

Attach is now driven by one declaration, `DeckBridge.setShownTerminals(targets)` — real on `TauriDeckBridge`, a genuine no-op on `FixtureDeckBridge`, which owns no PTYs. A target is the composite `{ deckId, agentId }`; see [the attach model has a deck dimension](#the-attach-model-has-a-deck-dimension) below for why it is not a bare id. It is the only attach trigger left. Its semantics, in the order they matter:

- **Shown terminals are always attached, and are never capped.** This is the constraint that shaped everything else: the deck mounts a terminal on every tile, so a bound applied over everything *attached* would kill six of nine visible panes the moment a nine-agent fleet is displayed.
- **Leaving a terminal does not detach it.** It moves into a bounded **warm** set, still attached, so bouncing between the handful of agents you are actually working with costs no scrollback replay.
- **The warm set alone is bounded**, by `MAX_WARM_TERMINALS` (`3`, exported from `desktop/src/lib/bridge.ts`), least-recently-left evicted first. Eviction is a full client-side teardown before the daemon is told: every map keyed by agent id — sessions, terminal channels, pending attachments, pending resizes, resize frames — is cleared, and only then does `desktop_terminal_detach` go out. A surviving channel entry would let a dead attach keep delivering output, and a surviving resize entry would push a size computed for the old pane at the next session.
- **An empty shown set flushes warm to zero**, not down to the bound. That is what makes the overview's "no terminals attached" true when you arrive from a nine-tile deck, rather than true only on a cold start.

**`setShownTerminals` is declarative, and must be called once per render commit with the whole set — never once per tile.** Nine single-id calls would leave eight of the nine warm and evict five of them, which is the same broken deck the bound exists to avoid. The deck does this in `desktop/src/App.tsx`, deriving the shown ids from the tiles whose tab is `"terminal"` (repeating `AgentTile`'s `?? "terminal"` default exactly) and keying one effect on those ids joined with a newline. That joined string is a dependency key and nothing else — never split it back apart, since a raw agent id containing a newline would come back out as two shown agents. The overview declares the empty set the same way — from `DeckShell` since PRD #1105 M4, not from `AgentOverview`, which declares nothing for itself any more. The move is why: an agent pane sits *over* a base screen rather than instead of one, so an overview that declared `[]` for itself while a pane above it needed `[agentId]` would be two declarations in one commit, which is the thing this rule forbids. [The attach model with the pane open](#the-attach-model-with-the-pane-open) has the rest.

What the overview does **not** claim is that every socket a previous screen opened is already gone by the time it renders. The declaration is fire-and-forget, and an attach still in flight is cancelled by *marking* rather than by awaiting — one slow attach must not freeze every later terminal switch — so its daemon-side tear-down lands shortly afterwards. The screen used to say exactly that in a footnote rather than claim the stronger thing; the footnote is gone (the app does not annotate its own design), so the precise claim lives here and in `AgentOverview`'s docstring.

The property is pinned at the bridge level rather than by inspection. `desktop/src/lib/bridge.test.ts` drives `TauriDeckBridge` directly — the live bridge, the only one that owns a PTY at all — and asserts that a nine-agent snapshot attaches zero terminals while nothing is shown, and that `connect()` alone attaches nothing. Note the asymmetry when you touch this code: deleting the deck's effect fails no bridge test, because the bridge is behaving correctly; it silently leaves the deck with no attached terminals at all.

#### The attach model has a deck dimension

**It did not, until the agent pane learnt to attach across decks.** `setShownTerminals` took bare agent ids, every map in `TauriDeckBridge` was keyed by bare agent id, and `desktop_terminal_attach` took an `agent_id` and nothing else — while agent ids are per-daemon monotonic integers, so two decks both mint `"1"`. That held for one reason and one only: `terminal::attach` resolved its daemon through `trusted_daemon()`, the process-global `selected_endpoint()`, so every attach this app issued went to whichever deck was selected at the time. A bare key was adequate because of the **single-deck screen**, not because of anything in the maps — they had no way to tell two decks' `"1"` apart and nothing asked them to.

**PRD #1105 is what asks.** The overview merges every observed deck, and the product decision is that any agent it lists opens as a working pane with a live terminal — the desktop app's whole reason to exist over the TUI (PRD [#802](https://github.com/vfarcic/dot-agent-deck/issues/802)). So a deck-screen tile on the selected deck and an overview pane on another deck are attached at the same time, both possibly called `planner`. Four things changed together, and none of them is optional on its own:

- **`setShownTerminals` takes `{ deckId, agentId }` targets**, and every per-agent map in `TauriDeckBridge` is keyed by `agentKey(deckId, agentId)` (`desktop/src/lib/agentKey.ts`) — `shown`, `warm`, `attached`, `sessions`, channels, pending attachments, pending resizes, applied geometry, the attach guard. The key is NUL-joined and is **never split back apart**; where the pair is needed again it travels beside the key as a value.
- **`desktop_terminal_attach` takes a `deckId`**, and `terminal::attach` resolves that deck's link through `DaemonLinks::trusted` instead of `trusted_daemon()`. The id is matched against `observed_decks()` and refused if it names nothing there (`terminal::endpoint_for_deck`), so a value from the webview can never become an address.
- **A `TerminalSession` stores the `Endpoint` it was attached over**, which is what makes the other three verbs deck-correct without a parameter of their own. Write and detach already resolved by session id; `resize` did not, and now takes its daemon from the session's endpoint rather than from the applied selection.
- **The session registry evicts by `(deck, agent)`.** `insert_unique_session` compared agent ids alone, so attaching build-box's `planner` closed the live local `planner` a user was watching.

**Read "adequate" as having been scoped to the maps torn down with the selection**, which is the correction the first security audit made and which the above generalises. "Every attach goes to the selected deck" protects a map whose entries die when the selection moves; it protects nothing about a map that **survives** one — and the runtime's retained PTY buffers, recorded send verdicts and applied geometry all did. Those three are keyed by `(deckId, agentId)` too (see [The composite identity, all the way down](#the-composite-identity-all-the-way-down)).

#### What a selection move now tears down, which is not "everything"

`retarget_selection` called `terminal::detach_all` whenever the selected deck moved. That was right while attach was process-global: every session belonged to the selected deck by construction.

**It is now wrong in both directions, so the rule changed rather than being relaxed.** A session names its own deck and resolves its own link, so a selection move says nothing about it — and tearing one down would close a pane the user deliberately opened on another machine, which is the feature. Meanwhile a deck can *leave the observed set* without the selected deck moving at all (removing a row while `All` is selected), and the old `if moved` gate could not see that case: its transport was retained away and its watcher ended under a session still holding a stream.

So the rule is **`terminal::detach_decks_outside(state, &observed)`, run unconditionally** — the exact sibling of `EndpointTunnels::retain` and `DesktopState::retain_watchers`, one line earlier so a DETACH frame still has a transport to travel over. What ends a session is now one of three things, and a selection change is none of them:

| trigger | what it detaches |
|---|---|
| a deck leaves the observed set | that deck's sessions (`detach_decks_outside`, from `retarget_selection`) |
| Stop deck / Replace deck | that deck's sessions (`detach_deck`) — these refuse anything but the local deck, so tearing down a remote pane would be collateral |
| app exit | everything (`detach_all`, now its only caller) |

Both directions are pinned at the **call site** rather than only on the helper, which matters: `a_selection_move_keeps_a_still_observed_decks_terminal` and `a_deck_leaving_the_fleet_loses_its_terminal_without_the_selection_moving` (`desktop/src-tauri/src/terminal.rs`) drive `retarget_selection` itself, and restoring `if moved { detach_all }` turns both red. A test that drove only the helper stayed green against that restoration, which is the "passes either way" trap.

### The connection states

The overview owns six states, and every one of them is reachable in the fixture without a daemon. **Since PRD #742 each is a state of *a deck* rather than of the screen** — one per group, so a fleet can hold several at once, and the table below reads as what a single deck's group says. `?fixture=1&state=fleet` is the scenario where they are on screen together: four decks, two connected, one that stopped answering and one that has not reported yet.

It was four until PRD #742, and the two it added are both about a deck that is in the fleet and has nothing to show, which the four could only express as a failure. Both keep the deck in the header's **denominator** and out of its numerator; neither offers a button, because in neither is there anything for the reader to press.

| State | What the screen says | Fixture URL |
|---|---|---|
| Connected | The fleet, grouped. `state=crowded` is fifteen agents across two orchestrations, a mode bucket and standalone panes — a four-agent fixture cannot answer a question about many agents | `?fixture=1&state=crowded`, or `?fixture=1` for the default four |
| Connected, zero agents | "No agents are running yet" — the first-run screen, stated explicitly as what a fresh install looks like rather than as a failure, with what to do next | `?fixture=1&state=empty` |
| Disconnected | Nothing can be said about the fleet until a daemon answers, so the list is blank rather than stale | `?fixture=1&state=disconnected` |
| Incompatible | A daemon answered the handshake but this build cannot read its agent list, with its reported running-agent count when it gave one | `?fixture=1&state=error` |
| Not configured (M12) | A configured deck whose socket path `Test connection` has not filled in — nothing was asked of it and nothing failed, so it gets the settings panel's own words rather than the disconnected note's | the third deck of `?fixture=1&state=fleet` is disconnected, not this; reachable live by adding a row and leaving `socket` blank |
| Waiting (M14) | A deck the app has been told exists and has not heard from yet — its watcher is acquiring a tunnel, handshaking and reading an agent list. Stated as what it is rather than as a failure | the fourth deck of `?fixture=1&state=fleet` |

A seventh, `loading`, is the initial seed and is not selectable from the URL. It and **Waiting** share a `status` and are told apart by `connection.pending`: the seed is the app having no deck, and Waiting is a deck having no snapshot.

**Waiting is bounded rather than hopeful, and that is the half worth knowing.** The three replies that stand between a deck joining the fleet and its first snapshot — the handshake, the event subscription and the `ListAgents` — each waited indefinitely until M14, so a deck that accepted a connection and then went silent produced no failure any caller could observe. Each is now bounded at `DECK_REPLY_TIMEOUT` (15 s), the `ssh` forward before them already was at `FORWARD_READY_TIMEOUT` (30 s), and a watcher whose *task* has ended no longer holds its deck's claim — so Reconnect starts a replacement instead of being silently refused.

The header instruments read `—` rather than `0` while **no deck is answering**. This is not decoration: a reconnect failure replaces the connection but *keeps* the previous snapshot's agents, and both the `disconnected` and `error` fixtures ship the default four, so deriving the counts unconditionally printed `AGENTS 4 · GROUPS 1` above a body correctly saying the fleet could not be read. "No agents" and "we cannot see the agents" are different statements and only one of them is true there. With one deck that test is exactly the old `connection.status === "connected"`; with N it is "at least one deck answered", because one reachable deck makes the instruments a true statement about a knowable part of the fleet — and `DECKS n/N` beside them is what says how much of it, per [the aggregate](#the-aggregate-is-honest-about-what-it-could-not-see).

The overview offers no deck lifecycle action — Start deck, Replace deck and Stop all stay on the deck screen, and the incompatible note says so. The one exception is **Connect anyway**, which is not a lifecycle action: it changes this app's own classification of a handshake it already performed, so it belongs on the screen that is refusing to show you the fleet. It appears on the overview's incompatible note under exactly the conditions described in [Attaching across a contract difference](#attaching-across-a-contract-difference-development-only) — live mode, and a refusal the desktop crate reports as overridable.

## The agent pane overlay

PRD #1105 gives the app a third rendering of an agent while deliberately refusing it a third *component*. A maximise control on a deck tile, and another on an overview row, open **that agent's pane at window size over the screen it was opened from**; `Esc`, or the close control in the pane's own header, returns you there. It lives in `desktop/src/App.tsx` — `AgentPaneFrame` positions it and `OverviewAgentPane` supplies the overview's half — and works in both fixture and live mode, so `?fixture=1&state=connected` reaches it with no daemon.

**Back is a field on the view value, not a history stack.** `DeckView` (`desktop/src/types.ts`) grew a third variant, `{ kind: "agent"; deckId; agentId; from: "deck" | "overview" }`, and `from` is the only record of where closing goes — `closeAgent` is `setView({ kind: current.from })` and consults nothing else. The first two variants replace the mounted screen and `DeckShell`'s own doc comment says so; this one does not, and that is the structural constraint the rest of the feature hangs off. An overlay that unmounted the screen beneath it would re-declare that screen's shown terminal set, which is expensive in both directions — see [the attach model below](#the-attach-model-with-the-pane-open). So `from` names the base screen to keep mounted underneath, and `DeckShell` renders the base screen *with* the pane on top rather than instead of it.

Carrying the origin in the value rather than popping it off a stack also makes an agent view that was never navigated *to* — the app's `initialView`, a future deep link — close to a real screen instead of to nothing. This is the mechanism #745's own open question 2 asked for (two paths to an agent view, different back behaviour each); when its group view arrives it adds a third `from` and nothing else here changes.

**`deckId` travels with it because an agent id does not identify an agent.** Agent ids are per-daemon monotonic integers, the overview merges every observed deck, and two decks both mint `"1"`. `OverviewAgentPane` therefore resolves its agent *and* its evidence out of the fleet entry `view.deckId` names, rather than out of the selected deck's snapshot where a bare-id lookup finds a different agent wearing the same id.

**And that had to reach further than the lookup**, which is [the security audit's finding](#the-composite-identity-all-the-way-down) below. The runtime's three remaining per-agent maps — the terminal feed's retained buffers, the recorded send verdicts and the applied geometry — were keyed by bare id and were deliberately left that way, on the reasoning that nothing could put a second deck's agent in front of them. That reasoning was wrong for a reason older than this feature: the ordinary `DeckSelector` moves the selection, and the next deck's same-id agent then read the previous deck's state. They are now keyed by `agentKey(deckId, agentId)` (`desktop/src/lib/agentKey.ts`) end to end, and the four lifetime paths a second audit round found above that keying are closed too — [the same section](#the-four-findings-it-had-to-reach) enumerates them.

### One component at two presentations

The grid tile and the pane are the **same** `AgentTile`, rendered at `presentation="tile"` or `presentation="overlay"` (`AgentPanePresentation`, `desktop/src/types.ts`). Every difference between the two is derived from that one prop inside the component, and the prop is emitted as `data-presentation` on the tile's root element, which is the seam the stylesheet keys off.

The outcome being prevented is an `AgentTileLarge`. An agent pane in this app is not a small widget — five panel tabs, evidence, rename, status, an embedded `TerminalViewport`, the Reader launcher — and `OutputReader` is already a second rendering of the same agent's output. A third would drift from the other two one bug fix at a time. The rule that keeps that from happening: **a difference that cannot be expressed as a prop means the difference is wrong**, not that a second component is needed. `OutputReader` is the counter-example that earns its separateness by being a different *projection* — resolved text, with no cursor, no colour, no alternate screen and no keyboard. "Bigger" is not a different projection.

**A test enforces it rather than a convention.** `renders the same AgentTile component from the deck and overview origins` (`desktop/src/AgentPaneOverlay.test.tsx`) pulls the real component through `vi.importActual`, wraps it in a mount-effect tracker, then starts directly in a pane from each origin and asserts the tracked component mounted for that agent both times. A parallel large-pane component that copied the markup would satisfy every DOM query in the file and never reach the tracker, so a fork fails the suite instead of merely being frowned upon. The tracker counts mounts rather than render-body calls, so ordinary rerenders cannot satisfy it either. Read the guarantee at its actual width: it covers the two paths the test drives, a deck-origin and an overview-origin pane. A fork introduced on some third path the test does not open is not caught by this, and the enumeration that keeps that honest is that `AgentPaneFrame` is the only thing in the tree that renders `AgentTile` at either presentation — `grep -rnE '<AgentTile\b' desktop/src` is one hit. Use the word boundary — without it the `Omit<AgentTileProps, …>` on the wrapper's own signature comes back as a second, which is a type and not a render.

What the prop covers, recorded on the prop's own doc comment in `AgentTile.tsx` as well as here:

- **All five panel tabs at both presentations.** The tab set is a property of the agent, not of the box. `tab` is controlled state owned by `App` and keyed by agent id, so the pane inherits whatever tab the tile was on and hands it back on close — which *is* the "panel state intact" commitment. A presentation that dropped tabs would have to coerce that shared state behind the user's back. What the presentation does change is the strip's *labels*: they are shown today by a `min-width: 1450px` media query, which asks how wide the **window** is when the question is how wide this pane is.
- **The same header, with the clamps relaxed.** Nothing is removed. Every header element identifies the agent, and identity matters *more* in the pane, where the grid position that used to tell you which tile you were looking at is gone. `.agent-assignment`'s two-line clamp exists for a half-width tile and is relaxed at overlay presentation.
- **The terminal's box, in CSS only.** `TerminalViewport` measures its host element with `FitAddon` under a `ResizeObserver`, so a bigger box reports a bigger geometry through the path that already exists — "enlarging really enlarges" needed no new mechanism and no measurement code. Overlay presentation drops `.agent-panel`'s fixed `height: 42vh` band for `flex: 1 1 auto`.
- **No Reader inside the pane.** `OutputReader` binds `Escape` on `window` and so does the pane. Two listeners on the same target both fire for one event — `stopPropagation` does nothing between them — so one `Escape` inside a Reader opened in the pane would dismiss both and land the user back on the deck. Hiding the launcher is not sufficient on its own: `readerOpen` is `AgentTile`-local state reset only when `agent.id` changes, so a user who has the Reader open on a tile and then opens that agent's pane carries it across. The **render** of `<OutputReader>` is gated on the presentation too.

  **That gate is necessary and was not sufficient, which the PR review caught.** It governs the tile being *promoted* and says nothing about the other tiles the pane is drawn over: those stay at `presentation="tile"` and keep any Reader they already had, mounted behind the scrim with its own `window` listener. Open the Reader on tile A, open the pane on tile B, press `Escape` once, and both closed. So there is a second gate — `panePresent`, a fact about the *screen* rather than about this box — and the two are deliberately separate props for that reason. It **dismisses** rather than hides: a Reader restored when the pane closed would answer the next `Escape` instead of the deck, which is the same surprise one key later. `dismisses a Reader open on another tile when a pane opens over it` is the guard.

Two things are deliberately **not** derived from `presentation`, and the line between them and the list above is worth keeping. `onOpen` and `onClose` are *capabilities* supplied by a parent that owns the view — closing acts on the view and `AgentTile` holds no view state — following the pattern `onRename` already used. And the prop cannot express that the tile and the pane share one xterm instance, which is the next section.

### One xterm per agent, and why the wrapper is always rendered

The required property is **exactly one live `TerminalViewport` per agent at any moment**. Two viewports for one agent means two WebGL contexts and two `ResizeObserver`s both writing a single per-agent coalesced resize entry, so the one viewer geometry the daemon holds would ping-pong between tile size and pane size every frame — an intermittent reflow rather than anything that reads as a wiring mistake. There is a second, independent reason in this codebase: `terminalRegistry` is a module-level `Map` keyed by bare agent id, so a second live viewport overwrites the first's registration and the first viewport's unmount then cleans up nothing.

That property is not something a prop can deliver, because it is a property of *where the element sits in the React tree*. React reconciles children by position, key and type, so a component rendered at a different place in the tree is an unmount and a remount — which tears down and rebuilds the xterm instance. So the tile's element is **promoted in place**: `AgentPaneFrame` wraps every tile in the grid, open or not, and flipping `open` changes only that wrapper's attributes and the tile's `presentation`. React keeps the same `AgentTile` fiber, the same `TerminalViewport` beneath it and therefore the same instance with its scrollback, selection and cursor. Closed, the wrapper is `display: contents`, so `.agent-tile` remains the grid item it has always been and the deck's layout is unchanged.

**Rendering the wrapper only when open would have been the bug.** That swaps a `div` in where an `AgentTile` was, which React treats as an unmount — a rebuilt xterm, a second WebGL context and a client-side transcript re-write on every open and every close. `promotes the deck tile in place, so the agent keeps one terminal across open and close` is what pins it, and it asserts against a mount effect rather than against the DOM: a tile that unmounted its viewport and mounted a fresh one in an overlay renders an identical-looking element and reads identically to every DOM query. The test asserts one build and zero disposes across the round trip, and that `getByTestId("terminal-planner")` is the same DOM node at all three points.

**The overview path is asymmetric on purpose.** The overview mounts no terminal — #745's commitment is untouched — so there is no tile there to promote, and the pane is a sibling of the screen rather than a promotion inside it. The one-viewport property still holds, because the screen underneath contributes none. An overview pane for an agent on a deck this app cannot reach mounts none either, by design rather than as a saving ([why](#an-agent-on-any-reachable-deck-opens-with-a-live-terminal)), so the required property is satisfied there with zero rather than one.

**The pane must override the tile's own responsive rules, and one of them is a trap.** `@media (max-width: 680px)` sets `.agent-panel, .agent-tabs { display: none }` — a reasonable thing to do to a half-width tile on a phone and the wrong thing entirely to do to a pane that *is* the window. A pane that inherited it would open full-screen with a header, an assignment, a footer and nothing where the terminal belongs — not the explicit no-terminal state [below](#an-agent-on-any-reachable-deck-opens-with-a-live-terminal), which is a different thing and says so: just the whole feature missing, with no tab strip either, rather than a layout blemish. jsdom evaluates no media query, so `desktop/e2e/agent-pane-narrow.spec.ts` asserts it at 400x780 in both browser engines instead. It drives the **overview** origin because that is the one reachable at that width — the deck's own `.agent-tile:not(.is-selected) { display: none }` hides every tile until one is selected, so there is no Open control on screen to press.

One specificity correction is written into the stylesheet at the rule it applies to, because it is easy to get wrong in the other direction: an attribute selector is `(0,1,0)`, exactly like a class, so `[data-presentation="overlay"] .agent-panel` is `(0,2,0)` and beats `.agent-panel` inside any media query — media queries add no specificity — but a bare `[data-presentation="overlay"]` does **not** beat `.agent-tile:not(.is-selected)`, since `:not()` carries its argument's specificity. The overlay's own root rule is therefore written `.agent-tile[data-presentation="overlay"]`.

### The attach model with the pane open

This builds directly on [the overview's attach model](#the-attach-model) above; `setShownTerminals` and `MAX_WARM_TERMINALS` are that section's.

**Over the deck, the pane changes no shown set and therefore costs nothing.** The deck's shown ids are derived from the tiles whose tab is `"terminal"`, and opening a pane changes neither `snapshot.agents` nor `tabs` — so the dependency key is unchanged and the open/close transition makes **no `setShownTerminals` call at all**. No attach, no detach, no eviction, no scrollback replay. (Read that as a claim about the transition, not about the interval: a snapshot that adds or retires an agent while the pane is up moves the key and declares, exactly as it would with no pane open. What the pane itself costs is nothing.) The steady-state cost of a pane over the deck is exactly today's deck cost, and the feature adds no sockets on this path. That is asserted rather than arranged: `does not redeclare the unchanged deck shown set while the pane opens or closes` records the initial four-agent declaration, clears it, and counts zero calls through an open and a close.

**Read that paragraph as the deck path's alone.** From the overview the pane costs **one** attach, whichever deck the agent is on, which is the minimum the feature can cost there: the overview's shown set is empty, so opening an agent goes from zero attached to one — exactly #745's "whatever is showing output is what attaches". The declaration names the agent's own deck, so the one attach goes to that deck's link and not to the selected one. Closing declares `[]` again, which flushes the warm set to zero, so the pane's terminal is detached on the way out. Where the agent's deck has **no live link** — disconnected, still reporting nothing, no address — it costs **none**: the declaration stays `[]` and the pane renders its no-terminal state instead ([below](#an-agent-on-any-reachable-deck-opens-with-a-live-terminal)). The no-flicker commitment belongs to the deck path and to nothing else.

**The arithmetic that makes unmounting the deck the wrong instinct**, since "the grid is hidden anyway, unmount it to save resources" is the intuitive optimisation and it is backwards. Unmounting `DeckSurface` invokes no cleanup declaration of its own — `useShownTerminals` has no cleanup — so unmount by itself leaves the bridge holding its previous shown set, stale; what costs something is whatever declares next, and there are two ways to get it wrong:

- **Declare `[agentId]` directly from nine shown ids.** The other eight move to the warm set, `8 - MAX_WARM_TERMINALS` = **five** are evicted immediately, and one shown plus three warm survive. Closing then re-attaches those five, and an attach is not cheap: the daemon replies OK and immediately follows with a single frame carrying the consistent scrollback snapshot before it starts streaming, over a socket opened per session. Five scrollback replays is precisely the flicker this design exists to avoid.
- **Declare `[]`,** which is what a base screen that declares nothing for itself produces. All nine move to warm and the overflow is `warm.size` rather than `warm.size - MAX_WARM_TERMINALS`, so it detaches **nine**, not five. A one-id pane after that costs one replay to open and eight more to restore the deck.

Both ways of unmounting the grid are expensive, which is what makes the keep-it-mounted decision stronger rather than weaker. There is no cheap way to do it.

**The shown set has one owner per mounted screen tree, and that is what M4 bought.** `setShownTerminals` must be called once per render commit with the whole set; before the pane existed that rule was kept by two independent effects agreeing not to be mounted at the same time — the deck's, and the overview's `[]` on mount. The pane broke the agreement, because it sits *over* a base screen rather than instead of one: the overview's `[]` and the pane's `[agentId]` are two declarations in one commit. Nothing about that fails loudly — depending on effect order the result is either a pane with a dead terminal or an overview that has quietly attached one PTY while its own doc comment says it attaches none.

So the declaration moved into `useShownTerminals` (`desktop/src/hooks/useShownTerminals.ts`), and there is one owner per tree: `DeckSurface` owns the deck's set, `DeckShell` owns the overview's. `AgentOverview` declares nothing for itself any more, and a comment stands where its effect was saying nothing there may declare a shown set again. The two trees are mutually exclusive, so exactly one owner is ever mounted.

**`undefined` is not the empty set, and collapsing them would be expensive.** A component that is mounted but is not the owner in its current state passes `undefined`, which makes **no call at all** — including on the commit that hands ownership over, where firing would be a second declaration racing the new owner's first. `[]` is a real declaration and a very expensive one, since it flushes the warm set to zero. The obvious `agentIds ?? []` would therefore detach every terminal on the very commit the new owner is about to declare them. The dependency key is `agentIds?.join("\n")`: `undefined` for "no declaration", `""` for "declare nothing shown".

#### The reachability condition, which is the one a future editor would remove

**An agent is declared shown only once its own deck is ANSWERING.** In `DeckShell` that reads as `paneDeck?.connection.status === "connected"`, where `paneDeck` is the fleet entry the pane's `deckId` names. It looks like defensive tidiness. It is a correctness condition.

**It used to ask whether the deck was SELECTED, and the change is the feature.** `terminal::attach` resolved its daemon through `trusted_daemon()` — whichever deck was linked at the instant the attach ran — and agent ids are per-daemon monotonic integers, so declaring `[agentId]` while another deck was in force did not attach early: it attached **that** deck's agent of the same id, on another machine, under the right name, with nothing on screen saying so. The fleet fixture has a `planner` on two decks, which is the ordinary case rather than a contrived one. The declaration now names the deck and the crate resolves that deck's link, so being selected has stopped being a precondition for having a terminal.

**What remains a precondition is being reachable**, and that is not a hypothetical branch. A deck can be disconnected, still waiting to report, configured with no address, or not observed at all — and there is no link to attach over in any of those. A pane there must say so rather than mount a viewport that will receive nothing. An agent on a deck this app is not observing falls here too, through `paneDeck` being `undefined`: a pane cannot promise an attach against a deck it cannot name.

**One expression, read twice, and that is deliberate.** `DeckShell`'s `paneDeckAttachable` decides both whether an attach may be declared and whether the pane shows a terminal or says why it has none, because those two must agree: a pane rendering a terminal that nothing attached to is a black rectangle the user reads as an agent gone quiet, and a pane explaining itself while an attach is live would be explaining a state it is not in. Two comparisons of the same values is how they would come apart. Note what is *not* folded in: `deckPaneRetargeted`, which closes a deck-origin pane, keeps its own comparison of the pane's deck against the selected one — the deck surface renders the selected deck and nothing else, so a deck-origin pane's claim stops being true of anything on screen. An overview-origin pane makes no such claim and is kept.

**It is necessary and it is not sufficient, and reading it as sufficient is the mistake the first security audit caught.** What it governs is the *attach* — when this app asks for a PTY. It says nothing about what a viewport reads out of the retained terminal state at mount, which happens before any attach at all; that is [the second blocker](#terminal-stream-state-is-invalidated-by-being-keyed-not-by-being-cleared) below, and it needed the state fixed rather than the declaration delayed.
