### An agent on any reachable deck opens with a live terminal

The overview offers every observed deck's agents. **Every one of those rows carries the same open control, and the pane it opens has a live terminal whichever deck the agent is on.** `OverviewRow` renders the control whenever it can read `OpenAgentContext` — which `AgentOverview` is the only provider of, and always provides — and asks no question about which deck the agent is on. The rule is the one a reader can hold: *if an agent is on the screen, there is a way into its pane, and that pane works.* The guard is `offers an open control on every row, whichever deck the agent is on` (`desktop/src/components/AgentOverview.test.tsx`), which also presses the control on a non-selected deck's row and checks the navigation carries **that agent's own deck** into the view.

**This is the product decision the feature exists for, and it was reached the long way round.** An intermediate commit on this branch gated the *control* on the deck, leaving a listed agent with no way to open it — a dead row, made worse by the fact that nothing on a row tells a reader which deck they are pointing at. A later one restored the control and opened a pane with no terminal in it. The owner's decision closed both:

> The point of the overview page is to be able to see all agents of the selected deck or from all decks. It's like a control room… **It's a control plane from which you can access any agent and interact with it.** That makes the desktop app "special" when compared to the TUI. […] Now, if that results in us not being able to open a full agent from the overview page when all decks are selected, I consider it a big failure to meet the idea.

So the pane carries the composite `(deckId, agentId)` through attach, output, input, resize and refit, and the crate resolves that deck's own link — [the attach model has a deck dimension](#the-attach-model-has-a-deck-dimension) has the mechanism. **The selected deck does not move**, and nothing writes `desktop.toml`.

#### The deck with no link, which is what the no-terminal state narrowed to

`overviewShown` declares a terminal only once the pane's deck is **answering** ([the reachability condition](#the-reachability-condition-which-is-the-one-a-future-editor-would-remove) above). Where it is not, a `TerminalViewport` mounted inside the pane would sit there receiving no bytes — a black rectangle reading *"this agent is producing no output"* about an agent that may be working normally on a machine this app has merely lost contact with. So the pane renders **no viewport at all** and an explicit state in its place:

- `.agent-terminal-stack` carries `data-terminal-state`, `"attached"` or `"unreachable-deck"`. It is the sibling of the viewport's own `data-input-state` and answers the outer question — is there a terminal here at all. `"attached"` is written positively rather than left absent so both directions are assertable from the DOM.
- Where it reads `unreachable-deck`, an element with `data-testid="terminal-absent-<agentId>"` and `role="status"` stands where the terminal would be, carrying one sentence: which deck the agent is on, named by `deckName` so it matches the overview group header and the label in the Deck selector, and that deck's **own** account of why it is not answering, repeated rather than guessed at.

The derivation is `unreachableDeckTerminalState` in `desktop/src/lib/terminalInput.ts`, beside #1042's input gate and deliberately **not** inside it: `terminalInputState` answers "will what is typed here be delivered" in the `SendResult` vocabulary, and that vocabulary is the wire's — it mirrors the Rust enum the daemon sends, so a desktop-only condition cannot be spelled in it without inventing a value no daemon will ever produce. The *shape* is copied rather than re-invented (one state attribute plus one `role="status"` sentence), because a second vocabulary for the same kind of fact is how two seams come to disagree. There is no input gate rendered beside it either: where this state is set no terminal is mounted, so there is nothing to type into and nothing a lease could say about it.

**The trigger was narrowed, not invented.** It read *"this agent is on `<deck>`, which is not the selected deck"* and fired for every non-selected deck, because attach was process-global. Being non-selected is no longer a reason to have no terminal, so that sentence would now be false about the ordinary case; what survives is the case it was always also covering. `desktop/src/AgentPaneNoTerminal.test.tsx` holds the pair plus its control: `replaces an unreachable deck's terminal with an explicit state naming that deck` drives a healthy cross-deck pane and then takes its deck away, `attaches the terminal and clears the notice when the pane's own deck answers again` runs it back the other way and asserts the terminal arrives on the **same DOM node** — no close, no reopen, no identity change — and `gives a non-selected but reachable deck's agent a live terminal instead` is the control that stops the other two passing against a production that reverted to the old trigger.

**A disconnected deck is entered through a pane that was already open, and that is not a shortcut.** The overview lists no agents for a deck that stopped answering, because such a deck cannot vouch for what it was running — so the only way a user meets this state is by having opened the pane while the deck was healthy, which is what those tests reproduce.

#### The deck switch stays withdrawn, and the history matters

PRD #1105 originally opened a non-selected deck's agent by writing the selected deck to `desktop.toml`, letting the crate apply it, and writing the selection back on close — `useCrossDeckSelection`, plus `selectionForDeck` in `lib/endpoints.ts` to map a deck back to a stored row by its `Endpoint::describe()` label. It worked on the happy path. Two security audits then found the same shape twice: **state created under deck A being attributed to deck B, because identity is read from the current selection at use time rather than captured at creation.**

Attaching on the pane's own deck is what made the switch unnecessary rather than merely unwise, and the audits' four findings had to be closed to get there — capturing an origin deck with each channel, attempt and session and checking it through installation, delivery and write authority is exactly what cross-deck attach needs anyway ([#1116](https://github.com/vfarcic/dot-agent-deck/issues/1116), and [the section below](#the-four-findings-it-had-to-reach)). The switch itself is not coming back: it moves the user's selection for them, which is a decision about the whole app taken on a navigation. The withdrawn implementation is in `git log` on this branch.

**Nothing about opening or closing a pane writes the settings document**, which is the withdrawal's user-visible half. Three things in this app call `settings.save` — the settings sheet, the `DeckSelector` and `useZoom` — and none of them is reachable from opening or closing a pane. This page previously said a cross-deck open rewrote `desktop.toml` through the crate's `toml::Table` → `toml::to_string_pretty` round trip, losing comments and hand formatting, and that a kill while such a pane was open left the selection moved. Both were true of the withdrawn behaviour and neither is reachable now. `writes no settings document when a pane opens or closes` (`desktop/src/AgentPaneDeckIdentity.test.tsx`) is the guard, so re-introducing a write there goes red rather than quietly restoring the side effect.

**And no multiplexing.** One overlay pane is one stream to a deck this app already holds a tunnel to, over the existing attach verbs at the existing `PROTOCOL_VERSION`. [#1073](https://github.com/vfarcic/dot-agent-deck/issues/1073)'s objection — that the five-byte frame header carries no stream id — is about N decks × M *visible tiles* on the deck screen, which this does not attempt.

### The composite identity, all the way down

A security audit of PRD #1105 found two blocker-class defects, both of the same shape: **a pane showing or writing another deck's agent under the identity the user believes they opened.** Neither was exotic. Agent ids are per-daemon monotonic, the fleet fixture has a `planner` on two decks, and both defects run through that ordinary case.

**The heading used to read "part of the way down", and the qualifier was earned.** A second audit round found four lifetime and ordering paths the storage-key fix below does not reach, all of the *same* shape one layer up: identity read from mutable current selection at use time rather than captured at creation. [The four findings it had to reach](#the-four-findings-it-had-to-reach) says what each one was and what closed it. They were closed because cross-deck attach could not be built over them, not as a tidying pass.

**The pane is a real modal now, because it was claiming to be one.** `AgentPaneFrame` renders `role="dialog"` with `aria-modal="true"` — a claim that the rest of the interface is unavailable — over a base screen that is deliberately still mounted, which is the whole no-flicker design. So every control behind it kept its place in the tab order: the rail, the other tiles, and the `DeckSelector`, which retargets the entire app. Reaching the selector under an open deck-origin pane and choosing a deck that runs a same-id agent promoted **that** agent into the open pane, attached its session, and resolved `sendTerminalInput(agentId, …)` to it. Same role, same display text, no deck identity anywhere in the dialog.

`useInertBackground` (`desktop/src/hooks/useInertBackground.ts`) is the mechanism. It walks from the pane's own element up to `document.body` and marks every element that is **not** an ancestor of it `inert`, then moves focus inside. Marking one subtree was not available: `inert` is inherited and a descendant cannot escape it, and on the deck path the pane *is* a tile inside the base screen, promoted in place so its xterm survives. Walking siblings is also what stops it rotting — a region added to the deck later is covered with no edit to that hook, where a list of a dozen `inert` props in the JSX would silently omit it. The effect deliberately has no dependency array, so background content that appears while the pane is open (a toast, a connection banner, a newly reported agent) is marked on the commit that adds it. It has exactly one exemption — the voice surface, which is a peer rather than a region; [the voice surface](#the-voice-surface-and-the-four-decisions-in-it-worth-knowing-before-you-edit-it) says why and what the fence still holds.

#### A deck-origin pane is fenced on identity, and closes rather than retargets

Inertness makes the attack unreachable through the UI; it does not make the pane's claim true. A deck-origin pane means *"this agent, on the deck you are looking at"*, and the selection can still move for reasons that are nobody's gesture — a `selectionFallback` the crate reports, another window writing the settings document. So `DeckShell` compares `agentView.deckId` against the selected deck and **closes** the pane when they part. Closing was chosen over suspending because it leaves nothing to be wrong about: a suspended pane still has to say whose agent it is showing, and the honest answer is nobody's.

The render does not wait for that effect, and that is the half worth reading twice. Effects run after the commit is painted, so a fence that was only an effect would still have one frame in which the arriving deck's namesake wears the pane. `DeckSurface` therefore promotes a tile only on the full `(deckId, agentId)` match — `openAgent` replaced a bare `openAgentId` prop — so the mismatched commit shows no pane at all and the effect closes the view behind it. `promotes no tile for an open-pane identity naming another deck` drives `DeckSurface` directly for exactly this reason; the `DeckShell`-level test can only observe the state after effects have run, and it passes with the bare-id match restored.

#### Terminal stream state is invalidated by being keyed, not by being cleared

The second blocker is the ordinary overview path and needs no unreachable gesture at all. `terminalFeed`'s buffers were keyed by bare agent id, and **leaving a deck detaches its sessions without clearing a single buffer**. So opening deck B's `planner` mounted a fresh `TerminalViewport` whose first act is `terminalFeed.get(agentId)` — which answered with deck A's retained bytes, and the `!previous` branch wrote them straight into the new xterm. Deck B's live `AgentSession.transcript` is empty, so what the user saw under B's correctly resolved heading was up to the feed's 1 MiB retention of another machine's output. On a successful switch B's own `replace` frame eventually resets it; where the switch is refused, the save fails or the attach fails, no replacement ever arrives.

**Delaying the shown-set declaration does not touch this**, which is the reason [the reachability condition](#the-reachability-condition-which-is-the-one-a-future-editor-would-remove) above — correct and kept — is not the fix. The viewport's backlog read happens at mount, before anything is attached at all.

So the three maps are keyed by the composite identity end to end. `TerminalFeed` takes `(deckId, agentId)`; `AgentTile` supplies `agent.daemonId`, which is the agent's **own** deck rather than the selected one; and `TerminalChunk` carries a `deckId` stamped by the bridge at `deliverTerminal`, the single funnel every chunk passes through. The stamp is applied at the producer because only the producer can name the deck the bytes came from — `selectedDeckId` there is read off `fleet[0]` on every snapshot arrival, while React's copy is a commit behind.

`terminalInputResults` and `appliedGeometry` were the same root cause and are keyed the same way. A verdict recorded for deck A's `planner` could disable deck B's pane and print A's rejection notice under B's heading; `appliedGeometry` is removed by no per-agent eviction, so B's pane applied A's grid, and an attach that beat the pane's first fit submitted A's cached dimensions to B — which the daemon's smallest-viewer policy turns into a real, transient reflow of B's PTY and of every other client watching it, from a viewport on another machine.

The bridge's own `appliedGeometry` cache — read at attach time to declare this client's viewport — is keyed by the composite identity too, and each entry carries the target its session was created for. **`adoptGeometryDeck` is gone with that change**, and its removal is the fix rather than a loss: it emptied the whole cache when the selection moved, and emptying is not an identity boundary. A deck-A geometry event arriving *after* the clear rewrote the bare-id cache and notified listeners with the deck selected now, so both caches recorded A's dimensions as B's. An entry keyed and stamped with its own session's deck cannot be read for any other deck at all, so there is nothing left for a clear to protect against. `adoptEvidenceDeck` stays: the evidence ring genuinely is the selected deck's, and PRD #742 DECISION 1 keeps it single-deck.

**This reverses a decision this PRD took, and the reversal is the point.** PRD #745 left nine maps keyed by bare agent id and #1105 explicitly declined to fix these three, on the reasoning that a tile's terminal is always the selected deck's and the overview mounts none — so nothing could put a second deck's agent in front of them. That was true, and a cross-deck pane is precisely a second deck's agent in front of them. **The keying is kept even though cross-deck opening was descoped**, because the defect it closes is not the pane's: the sentinel scenario below needs no overlay at all.

#### The four findings it had to reach

**What the storage keying alone does.** A **fresh mount** — a viewport built for `(deckId, agentId)` — reads the buffer, the verdict and the applied geometry belonging to the deck it names, rather than whatever the bare id last held. The reproduction that needs no overlay: with two decks running a `planner` each, stream a sentinel into deck A's, switch decks with the ordinary `DeckSelector`, and deck B's same-id `planner` used to come up carrying A's scrollback under B's correctly resolved heading. The test reproduces the disclosure rather than the fix's shape (`does not replay the previous deck's buffer under the next deck's namesake`, `desktop/src/CrossDeckTerminalState.test.tsx`).

**What it did not, and what closed each one.** A second audit round found four paths above that keying, all carried forward with `file:line` evidence in [#1116](https://github.com/vfarcic/dot-agent-deck/issues/1116). Cross-deck attach could not be built over any of them, so all four are closed here rather than deferred:

| finding | what closed it |
|---|---|
| **The terminal stamp identified the deck selected when `deliverTerminal` ran**, not the deck that created the channel. `foldSnapshot` moves `selectedDeckId` before React receives the fleet while existing channel callbacks stay valid, so a late frame from deck A was stamped B and stored under B's composite key — a confidently wrong producer defeating a correct storage key. | `deliverTerminal` takes the origin `AgentTarget` as a parameter and stamps from it. Every producer holds its session's target in scope from before the attach was invoked, captured once in the `attachAgents` closure, so no path has to guess. |
| **A pending attach from the previous deck installed into the bridge's bare-id `sessions` map and took `sendTerminalInput`.** | It installs under its **own** deck's composite key, which is issue #1116's own suggested shape and is also simply correct: the pane that asked for the stream is still showing it. There is nothing to discard and nothing to reattribute, and `sendTerminalInput({ deckId, agentId }, …)` resolves the deck it was asked for. |
| **`TerminalViewport` reused one xterm and its rendered scrollback across a deck change**, because its build effect depended only on `agentId`; the `deckId` prop changed the feed subscription and nothing reset the bytes already on screen. | `deckId` is in that effect's dependency list, so an identity change rebuilds the terminal. The module-level registry is keyed by `(deckId, agentId)` for the same reason — two decks' same-id xterms are now routinely live at once, which is exactly the premise the first audit cleared the bare-id registry on. |
| **A late geometry event from the previous deck repopulated the cache `adoptGeometryDeck` cleared**, under the new deck's identity, and a later attach submitted it. | The geometry listener resolves its session by **session id** — minted per attach by the desktop crate, so it names one stream on one deck — and notifies with that session's creating deck. `adoptGeometryDeck` is gone; see above for why clearing was never an identity boundary. |

**Every one of those is mutation-checked**, because a test written alongside a fix that passes either way is what this branch has already been caught by three times. Breaking each fix alone — stamping from `selectedDeckId`, resolving the write session by bare agent id, making the registry bare-id again, resolving `resize`'s daemon from the applied selection, putting the registry's eviction back on the bare agent id, restoring `if moved { detach_all }` — turns a named test red in each case.

**The value-not-identity rule is part of the fix, not a style note.** A target is re-allocated on every render (`{ deckId: agent.daemonId, agentId: agent.id }` is a fresh object each time), so anything keying a `Map` on the object passes a test that happens to reuse one reference and finds nothing in production. `agentKey` is the one way to turn a target into a key, and `routes input and geometry to the requested deck when two attached agents share an id` (`desktop/src/lib/bridge.test.ts`) deliberately hands in a newly allocated but value-equal target to prove it.

### What the two entry points do not share

The overview-origin pane is given no `onRename` and no `onEvidenceSelect`, so it renders no rename control and selecting a handoff's evidence does nothing. Both are **capabilities the screen genuinely lacks** rather than presentation differences, which is why neither is expressed through `presentation`: the evidence drawer is the deck's and no screen is mounted underneath that could open it, and a rename reports its outcome through the deck's **toast**, so wiring it there would fail silently — which is worse than a header without the pencil. It is stated at the call site in `App.tsx` as well as here so it is not rediscovered as a bug. Closing the rename half needs `DeckShell` to grow a notice surface of its own, which is a new user-visible surface and therefore `CLAUDE.md` rule 9's question rather than a loose end.

One more behaviour is an open question rather than a decision: an agent that leaves the snapshot while its **overview** pane is open renders nothing, leaving the base screen up with `Escape` and no visible close control, because `OverviewAgentPane` returns `null` when the lookup fails. From the deck the same event unmounts the tile and the frame goes with it. A second, smaller edge rides on the same case: the overview's shown set still declares that agent's target — `overviewShown` asks whether the pane's deck is answering, not whether the fleet still lists that agent — so there is one declaration with no viewport consuming it, reconciled by a later `End`. PRD #1105 records both under the same open question.

### What the automated tiers prove about the resize, and what they do not

The pane asks the daemon for a bigger PTY rather than scaling a small one up, which is a product decision with a consequence for anyone else watching the same agent — `prds/1105-desktop-agent-pane-overlay.md` has the decision and the policy it rests on. A PTY has one window size, and since PRD #1105 M11 the daemon decides it by **focus**. The last client to claim focus decides the size of every agent on which it has a viewer that has **reported** a geometry: the smallest each axis over that client's own reporting viewers, which is exactly the one viewer's geometry in the ordinary case. Where no client has claimed focus, or the focused client has no reporting viewer of the agent, the daemon falls back to PRD #882's rule, the smallest each axis over every registered viewer that has reported one. This app claims focus when its window gains focus, and when it attaches a terminal while its window is focused, on each deck where it holds a viewer (`terminal.rs`'s claim path). A TUI claims on a terminal focus-in and, throttled to once a second, on keyboard or mouse input. So with this window focused, opening the pane gives the agent the pane's size and closing gives it back the tile's, and a TUI user attached to the same agent sees their pane reflow twice per open/close cycle.

**Claims are bounded on the daemon and dropped on this side when they go out of date** (PRD #1105's security audit). The daemon applies focus claims at most once per `FOCUS_REAPPLY_INTERVAL` (250 ms): claims arriving inside one window are applied together when it ends and only the newest of them is recorded, so two clients alternating claims cannot cost one PTY resize — a SIGWINCH, a cleared scrollback ring and a geometry push to every viewer — per claim. A claim is answered once it or a newer one has been applied, so `ok` still means the size it decides is in force; a claim landing inside a window is answered up to an interval late. On this side, every window focus report drops the claims of the report before it that have not been written yet, and so does a pane's attach-time claim: a claim still waiting on a slow deck's link when the window loses focus is never sent, rather than landing after the person moved to a TUI, and taking the size back to a window they are no longer looking at. A claim whose request was already written cannot be recalled.

**Under focus, the other client is not protected, and that is the accepted cost rather than an oversight.** The fallback's minimum is what keeps a viewer from being handed a grid larger than the geometry it last reported. The focused branch gives that up for every viewer that does not belong to the focused client: while this window holds focus, a TUI whose pane is narrower or shorter than the desktop's is handed the desktop's grid and clips at its right and bottom edges, and the same happens to the desktop pane when a larger TUI takes focus. The client being clipped is the one nobody is looking at. A TUI from a build before M11 never claims focus, so it pays this for as long as this app holds focus on an agent they share (`changelog.d/1105.breaking.md`).

**Where the fallback applies, its protection carries a precondition, and it is a precondition rather than a given.** Because the fallback takes a minimum, raising the desktop's request can only raise the PTY up to the smallest of the other *reporting* viewers — so a viewer is not handed a grid larger than the geometry it last **reported**, which is the same as "larger than itself" exactly while that report is current. A viewer that has reported nothing is not protected at all, and that is not only the legacy case: this app's own tiles attach through `attach_pending_viewport` and contribute no constraint until their first measured fit, which is a frame or two on every attach for an agent the bridge has not yet measured. The same unmeasured window is why a focused client's freshly attached viewer does not decide the size until its first fit: until then the daemon falls back rather than pinning a guess. Neither class is created by the pane. What the pane does is exercise the first-attach window again on the overview path, where it is a *first* attach, and make the largest single jump in requested geometry the app has.

The automated coverage stops well short of that sentence, and the boundary is worth stating precisely because the tests are green either way.

`reports the pane's larger proposed grid and restores the applied grid` (`desktop/src/components/TerminalViewport.test.tsx`) drives the real `DeckShell` and the real `TerminalViewport` with fake xterm and WebGL dependencies and a controllable `ResizeObserver`, and observes two things: `runtime.resizeTerminal(agentId, cols, rows)` — the **client's proposed geometry handed to the runtime** — and the grid the fake xterm is left parsing at. It records the tile's proposal, opens the pane, asserts both reported dimensions grow, asserts the terminal itself is back at the daemon-**applied** 80×24 rather than the 160×48 just proposed, then closes the pane and asserts the tile's dimensions are reported again. Those two halves need separate observations, and this paragraph previously claimed the opposite: `fit()` reports the proposal *before* `applyAppliedGrid()` runs, so the reported numbers are identical whether the applied geometry is found, missing or ignored, and an earlier version of this test — which asserted on `resizeTerminal` alone and said here that inspecting `terminal.cols` would pin the wrong authority — stayed green when the production `appliedGeometry` handoff was severed at its `App.tsx` call site. Asserting that `cols` equals the *proposal* would pin the wrong authority; asserting it equals the applied grid is the `applyAppliedGrid` contract itself, and it is what makes the pane's deliberate small-then-grow round trip observable. Two negative controls were run: making the pane's fit equal the tile's turns the test red, and passing `appliedGeometry={undefined}` in `App.tsx` turns the restoration assertion red while every `resizeTerminal` assertion stays green.

What that leaves unproven, enumerated rather than summarised:

- **That a real PTY changed size.** The fixture runtime's resize method is a no-op. Nothing in the jsdom tier reaches IPC, let alone a daemon.
- **That a native window measured the same cells.** jsdom has no layout engine, and the browser tier runs Playwright's own Chromium and WebKit builds rather than the WebKitGTK your distribution packages or macOS's WKWebView.
- **That the daemon's focus-driven arbitration produced the size the client asked for.** The tests see the proposal and a fixture's stand-in applied geometry, never a real daemon's disposition. The daemon's rule is tested below this app — `resize/policy/001`–`011` in `tests/e2e_pty_size_policy.rs` read `stty size` inside a real agent PTY — but with client-library stand-ins for the desktop, so they say nothing about this app's own claims or proposals.
- **That a TUI client attached to the same agent visibly reflowed.** Nothing automated puts this app and a TUI on one agent.

All four are the manual smoke check's, below. There is no `tauri-driver` and no WebDriver session anywhere in this repository ([#953](https://github.com/vfarcic/dot-agent-deck/issues/953)), so the real Tauri window is driven by the walk and by nothing else — and manual checks decay.

The browser tier does answer the geometry questions it can: `desktop/e2e/agent-pane-overlay.spec.ts` measures that the pane's box really occupies the viewport in both engines, that the grid beneath is the **same DOM node** and still `isConnected` while the pane is up, that the overview beneath is likewise, and that `Escape` returns to the screen the pane was opened from. Those are engine-side measurements about this app's layout; they say nothing about a PTY.

## Release bundles

Every tagged release publishes the GUI as an **unsigned alpha** bundle, built by `release.yml`'s `desktop-bundle` job: a `.dmg` for macOS arm64 and a `.deb` for Linux x86_64. They arrive on the release as `dot-agent-deck-desktop-alpha-<platform>.<ext>` with their own `checksums-desktop-alpha.txt`, and the release notes carry a fixed section saying they are unsigned and how to get past the OS. There is no user-facing install page on purpose while the GUI is alpha; [#765](https://github.com/vfarcic/dot-agent-deck/issues/765) writes one when that changes.

**That note is the only installation instruction this project publishes, so two things about its wording are guarded rather than left to taste.** Its macOS half must offer the **System Settings > Privacy & Security > Open Anyway** route alongside the `xattr -dr com.apple.quarantine` command — macOS Sequoia removed the Control-click bypass, so a note carrying only the terminal command leaves a user unwilling to run a recursive command against `/Applications` with nothing, and teaches everyone else a habit that transfers to the next thing that asks for it. And its Linux half must speak for itself: it used to be covered by "unsigned, so your OS will warn you", which is false for the `.deb`, because `dpkg` ships with `no-debsig` and `dpkg -i` verifies no package signature and issues no warning. `the_alpha_note_does_not_leave_a_user_with_only_a_terminal_command` in `release_workflow_wiring.rs` asserts both. Signing and notarization — the thing that would remove the macOS dialog entirely — are [PRD #757](../../prds/757-desktop-artifact-trust.md), which also records why there is no free path to it on macOS and why the free path on Windows is better than any paid one.

**Every release asset carries build provenance, and it needs no key.** A separate `attest` job runs `actions/attest-build-provenance` over the `.dmg`, the `.deb` and the four CLI binaries, so a user can run `gh attestation verify <file> --repo vfarcic/dot-agent-deck` and learn that the exact bytes they hold came out of this repository's release workflow at a named commit — a stronger claim than `checksums.txt`, which only says a file matches a number printed on the same page that served it. The signing certificate is short-lived and comes from Sigstore through the job's OIDC token (`id-token: write`), so **there is no private key here to rotate or leak**; that property is what `the_attest_job_holds_no_credential_and_needs_none` pins, along with the job's exact permission set. It is the only trust mechanism that reaches the `.deb` at all, since `dpkg -i` verifies no package signature. It does **not** remove the macOS Gatekeeper dialog or Windows SmartScreen — those check different chains — and no OS verifies it on a user's behalf. Like the desktop jobs, `attest` hangs off `finalize` without `finalize` waiting on it, so an attestation-service blip reddens the run and leaves the release complete; PRD #757 Decision 12 has the reasoning.

Three things about that job are worth knowing before editing it, because each is a single line that reads as housekeeping and is not. It hangs off `prepare` rather than `build`, so it runs beside the CLI matrix instead of extending the critical path. `finalize` — which creates the release and uploads the five CLI assets — deliberately does **not** list it in `needs:`, so a bundler that fails, hangs, or is skipped cannot delay or break an ordinary tag; the run goes red while the release stays green and complete. And `finalize`'s artifact download is constrained by `pattern: dot-agent-deck-*` because `merge-multiple: true` would otherwise flatten the desktop bundles into the same `dist/` directory, where the release glob would sweep them up and `task checksums`' `shasum -a 256 dot-agent-deck-*` would choke on the space in Tauri's `Agent Deck_<version>_amd64.deb`. All three are asserted by `xtask/linkage-check/src/release_workflow_wiring.rs`, since nothing else can check a workflow that only fires on a tag.

`tauri.conf.json` hardcodes `version: "0.1.0"` — the same deliberate placeholder as `Cargo.toml`. The job merges the real version into the bundle config at build time with `jq` and then fails the build if no output filename carries it, so a bundle labelled `0.1.0` cannot be published. `bundle.active` is likewise `false` in the base config and only the overlay flips it, which means an invocation that loses its `--config` produces no bundle *and still exits 0*; the job asserts the output exists rather than trusting the exit code.

**Windows is not built.** A bundle carries the daemon as a sidecar and no Windows daemon binary is published; a Windows GUI cannot attach to a remote Linux daemon instead, because the IPC transport is a named pipe on Windows and a Unix socket everywhere else. **PRD [#741](https://github.com/vfarcic/dot-agent-deck/issues/741) did not change that, and it is worth saying so rather than leaving the earlier expectation standing**: its DECISION 1 landed on `ssh -N -L` (option 1A), which forwards a remote Unix socket to a *local Unix socket* and therefore still has nothing to bind on Windows. The option that would have removed the local socket entirely — `ssh <host> dot-agent-deck daemon proxy-stdio`, where the ssh child's stdin/stdout is the transport — is [filed as the deferred follow-up](#why-ssh--l-and-what-was-rejected) for exactly this reason, and it is that follow-up plus [#754](https://github.com/vfarcic/dot-agent-deck/issues/754) that makes a Windows GUI reachable. `prepare-sidecar.sh` nonetheless handles the `.exe` suffix on `*-pc-windows-*` triples — in both the cargo output path and the staged name Tauri resolves as `{path}-{triple}{ext}` — so that groundwork is done whenever it arrives. It is covered by `xtask/linkage-check/src/sidecar_staging.rs`.

Bundling locally needs more than the compile set `ci.yml` installs: on Linux the `deb` bundler additionally wants `patchelf`, `fakeroot`, `file` and `desktop-file-utils`.

AppImage is deliberately not built. Its bundler downloads five third-party artifacts at build time, two of them shell scripts from a `master` ref, which is not a supply-chain property a release pipeline should have; it also failed on the first real attempt while the `.deb` from the same run verified clean.

One trap if you bundle locally in a `devbox shell`: the shell runs under nix's glibc, whose `ld.so.cache` does not exist, so `ldconfig -p` returns nothing and system libraries are invisible to the loader. Exporting `LD_LIBRARY_PATH=/usr/lib/x86_64-linux-gnu` fixes the Rust side but then breaks nix's `node` (`undefined symbol: uv_tcp_keepalive_ex`, from picking up the system libuv). Bundle without it; the Rust link resolves through pkg-config's `-L` flags and does not need it.

## Security and ownership model

The daemon remains the single source of truth for agent lifecycle, PTYs, hooks, and orchestration. The desktop opens no HTTP API or TCP listener. For a **local** deck the Rust core connects to the same per-user IPC endpoint as the TUI, verifies that endpoint's ownership and permissions, performs the existing `Hello` protocol and build handshake, and then bridges typed snapshots and bounded terminal chunks through Tauri IPC. For a **remote** deck it does the same over an app-managed `ssh -N -L` child, and what it trusts is different in kind rather than weaker in degree — ssh host-key and user authentication instead of a uid-and-mode check on an inode that would only ever prove the local `ssh` client is ours. [Trust is a property of the deck's kind](#trust-is-a-property-of-the-decks-kind) is the whole of that split, including what is deliberately *not* checked and why. PRD #742 lets the app hold several such connections at once, and changes nothing about the split: trust is established **per deck, per connection**, keyed by `EndpointIdentity`, so no deck in a fleet inherits another's — but the two kinds still clear different gates while looking identical on screen, which is [the fourth `as_local()` gate](#the-trust-check-is-the-fourth-as_local-gate-and-the-only-one-that-substitutes-rather-than-refuses). PTY output travels daemon → Tauri channel → xterm.js; focused terminal input and resize requests travel in the opposite direction.

The window uses a restrictive content-security policy and a minimal Tauri capability set. Bridge commands are scoped to the main webview, connection errors are sanitized before reaching it, terminal input and launch commands are bounded to 64 KiB, dimensions are constrained to `1..=4096`, and terminal sessions use opaque IDs and are detached when the bridge is disposed. A build mismatch is shown as incompatible rather than triggering an automatic daemon restart, and the only way past it is an explicit, confirmed, session-scoped **Connect anyway** that relaxes the stamp comparison alone.

Treat every live terminal as equivalent to its underlying agent CLI: it has the daemon user's filesystem and process permissions, and terminal input can authorize consequential work. The stop control has a confirmation step, but this preview is not a sandbox or an access-control boundary.

## Ownership: where every value comes from

This is PRD [#819](https://github.com/vfarcic/dot-agent-deck/issues/819)'s M1 ownership sweep. It exists so the boundary can be stated as a **checked list** rather than as "everything except settings" — the false-absolute shape `CLAUDE.md` rule 17 exists to stop, and one this PRD's own history has already had to correct twice.

The governing principle it checks against, from #819 and [#803](https://github.com/vfarcic/dot-agent-deck/issues/803): **a client gets everything from the daemon, wherever that daemon runs; the only thing it owns is its own settings plus genuinely presentational state.** Where the daemon does not expose something a client legitimately needs, the daemon is extended — "the client can compute it locally" is not an acceptable answer even when the client is on the same machine.

Every value below is one of three things. **Daemon-sourced**: it comes over the wire. **Client-owned by policy**: it describes this machine, this display or this installation, so the client is the only party that knows it. **Computed locally**: the client derives it — each of those is either fixed by #819 or carries an issue.

### The sweep is mechanical, and here are its sources

The done-condition is that the enumeration is *derived*, not asserted, because the rule-17 trap this sweep exists to close could otherwise recur inside the sweep. Three mechanical sources, each with the command that reproduces it:

1. **Every field of the frontend snapshot DTO** — `DesktopSnapshot` and the types it nests in [`desktop/src-tauri/src/dto.rs`](../../desktop/src-tauri/src/dto.rs), mirrored field-for-field (camelCased) by `DesktopSnapshotDto` in [`desktop/src/lib/bridge.ts`](../../desktop/src/lib/bridge.ts). `grep -n 'pub struct\|pub enum' desktop/src-tauri/src/dto.rs` enumerates the Rust side; `DesktopSnapshotDto` and `DesktopAgentDto` are the TS side. The two agree on every field name and optionality but one, noted in the table.
2. **Every `localStorage` key** — `grep -rn 'localStorage\|sessionStorage' desktop/src/ | grep -v '\.test\.'`. There are **five**: #819 M6 removed `projects.v1` and PRD [#803](https://github.com/vfarcic/dot-agent-deck/issues/803) added the fixture bridge's settings key. **Four** of the five go through `modeScopedKey` so a fixture visit cannot write a key live mode reads back; the fifth deliberately does not, for a reason given in its row below.
3. **Every settings key in an on-disk desktop store** — **there is one store, and it now holds four top-level keys.** PRD #803 landed `desktop/src-tauri/src/settings.rs`, whose document is `platform::paths::config_dir()/desktop.toml` (`DOT_AGENT_DECK_DESKTOP_CONFIG` overrides the path). `awk '/pub struct DesktopSettings /,/^}/' desktop/src-tauri/src/settings.rs` enumerates it: `version` (the document's own schema number), `appearance.mode` (`light` / `dark` / `system`), `endpoints` (PRD #741's decks — an `Option`, and the one key whose absence means *unspecified* rather than *empty*) and `zoom.level` (PRD #744's scale factor). **This bullet said "exactly two keys" until #741 wrote it, and the count had already been wrong once**: zoom arrived with #744 and did not update it, which is the same unremarked drift the `localStorage` count below records against itself. All four are **client-owned by policy** and correctly so — a schema number describes the file, an appearance override and a zoom level describe this display, and an endpoint describes reachability *from here*, which is the criterion in [The ownership boundary](#the-ownership-boundary) rather than an exception to it. Still no `tauri-plugin-store` and no window-state plugin: the window's geometry remains a static literal in `tauri.conf.json`, not a persisted preference. [Application settings](#application-settings) below is that store's contract, including the criterion for what may join these four.

### The snapshot DTO

| value | class | where it comes from |
| --- | --- | --- |
| `connection.status` | computed locally | the client's verdict on its own connection (`classify_handshake`). Its own state, legitimately. |
| `connection.socketPath` | client-owned by policy | how the deck *this* client dials is named. For a local deck that is its socket path, which is what this reported before the endpoint type existed; for a remote deck it is `user@host` with the port when it is not 22, derived by `RemoteEndpoint::describe` from validated fields. Scrubbed through `safe_display_text` — bidi format characters stripped, not only category `Cc` — because this is the line that tells a user which machine they are talking to. **A label and never a key.** The TS layer used it as the deck identity until [#742](https://github.com/vfarcic/dot-agent-deck/issues/742) M5, and that was the collision described in [`socketPath` is a label; `deckId` is the key](#socketpath-is-a-label-deckid-is-the-key); `daemonId` is derived from `deckId` now. |
| `connection.deckId` | client-owned by policy | what the deck **is**, for anything keying on it: `EndpointIdentity::wire_id()`, rendered `deck-<16 hex>`, minted from the endpoint this client dials — so the client is the only party that knows it, the same way `socketPath` is. Always emitted. `daemonId`, the agent composite key's first half, every `data-daemon-id`, the `desktop://daemon-event` stamp and `DesktopSnapshot.fleet`'s entries are all this one value. |
| `connection.deckKind` | client-owned by policy | `"local"` or `"remote"`, from the selection in force (PRD #741 M7). Always emitted, because the webview branches on it to disable **Stop deck** and **Replace deck** and an absent field must not read as `"local"`. |
| `connection.localOnlyReason` | computed locally | `Endpoint::require_local("Stop deck")`'s own refusal, present exactly when `deckKind` is `"remote"`. M2 made the operation unreachable by type and wrote the sentence at the same time, so this renders an error that already exists rather than inventing one. |
| `connection.selectionFallback` | computed locally | `SelectionFallback`'s own sentence: why the app is on the local deck when the stored selection named another. Reported rather than folded into "connected", because "that deck is gone" and "that deck has no socket path yet" are different things to tell a user, and a silent substitution is how someone ends up acting on the wrong machine's agents. |
| `connection.projectActionsReason` | **daemon-sourced**, in the sense that matters | why the project-aware surfaces are unavailable, derived from the capability set the daemon **advertised in its `Hello` reply** through `DaemonCapabilities::supports` (PRD #741 M8). The client composes the sentence; the daemon owns the fact. It is what replaces the build stamp as the thing a screen acts on — "can this deck do what I am about to ask" rather than "is this deck the same build as me". `None` means available, and is omitted from the wire. |
| `connection.error` | mixed | the daemon's own `Hello` rejection when there is one, otherwise the client's transport error. Sanitised locally by `safe_message` (control characters stripped, 2048 chars). |
| `connection.clientProtocolVersion` | client-owned by policy | this binary's `PROTOCOL_VERSION`. |
| `connection.serverProtocolVersion` | daemon-sourced | `AttachResponse::server_version`. |
| `connection.clientBuildVersion` | client-owned by policy | this binary's `DAD_BUILD_ID`. |
| `connection.daemonBuildVersion` | daemon-sourced | `AttachResponse::build_version`. |
| `connection.daemonVersion` | daemon-sourced | `AttachResponse::daemon_version`. |
| `connection.runningAgentCount` | daemon-sourced | `AttachResponse::running_agents.count`. |
| `connection.buildStampMismatchOnly` | computed locally | the client's compatibility verdict, comparing a daemon-sourced stamp against its own. Always emitted from Rust including as `false`; the TS type marks it optional so a test DTO need not restate the common case. This is the one field where the two declarations differ. |
| `agents[].id` | daemon-sourced | `AgentRecord.id`. |
| `agents[].paneId` | daemon-sourced | `AgentRecord.pane_id_env`. |
| `agents[].displayName` | daemon-sourced | `AgentRecord.display_name`. |
| `agents[].cwd` | daemon-sourced | `AgentRecord.cwd` — the daemon's own view, which is the point: it diverges from any client's `current_dir()` the moment an agent has `cd`'d. |
| `agents[].rows` / `agents[].cols` | daemon-sourced | `AgentRecord.rows` / `.cols`. |
| `agents[].agentType` | daemon-sourced | the live snapshot's type, else `AgentRecord.agent_type`. Falls back to the literal `"none"`, which is honest: it is the value that also catches a type this build has never heard of. |
| `agents[].cliName` | daemon-sourced | `AgentRecord.cli_name`, stamped at the `ListAgents` wire boundary by `attach_cli_names` from the daemon's own `agent_registry`, for the identity the reply reports. It **used to be computed locally** — looked up in the client's own compiled-in copy of that table — which was the last entry in the third class no other issue covered, and [#856](https://github.com/vfarcic/dot-agent-deck/issues/856) closed it. It could not diverge then, because the handshake required an exact `PROTOCOL_VERSION` match *and* matching build stamps, so both sides compiled one table — but that was a property of the gate, not of the design, and [#801](https://github.com/vfarcic/dot-agent-deck/issues/801) has since removed the stamp half of it. Absent means the daemon named no binary; the cell is then empty, and **nothing falls back to the local table**. |
| `agents[].status` | daemon-sourced | the live snapshot's `SessionStatus`. Falls back to `"running"` when the daemon reported no live session, which is a *reading* of daemon-held state rather than an invention — the record exists because the daemon holds the process — and not a placeholder the user could mistake for a report. |
| `agents[].activeTool` | daemon-sourced | the live snapshot's `active_tool`. |
| `agents[].toolCount` | daemon-sourced | the live snapshot's `tool_count`, `0` when there is no snapshot. |
| `agents[].lastUserPrompt` | daemon-sourced | the live snapshot's `last_user_prompt`. Absent, never blank. |
| `agents[].writeLease` | daemon-sourced | projected from the live target's `Writable` into read/write/none. Absent when the daemon declared no live target, which is not the same as declaring a non-writable one. |
| `agents[].lastActivityMs` | daemon-sourced | the live snapshot's `last_activity_ms`, copied through unclamped. The daemon owns the instant; the webview owns the wording and the clock-skew rule. |
| `agents[].spawnedAtMs` | daemon-sourced | `AgentRecord.spawned_at_ms`, same split. |
| `agents[].tab` | daemon-sourced | `TabMembership`, every field bound explicitly in `map_tab` rather than through a rest pattern, so the next field added there is a compile error at this seam. |
| ~~`projectCwd`~~ | **gone** | It was `desktop_project_cwd()` — `option_env!("CARGO_MANIFEST_DIR")`'s grandparent if it held a `.dot-agent-deck.toml`, else `std::env::current_dir()` — and it was the defect #819 exists to remove: a compile-time constant cannot name a runtime machine's project, and against a remote daemon neither leg was even on the right host. **#819 M6 deleted the function and the field**, with nothing replacing either. The project a launch runs in now comes from `desktop_list_projects` / `desktop_resolve_project`. |
| `scheduleRevision` | daemon-sourced | `AttachResponse::schedule_revision`, the daemon's monotonic count of changes to its registered schedule set, copied through and rendered by nothing. It exists because the daemon seeds its project list partly from every registered schedule's working directory, and no schedule data reaches this app at all — so a schedule registered while the app is open added a project the picker had no way to notice ([#887](https://github.com/vfarcic/dot-agent-deck/issues/887)). `projectsRevision` in `App.tsx` keys on it. Absent from a daemon that reports none, in which case the manual **Refresh** button is the remedy it always was. Comparable only against earlier values on the same connection: it counts from 0 on every daemon start. |
| `fleet` | client-owned by policy | which decks this installation is observing, by `connection.deckId`, in observed order with the selected deck first and never empty (PRD #742 M5). It is a property of the **applied settings document** — which decks the user configured and chose — so it is client-owned by the same criterion every endpoint field is, and every deck's snapshot carries the identical list. No daemon is told it exists, let alone which others do. See [Fleet membership rides on every snapshot](#fleet-membership-rides-on-every-snapshot). |
| `protocolVersion` | client-owned by policy | this binary's `PROTOCOL_VERSION`, restated at the top level. |
| `source` | client-owned by policy | the literal `"daemon"`, distinguishing a live snapshot from the fixture bridge's. |

### Beyond the snapshot

The bridge emits **six** more DTOs plus one event payload. They are listed because leaving them out would make "every value the desktop presents" the kind of claim this sweep exists to replace. (It said *four* until this line was recounted at PRD #742 M7, and the drift is the same unremarked kind the `localStorage` count below records against itself: `DesktopSettingsSnapshotDto` arrived with PRD #803 and `EndpointTestReportDto` with PRD #741, and neither updated the number.)

| value | class | where it comes from |
| --- | --- | --- |
| `DesktopActionResult.ok` / `.message` | computed locally | the client's outcome for the action it just issued. |
| `DesktopActionResult.agentId` / `.agentIds` | daemon-sourced | ids the daemon minted for the agents it just started. |
| `DesktopActionResult.sendResult` | daemon-sourced | `AttachResponse::send_result`, the daemon's honest delivery outcome (PRD #20). |
| `DesktopActionResult.snapshot` | — | the table above. |
| `TerminalAttachResult.sessionId` / `.generation` / `.reused` | client-owned by policy | the desktop's own per-window terminal bookkeeping: an opaque id minted from a process-local counter, for a session that exists only inside this app. |
| `TerminalAttachResult.agentId`, `TerminalStateEvent.agentId` | daemon-sourced | the agent the session is attached to. |
| `TerminalStateEvent.state` / `.message` | computed locally | this client's view of its own stream. |
| `DesktopSettingsDto.version`, `.appearance.mode`, `.zoom.level` | client-owned by policy | PRD #803's settings document, source 3 above. The daemon is never told and never asks. |
| `DesktopSettingsDto.endpoints` | client-owned by policy | PRD #741's decks: the remote rows and the selection token. An endpoint describes reachability *from this installation* — a socket path, an ssh host, a forwarded socket — so it is client-owned by the criterion rather than by exception, and none of it syncs. **Optional, and `undefined` means *unspecified* rather than *empty*** — see [`Option<EndpointSettings>` is merge protection](#optionendpointsettings-is-merge-protection-and-it-puts-an-obligation-on-every-future-panel). The daemon is never told which decks exist, including the one it is. |
| `EndpointTestReportDto.endpointId` / `.deck` | client-owned by policy | the `Selection` token this report is about, and how that deck is named to a user — `RemoteEndpoint::describe`, or the local socket path. |
| `EndpointTestReportDto.state` / `.ok` / `.message` / `.remedy` | computed locally | `Test connection`'s verdict on a deck, as one of twelve named states. The classification is this client's and so is every sentence: the vocabulary is protocol versions, build stamps and forwarded sockets, and a state whose sentence lives beside its classification cannot drift from it. `ok` is emitted rather than derived in the webview so the twelve-way judgement stays in one place, and it is stamped once on the way out by `sealed()` rather than maintained beside each assignment to `state`. |
| `EndpointTestReportDto.detail` | mixed | ssh's own stderr, or this client's own transport error, absent when there were none. Scrubbed through `safe_display_text` — control **and** bidi — because these bytes were written by a remote host or by a `~/.ssh/config` someone else may have planted. (A daemon's *handshake* rejection reaches `message` instead, where it is the daemon's own sentence.) |
| `EndpointTestReportDto.clientProtocolVersion` / `.clientBuildVersion` | client-owned by policy | this binary's `PROTOCOL_VERSION` and `DAD_BUILD_ID`, the same two values the connection DTO restates. |
| `EndpointTestReportDto.serverProtocolVersion` / `.daemonBuildVersion` / `.runningAgentCount` | daemon-sourced | the `Hello` reply's own fields, when the probe got that far. |
| `EndpointTestReportDto.forwards` / `.forwardsKnown` | computed locally | what this deck's tunnel will inherit from the user's `~/.ssh/config`, resolved by `ssh -G` against the tunnel's own generated config and forced options. It describes *this machine's* ssh configuration, which no daemon can know. `forwardsKnown` is the difference between an answer and an absence, and is separate precisely so an unknown is never rendered as "none": empty **and** known is the only combination that means there are none. |
| `EndpointTestReportDto.discoveredSocket` | daemon-sourced | the remote attach socket path, learned over the probe's own round trip. Discovered rather than derived, because OpenSSH expands neither `~` nor an environment variable on the remote side of `-L` and the far host's `XDG_RUNTIME_DIR` and uid are not knowable from here. The **panel** writes it back into the row, not this reply — `useDesktopSettings` already serialises the document's read-modify-write, and a second writer in Rust would be [#828](https://github.com/vfarcic/dot-agent-deck/issues/828) created on purpose. |
| `DesktopSettingsSnapshotDto.path` | client-owned by policy | where *this installation* keeps that document. Absent from the fixture bridge's reply, which has no file — the surface renders the browser-preview wording rather than naming a path that does not exist. |
| the `deck` field on a `desktop://daemon-event` payload | client-owned by policy | which deck a forwarded daemon broadcast came from (PRD #742 M3) — `connection.deckId`'s own value, minted by this client from the endpoint its watcher is pinned to. The **event** underneath it is daemon-sourced and unchanged: `deck` is flattened beside a `BroadcastMsg` rather than wrapping it, so every snake_case field `daemonEvents.ts` reads stays exactly where it was. It exists because `BroadcastMsg` carries no deck id, so "which deck is this event from" used to be answerable only by "there is exactly one". |

### The four values the deck still invents, and who owns them

`mapDesktopSnapshot` builds the deck's run model on top of the DTO, and four of its fields have no daemon counterpart at all. PRD #745 deleted the branch chip and the attempt readouts outright for exactly this reason — a fabricated `ATT 01` reads as data in a way an explicit "unavailable" does not — and these are what remain.

| value | class | verdict |
| --- | --- | --- |
| `repo` | computed locally | the basename of `cwd`, split in the webview. Still split here for the *agent-derived* `cwd`, which is a working directory rather than a project identity. The **project** header no longer goes through it: since #819 M6 the topbar prefers the resolved selection's `name`, which the desktop crate derives once from the daemon's canonical path, so the picker and the header cannot disagree about a symlinked project's basename (PRD #220's bug). |
| `worktree` / the `cwd` fallback chain | computed locally | **Three** legs since #819 M6, not four: a daemon-reported agent `cwd`, then the *previous snapshot's* `worktree`, then the literal `"No active project"`. The removed leg was `projectCwd`, the client's own guess, and it is what made the header and the panes able to name different hosts. The surviving middle leg carries a value that WAS daemon-reported, so the header does not blink to "No active project" when the last agent exits. |
| `spend`, `elapsed` | computed locally | hardcoded `0` and `"—"`. Model, cost, tokens and context window are [#633](https://github.com/vfarcic/dot-agent-deck/issues/633)'s subject — out of #819's scope but not out of the principle: #633's discovery work is choosing how the *daemon* acquires them. |
| `profiles` | computed locally | `DEFAULT_PROFILES`, overlaid from the `agent-profiles.v1` key below. [#824](https://github.com/vfarcic/dot-agent-deck/issues/824). |

`runId`, `stages`, `health`, `currentNode`, `totalNodes` and `paused` are presentation derived from daemon-sourced agent state, or inert constants in live mode (`paused` is only ever written by the fixture bridge). They are client-owned by policy.

### The five `localStorage` keys, and the one that is gone

**Four** of the five are written through `modeScopedKey`, which suffixes the runtime mode — a fixture visit cannot write a key live mode reads back, which it once could, and which handed a real workflow launch a working directory that never existed. The fifth, #803's fixture settings key, is deliberately outside that scheme; its row says why, and it is the one row here where an unscoped key is not a defect.

| key | class | verdict |
| --- | --- | --- |
| ~~`dot-agent-deck.desktop.projects.v1`~~ | **gone** | It was a list the user typed into, with nothing validating it against the daemon's world, and it was the source of truth for the launch cwd. **#819 M6 removed it, with nothing persisted in its place** — the picker holds a resolved selection in React state and it dies with the launch. Any future convenience must be prefill for a field the daemon still resolves, never an authority. Record the decision on #824, which stays open for the other three. |
| `dot-agent-deck.desktop.agent-profiles.v1` | computed locally | per-project draft content. [#824](https://github.com/vfarcic/dot-agent-deck/issues/824), which stays open for this one. |
| `dot-agent-deck.desktop.prompts.v1` | computed locally | the prompt library. #824. |
| `dot-agent-deck.desktop.workflow-preview.v1` | computed locally | workflow role order. #824. |
| `dot-agent-deck.desktop.overview-columns.v1` | client-owned by policy | which columns *this display* shows. Genuinely presentational and correctly client-side. |
| `dot-agent-deck.desktop-settings` | client-owned by policy | PRD #803's settings document as the **fixture** bridge stores it, so a `pnpm dev` + `?fixture=1` preview survives a reload. Note the spelling: no `.desktop.` segment, no `.v1`, and **no `modeScopedKey`** — which is safe here structurally rather than by care, because `FixtureDeckBridge` never imports `@tauri-apps/api/core` and `TauriDeckBridge` reads `desktop.toml` and never reads `localStorage`, so the two cannot collide over it. Correctly client-side either way. |

**#824 says "the four `localStorage` keys" and there are now five — and none of the three moves since it was filed cancel out.** `overview-columns.v1` arrived with PRD #745, taking the count to five; PRD #803 added the fixture settings key, taking it to six; and #819 M6 removed `projects.v1`, bringing it to five. That is the order they actually landed in — #745 and #803 are both on `main`, and M6 is this PRD — so the count never passes through four on the way. The total is one above where #824 found it and the substance has moved further than that: the two keys that arrived are both on the right side of the boundary, and the key that left was the one furthest from it, so **#824 is now open over exactly three keys** — `agent-profiles.v1`, `prompts.v1` and `workflow-preview.v1` — rather than the four it names. All three moves are recorded here because a count that drifts unremarked is how the next key lands unclassified.

### The whole client-local filesystem surface

The criterion is **"the client resolves no project against a filesystem"**, not "the client touches no filesystem" — the wide version fails on day one and is quotable, so the narrow one is stated with its exceptions enumerated rather than waved at.

The production filesystem surface of `desktop/src-tauri/src/` falls in **two** places, enumerated below. Neither grep alone finds all of it: `grep -n 'std::fs' desktop/src-tauri/src/*.rs` misses every probe in group 1, because those go through `Path::is_file` and `Path::metadata`, while a probe-shaped grep misses the file handles in group 2. Both together are the enumeration that works:

```
grep -n 'is_file()\|is_dir()\|\.exists()\|metadata(\|read_dir\|canonicalize' desktop/src-tauri/src/*.rs
grep -n 'std::fs\|OpenOptions\|File::open' desktop/src-tauri/src/*.rs
```

1. **Locating the bundled daemon sidecar** for **Replace deck** — two call sites in one function: `is_executable_file` (`daemon_bridge.rs`: the `is_file` and the `metadata` inside it), the `current_exe` sibling search that calls it, plus the `DOT_AGENT_DECK_BINARY` and `PATH` env lookups beside them. **Legitimate and permanent:** this is the client finding *its own installation*, which no daemon can tell it.
2. **The desktop's own settings** — `settings.rs`, allowed by the same rule and **no longer an empty set**: PRD #803's store arrived after this sweep was first written. Its production call sites are the path vetting (`symlink_metadata`), the bounded read (`File::open` plus a capped read), and the atomic publish (`OpenOptions` create-new for the temp file, `write_all`, `sync_all`, `rename`, and a `remove_file` on the failure path), against a path from `config_dir()` or `DOT_AGENT_DECK_DESKTOP_CONFIG`. Every one of them touches **this installation's own preferences file and nothing else**, which is the client-owned side of the boundary by the criterion in [The ownership boundary](#the-ownership-boundary). It resolves no project and reads no project state; [check 12](#the-tripwire-on-that-invariant-and-what-it-cannot-do) passes over it unchanged.
3. ~~**`desktop_project_cwd()`**~~ — two `is_file` probes for a `.dot-agent-deck.toml`. **This was the defect, and #819 M6 deleted the function.** Nothing replaced it: the two groups above are the whole surface.

One more read used to be invisible to every grep above because it happened inside the shared root crate: `prepare_orchestrator_prompt`'s `create_dir_all` and `write`, reached from `lib.rs`'s workflow-launch path. It was a **write** rather than a read and it was the other half of the defect; #819 M4 moved it daemon-side and M6 moved the desktop's launch onto the `prepare-workflow` verb that performs it there. That it cannot be found by searching this crate is precisely why #819 M7 adds the linkage-check tripwire described [below](#the-tripwire-on-that-invariant-and-what-it-cannot-do) — and why the tripwire is labelled a tripwire and not a boundary, since the desktop crate path-depends on the root crate and every `pub` item there stays reachable.

### The tripwire on that invariant, and what it cannot do

PRD #819 M7 adds **check 12** to `cargo xtask linkage-check` ([`xtask/linkage-check/src/desktop_project_boundary.rs`](../../xtask/linkage-check/src/desktop_project_boundary.rs)), over the production sources of `desktop/src-tauri/src/`.

**It is a regression tripwire. It is not enforcement, and it is not a security boundary.** The PRD's first draft called it enforcement and the security audit was right to object, so the framing is stated here rather than left to be inferred. `desktop/src-tauri/Cargo.toml` carries `dot-agent-deck = { path = "../.." }`, so every `pub` item in the root crate stays callable from the desktop crate whatever this rule says — which is exactly how `load_project_config` was reachable from `lib.rs` in the first place. The rule can notice the shapes that read like a client-side project resolution; it cannot make one unreachable.

What it catches, by parsing Rust rather than matching substrings — a substring scan drowns in comments and string literals, and a sweep that produces noise gets switched off:

| finding | what trips it |
| --- | --- |
| `root-module` | a `use` or qualified path naming a root-crate module that is not on the rule's positive allowlist. The allowlist is the **boundary as a positive statement** — the modules the production desktop may reach across — so a fifth forbidden name does not need adding to a blacklist to be caught. |
| `forbidden-symbol` | `project_config`, `orchestrator_context`, `load_project_config` or `prepare_orchestrator_prompt` as a path segment anywhere, including through an alias (`use … as pc` still spells `project_config` in its path), a grouped or multi-line import, or a fully qualified call with no import at all. |
| `project-state-literal` | a string literal carrying `.dot-agent-deck.toml` or `orchestrator-context.md`, including inside a `format!` — the desktop's project prose lives almost entirely in macro arguments, which syn leaves as opaque tokens, so the rule walks those token streams too. |
| `cwd-fallback` | `std::env::current_dir`, the guess `desktop_project_cwd()` fell back to. On a remote daemon that guess resolves against the wrong filesystem and is silently wrong, which is the defect #819 exists to remove. |

`#[cfg(test)]` items are out of scope — the desktop crate's test modules legitimately build project fixtures, and a rule that failed on them would be worked around rather than obeyed. `#[cfg(not(test))]` is production and *is* scanned.

**The residual, named rather than implied.** A source rule is bypassable, unintentionally as easily as deliberately:

- **a root-crate wrapper with an innocuous name** — a new `pub fn` in an already-allowlisted module whose body calls `load_project_config`. Nothing in the desktop's source says so, so nothing in this rule can see it. This is the likeliest accidental bypass and it is pinned by a test (`a_root_crate_wrapper_with_an_innocuous_name_is_not_caught`) that asserts the rule does **not** catch it, so the gap stays visible instead of being rediscovered;
- **a macro that generates a forbidden path** out of tokens that are not one — syn does not expand macros;
- **an already-imported module gaining a new method** that reads project state;
- **a trait method** brought in by an allowlisted `use`;
- anything reached through `std::fs` by a path the rule has no literal for.

Only removing the desktop's dependency on the full root crate gives compiler-enforced reachability, and that is [#176](https://github.com/vfarcic/dot-agent-deck/issues/176) M1.1 — out of scope for #819 and one more reason to do it.

**The rule shipped before the milestone that satisfies it, and the milestone has now landed.** M6 (the desktop launch flow) deleted `desktop_project_cwd()` and moved the launch onto the daemon's `prepare-workflow` verb. While the tree still violated check 12, those violations were enumerated one by one in the rule's `PENDING_M6` allowlist rather than the rule being weakened to let them through — a weakened rule catches nothing new either. An allowlist entry that matches **no** finding is itself a failure, and that forcing function fired exactly as designed: M6 removed the last client-side project read, the check went red on ten stale entries, and they were deleted along with what they excused. **`PENDING_M6` is now empty, and it should stay that way** — a new entry excuses a new client-side project read rather than fixing it.

## Application settings

The desktop app owns exactly one thing and gets everything else from the daemon: **its own settings**. They live in a per-installation TOML document at `platform::paths::config_dir()/desktop.toml` — a **sibling** of the TUI's `config.toml` and `keybindings.toml`, never a section inside them, because `DashboardConfig::save()` serialises its struct and would silently delete a `[desktop]` table it did not know about. `DOT_AGENT_DECK_DESKTOP_CONFIG` overrides the whole path and is the seam the tests use. Whatever resolves it, the path is **vetted before it is read or written**: absolute, with a file name, and either absent or a plain regular file — a symlink, directory, FIFO, socket, device or Windows reparse point is refused — and at most 256 KiB is read. That is not a privilege boundary (anyone who can set your environment can already run code as you); it is there because an app that hangs forever on a FIFO or dies on `/dev/zero` is a miserable thing to debug. A refused path is logged with the path and reported to the webview without it, the same split every write error uses. The store is `desktop/src-tauri/src/settings.rs`; the surface is the **Settings** rail button, *Open settings* in the command palette (⌘K), and the same Escape/backdrop/close-button trio as every other sheet. Saves are optimistic and **serialised**: `useDesktopSettings` applies the choice immediately, writes behind it one request at a time, and drops a superseded response, so two rapid choices land on disk in the order they were made. Two *processes* racing on the same field — two app windows, or the app and a hand edit — is still unhandled and tracked as [#828](https://github.com/vfarcic/dot-agent-deck/issues/828); the merge already stops either of them losing an unknown *section*. PRD #803 has the full design; this section is the contract.

Four behaviours are worth knowing before building on it. **Loading never fails** — a missing, unparseable or unreadable file, and an unknown enum value, all yield defaults, because a settings file is not worth failing an app launch over. **A document this build cannot read at save time is never written over** — see [When the document cannot be read](#when-the-document-cannot-be-read), which is the one behaviour here that changed rather than accumulated. **Writing is atomic and owner-only** — a temp file in the same directory, then a rename, with mode `0o600` on Unix. And **writing preserves what this build does not understand**: the save merges the serialised struct into the document already on disk rather than replacing it, so a section written by a newer build survives an older build's save. That last one is not something `#[serde(default)]` gives you — without `deny_unknown_fields` it means *ignore*, not *retain*, so the unknown table is dropped at load and the next save would write over it.

**It preserves layout as well as data, and [#825](https://github.com/vfarcic/dot-agent-deck/issues/825) is where the second half arrived.** The merge used to round-trip through `toml::Table`, which models data, so every save re-rendered the whole document canonically and a user's own comments went with it — which matters because this file is one PRD #803 explicitly invites people to hand-edit. It runs on a `toml_edit::DocumentMut` now and writes only the keys whose data actually changed, so comments (including a trailing one on a line whose value *did* change), inline tables, inline arrays, key order and blank-line grouping all survive, and a save with no new value to write leaves the file byte for byte as it found it. Two limits, because the wide claim would be false: a **changed** list is rewritten whole, so a comment between `[[endpoints.remote]]` rows goes with it the first time a deck is added, removed or edited — an unchanged list is left alone; and a document missing a section this build owns gains that section, appended after what is already there. `merged_document`'s doc comment in `settings.rs` carries both, and the properties are pinned by `a_hand_written_comment_survives_an_app_driven_save`, `a_save_that_changes_nothing_rewrites_nothing` and `a_comment_inside_a_list_survives_until_that_list_changes`.

**This is why the desktop crate names `toml_edit` and not `toml`.** It is a replacement rather than an addition: two TOML libraries in one crate means two parsers that can disagree about the same file, and `toml_edit::de::from_str` / `toml_edit::ser::to_string_pretty` cover every call site `settings.rs` had, byte-identically (pinned by `default_document_shape_is_pinned`). It costs nothing in dependency surface either — the root package has declared `toml_edit = "0.25"` since PRD #20 for exactly this reason (a format-preserving edit of the user's own `~/.codex/config.toml`, where a serde round trip would strip every comment), the desktop crate already links the root package, and the switch adds **zero** packages to `Cargo.lock`: the only change is two feature edges (`serde_core`, `serde_spanned`) on a crate that was already being built, both of which the graph already had through `toml`.

### When the document cannot be read

A `desktop.toml` this build cannot load leaves the app on defaults **for the sections it could not read**, and blocks every save until the file is fixed or removed ([#1072](https://github.com/vfarcic/dot-agent-deck/issues/1072)). Four things follow from that and all four matter:

- **Only the refused section goes to defaults.** `toml_edit::de::from_str` is all-or-nothing, so until PR #1163 one value this build rejected reset *everything* in memory — the user's `[endpoints]`, their appearance, their zoom, their deck selection — and a stale `[voice]` token from an older build was enough to do it. `sections_this_build_can_read` now judges each top-level key on its own and, for a section that fails as a whole, each of its children the same way; what survives is kept and only what does not is defaulted. The recursion stops at the section rather than the field, **deliberately**: pruning one refused key would leave its siblings, and for a voice stage a surviving `backend = "remote"` beside a dropped `endpoint` fills the endpoint from the *hosted* preset, which is a user's audio going somewhere they did not write. Nothing about the bullet below changes — the file itself was never modified, and still is not.
- **The app still starts, and starts normally.** Refusing to launch over a preferences file would be disproportionate and would hand anyone who can write that file a denial of service on the whole app; the existing "loading never fails" property is deliberately kept.
- **The reason is on screen, not only in the log.** `load_document` returns a `SettingsDocumentProblem`, `load_snapshot` puts its public half on the snapshot as `problem`, and `useDesktopSettings` folds it into the same slot a failed save uses, so every settings panel renders it the moment the sheet is opened — before the user has changed anything. It is a **locator** (`line 3, column 9`) plus fixed prose, never a byte of the document; the footer beside it names the file.
- **`save_to` refuses rather than merging.** It re-reads the document, parses it as `DesktopSettings` — not as a bare TOML document, because a document that is valid TOML which this build's *schema* rejects is equally unreadable and equally destructive to merge into — and returns an error naming the line. The file is left byte-identical. The check is at **save** time rather than inherited from the launch read, which is what makes the remaining window a microsecond-wide race between two writers rather than the whole lifetime of the app: a document that becomes unreadable between that read and the rename is still replaced, which is [#828](https://github.com/vfarcic/dot-agent-deck/issues/828)'s subject and the same residual the path vet already accepts.

**What this replaces is worth stating, because it shipped and it was silent.** The load answered every failure with defaults and an `eprintln!`, so the app came up looking like a fresh install. The next save then merged the struct over the file, and the merge makes the struct authoritative over every key it owns — appearance, zoom, endpoints and the deck selection all replaced with defaults. The symptom a user saw was not an error; it was "my settings reset themselves", one click later, with the original already gone.

**A schema that is still moving is the ordinary route into all of this, and PRD #802's `[voice]` section is the worked example.** Its three keys were scalars — `transcription = "off"` — and two of them became tables. A document written by that build therefore hands the parser a **string where a table is expected**, which is a *type* error rather than an unknown token, so the folding deserializers every `[voice]` enum has never get a look in and `#[serde(default)]` does not fire for a value that is present and wrong. It is not a corrupt file, nobody hand-edited it, and it cost the whole document. `StageSpec` in `settings.rs` is what stops it failing at all: a stage accepts either shape, and a bare token means *this backend, and say nothing else* — so the value lands on that backend's current coordinates through `for_backend`, and a token naming a backend this build deleted folds to the default the same way any unknown token does. The first save after that writes the section back in the new shape, so the migration completes rather than being re-folded on every load. **If you change a section's shape, write the fold at the same time** — a partial recovery is the safety net, not the migration.
