**The failure is reachable without anyone hand-editing anything.** An interrupted write, a dotfile-sync conflict marker or disk damage all produce it. So does this codebase's own habit of tightening field validators, which converts previously-valid documents into unloadable ones: `port` moved to a newtype over `NonZeroU16` after a hand-edited `port = 0` reached OpenSSH as `-p 0`, and PRD #742 made `EndpointId::parse` reserve `all`. That is why the fix is here rather than in a rule about who may edit the file.

**`desktop.toml` stays hand-editable**, which #1072 raised as an open question. The argument for dropping it is that someone who edits config by hand is not reaching for a desktop GUI. It does not survive contact with the list above: dropping it removes neither the failure nor the need for this fix, while `DOT_AGENT_DECK_DESKTOP_CONFIG`, [Configuring a remote deck by hand](#configuring-a-remote-deck-by-hand) and PRD #803's "a file a user can read, edit and delete without the app running" all depend on it. The validating newtypes are what make hand-editing safe rather than what makes it costly.

**Two things this does not do.** There is no in-app "replace it anyway" button: the destructive action is exactly the one being prevented, the message names the line to open, and a user who wants defaults can delete the file. And a *save* failure is still not a *load* failure — a read-only directory or a full disk reports "saving failed, your change will not survive a restart" and leaves the change applied, because there the document on disk is fine.

### The ownership boundary

The rule the whole app is arranged around, and the thing to read before deciding where a new setting goes:

> The desktop app gets everything from the daemon, wherever that daemon runs. The only thing it owns is its own settings.

| | |
| --- | --- |
| **Daemon-owned** | the project config, the coordinator context, cwd and project paths, available orchestrations/modes/roles, dispatch targets, the agent list and PTY streams, telemetry |
| **Client-owned** | settings — endpoints, appearance, model backends — plus genuinely presentational state: window size and position, focused tab, zoom |

The criterion for anything not on those lists, stated so it can be applied without re-deriving the reasoning: **a setting is client-owned when it describes the client itself — this machine, this display, this installation. If it describes the work, the project, or the machine the agents run on, it is daemon-owned and does not belong in this store, however convenient it would be to put it here.**

The failure this prevents has already shipped once. Reading `.dot-agent-deck.toml` client-side gives the right answer whenever the daemon is local, so nothing looked wrong until a real remote session put a local `/Users/…` path in the header beside the remote's `/home/…` panes. A rule that applies only to remote leaves the same trap for the next feature; a rule that applies always cannot. The converse matters too: **if a settings item ever needs the daemon to know about it, that item is by definition daemon-owned** — and at that point CLAUDE.md rule 12's cross-version contract check applies to it, on the other side of the line.

Everything in the store is deliberately about *this installation on this machine*, and none of it syncs. An endpoint describes reachability from here — a socket path, an ssh host, a forwarded socket. An appearance override is about this display. Downloaded models are on this disk. The cautionary precedent is the TUI's: `keybindings_path()` resolves through `platform::paths::config_dir()` on the machine the *process* runs on, and `dot-agent-deck connect` runs the TUI on the remote, so a `keybindings.toml` edited on the laptop is never read. Never store desktop settings daemon-side, and never read daemon-owned state from the client's disk.

### Adding a setting

Three steps, and the contract is deliberately this small. Adding a **setting** is step 1 plus an edit to your own panel; adding a **section** is one registry row and one component. Neither requires touching the store, the sheet, or anything belonging to another feature.

1. **Storage.** Add a field to your feature's section struct in `desktop/src-tauri/src/settings.rs`, or add a new section struct plus one line on `DesktopSettings`. `#[serde(default)]` throughout gives it a default; there is deliberately no `deny_unknown_fields`, so a field a newer build writes survives an older build reading the file. Field names stay `snake_case` and single-word where that is natural: the same struct is serialised to TOML for the user *and* to JSON for the webview, and every name today is one word, so the two agree byte for byte. The first genuinely multi-word field is the point at which a separate webview DTO has to be introduced — not a `rename_all` on this struct, which would make the TOML read badly.
2. **UI.** Add one row — `id`, `label`, `icon`, `component` — to `SETTINGS_SECTIONS` in `desktop/src/lib/settingsRegistry.ts`, and own your panel component. The registry is a module of its own rather than a constant inside the sheet, so registering never means editing a #803-owned rendering component and two dependents cannot collide on adjacent lines of one array; `SettingsSheet.tsx` reads it and is otherwise none of your business. Implement `SettingsPanelProps` from `desktop/src/lib/settingsContract.ts`: that file *is* the type to build against, and it is short on purpose. A panel is an ordinary React component and owns its own layout — there is no generic key/value renderer, because #741's endpoint list and #802's model manager are not key/value widgets and a renderer built to fit both would fit neither. Send the **whole document** to `onSave` (spread `settings`, replace your own section) so a save can never drop a section this build's UI has not loaded.

   **That is necessary and not sufficient, which PRD #741 M6 found out the hard way.** The document a panel spreads is the one `normalizeDesktopSettings` produced, and that function constructs a fresh object with a **fixed key set** — which is itself a credential guard (check 3 below), so it is not going away. A section the frontend does not declare is therefore already gone before any panel spreads it, and `desktop_set_settings` then merges the struct it decoded over the file. If the field is a plain section struct, `#[serde(default)]` turns "the webview said nothing" into "the webview said empty", and the merge writes that empty section over the user's data. So a section whose frontend half has not landed yet is declared `Option<…>` on `DesktopSettings`: `None` means *unspecified*, TOML omits it, and `merged_document` preserves what is on disk — the same protection it gives across builds, now across clients. `#741`'s `endpoints` is the worked example, and the rule that comes with it is that the *frontend* half has to round-trip the section the moment it can render it, never fabricate a default for it.
3. **Secrets never go in the document, and never in `localStorage`.** The document may hold a non-secret *reference* — which backend holds the key, or a boolean saying one is stored — and nothing more. A real credential belongs behind the `SecretStore` seam in `desktop/src-tauri/src/secrets.rs`: store/load/delete keyed by a `SecretId`, over the OS keychain. PRD #803 M5 named it and PRD #802 M4 built it, and #802's `[voice]` section is the worked example of what the rule costs in practice — the *choices* became closed enums and the *key* went to the keychain, so the document holds no field for it at all. Note it holds no boolean either, although the rule would allow one: the panel asks the store, so the answer cannot go stale against the keychain. #802's later provider work is the **second** half of that example and the more instructive one: making each stage's endpoint and model user-selectable needed two values that genuinely are text, and the answer was two validating newtypes in `desktop/src-tauri/src/model_service.rs` — `ServiceUrl`, which refuses a `user:password@` authority and allows `http` only to loopback, and `ModelId`, bounded at 128 bytes over a charset with no whitespace. Neither is a way around the guard; each is a written claim about what that field can hold, which is what an `ALLOWED_FIELD_TYPES` row is. **A field-level claim is not always the whole rule, though, and #802's speech stage is where that bit.** `TranscriptionBackend::Local` means exactly one thing — *no `Authorization` header is sent* — and `ServiceUrl` on its own will accept any `https://host`, so the two fields are each valid and the *pair* is a captured utterance uploaded to a third party with no credential on it, reachable by picking the keyless backend in the panel and then editing the Endpoint field. Where a rule spans two fields it has to be enforced where the struct is built: `TranscriptionSettings`'s hand-written `Deserialize` refuses the pairing — covering the document and the IPC seam, which is every route an untrusted value arrives by — and `HttpTranscriber::keyless` refuses it again at construction, which is the route Rust arrives by. The panel still offers the Endpoint field for that backend, because a user who publishes the container on another port has to be able to say so; it narrows the hint instead, and is not the boundary. The next section is what enforces that, and the practical consequence for step 1 is short: **your field's type has to be on `ALLOWED_FIELD_TYPES`** in `xtask/linkage-check/src/desktop_settings_secrets.rs`, and `String` is deliberately not on it. If you need free text, that is the conversation to have before writing the field, not after.

**A setting is one row, and the row is two columns** — a **132px label column**, a 16px gutter, then the control left-aligned in the second column. Not "label left, control right": the row is 743px wide in the desktop sheet, so a flush-right control leaves ~500px of nothing between a label and the thing it labels, and the two stop reading as one setting. The label column is a fixed width rather than `auto` because that is the entire reason to use a grid here — every row's control starts at the same x, so a panel of ten settings reads as two columns instead of a ragged stack, and a dependent panel gets that alignment without measuring anything. 132px is chosen against labels that do not exist yet: it fits any two-word label at the size below (`Daemon endpoint` is 130px, the longest realistic one), and it is about the widest column that still leaves the control looking attached to its label. A longer label wraps inside its column and grows the row by one line rather than breaking anything — `Downloaded models` is 148px, and it takes the row from 27px to 30px. Below a 420px viewport the row stacks, label over control, because the columns plus a segmented control need 322px and the sheet goes full-bleed under 680px; that fallback is the one place the row is not two columns.

**A row's label is never a `<legend>`, and that is a WebKit finding rather than a preference** ([#1032](https://github.com/vfarcic/dot-agent-deck/issues/1032), found by PRD #741 M7, measured on the browser tier). `AppearancePanel` used to put the row's visible label in a `<legend>` and float it, because a floated legend stops being the fieldset's *rendered* legend and can then be an ordinary box among the fieldset's contents — the grid item in column one. Chromium honours that. **WebKit forces the rendered legend's `float` to `none`**: measured at the same `?fixture=1` page in both projects, `getComputedStyle(legend).float` is `"left"` under `chromium` and `"none"` under `webkit`, so the legend never became a grid item there, the segmented control landed in column **one** beside its label 148px to the left of every other control in the sheet, and the 132px column was wasted. The app ships on WebKit — WebKitGTK under Tauri on Linux, WKWebView on macOS — so the fieldset form was the one that did not hold where it matters, and the form that worked was the one only Chromium saw. Nothing caught it because no browser spec asserted settings-row geometry until `e2e/decks.spec.ts` did.

**So: use a `<span class="settings-row-label" id>` plus `role="radiogroup" aria-labelledby` on the control for a row that needs a group label**, which lays out identically in both engines and keeps the accessible group name — `EndpointsPanel`'s deck chooser and the converted `AppearancePanel` are the worked examples, and `DeckSelector`'s menu uses the same form outside the sheet. A row whose label is a plain `<label for>` — the select rows, `ZoomPanel` among them — was **never** affected: a single control needs no group, and a `<label>` is an ordinary grid item in both engines. There is no `<fieldset>` left in the app, and no `.settings-row > legend` rule in the stylesheet.

**The convention is pinned by measurement rather than by this paragraph.** `desktop/e2e/settings-rows.spec.ts` walks every section the registry offers, in **both** browser-tier projects, and holds each row to the same numbers `e2e/decks.spec.ts` holds the Decks panel to: every label and every control starts at the same x across the whole sheet, the gutter is 148px, the first grid track is `132px`, and every label is 13px/650. A panel registered later inherits the guard without anyone remembering to write one — which is the half that was missing when two panels were free to diverge. It also asserts directly that no row labels itself with a legend, so a failure names the cause rather than only reporting that a control moved.

**The label is 13px at weight 650, and that is part of the convention** — the row label is a sub-header, not body text, so it holds its own against the control beside it and gives a list of rows something to scan down. It sits well below the sheet's own 20px title, one notch lighter than the 13px/680 the app's in-page section headings use, and larger than the 11px of the sheet's section list — a sub-header inside a panel, not a heading over one. Copy it; a panel whose labels are 11px next to another panel's 13px is the same setting rendered at two different weights.

Pick the control by cardinality: a switch for a boolean, a **segmented control** for up to about four exclusive options with short labels (`.settings-row` + `.segmented` in `desktop/src/styles.css`; `AppearancePanel.tsx` is the worked example), a select beyond that. Per-option prose is the **exception, not the default**: write it only where an option's consequence genuinely cannot be read off its label, because a hint that restates its label costs a row and teaches nothing. Panel-level notes are a narrow exception with **no current instance**: the only one that ever existed — the terminals-stay-dark note in the Appearance panel — was deleted for failing the text rule below, and the `.settings-note` class went with it. If you think you have the first genuine one, that rule is the bar it has to clear, and you are adding the class back rather than reusing one.

**UI text earns its place by being actionable, not by being true.** The test is applied a sentence at a time: does it change what the reader does next? Explanation of *why* the software is built the way it is belongs in `docs/` and in the PRDs, and **never on a panel**. It is the easiest kind of text to justify keeping, precisely because it is accurate — which is exactly why it needs a rule rather than taste. Three worked examples, all of them deleted, because a deleted thing teaches this better than an abstraction: the Appearance panel carried a paragraph explaining why the agent terminals stay dark in both appearances — an engineering constraint, in vocabulary (truecolor SGR) nobody outside this repo has, answering a question nobody asked, while the *observable* fact needs no note at all; the settings sheet's header carried the client/daemon ownership boundary, an architectural principle for whoever adds the next setting, put in front of a user who only wanted dark mode; and the footer said the file was "readable, editable and deletable without Agent Deck running", which is reassurance about a design decision, leaving `Stored in <path>` as the actionable half. The first two are still written down — the terminals in this document and in `prds/743-desktop-light-dark-appearance.md`, the boundary in *The ownership boundary* above — which is where they belong. **An error, or a consequence the user has to act on, is a different thing and stays**: the Appearance panel's failed-save alert (`.settings-error`) tells the user their choice will not survive a restart, and that changes what they do next.

Why this is a rule rather than a preference: the sheet is 780px wide and full height, so **vertical space is the scarce resource**, and it is shared with every other feature's section. Appearance was first built as three full-width radio cards with a sentence of hint each — 195px for a single setting, and the hints ("Always light, whatever this machine is set to.") only restated their labels; as one row it is 27px. One row per setting is what lets a panel hold ten settings instead of three, and whatever density the first panel establishes is the one the next one copies.

**Do not put a heading above a control that already labels itself.** A section heading is chrome you add when there are sections to tell apart, not by default. With one section whose single setting shares its name, the heading was the word "Appearance" twice: the panel opened with a `.form-heading` carrying an `APPEARANCE` eyebrow over a `Light and dark` title, directly above a row whose own label then read *Appearance* from a `<legend>` — and `Light and dark` restated the option labels the three buttons were already showing. That cost 70px above a setting that occupies 61px, and it bought nothing a reader could not see. The row's own label is the one that survives, because it is doing two jobs the heading was not: it is the row's visible label *and* the group's accessible name, so `getByRole("radiogroup", { name: "Appearance" })` rides on it. (It was `getByRole("group", …)` on a `<legend>` until [#1032](https://github.com/vfarcic/dot-agent-deck/issues/1032) made it a `<span>` named through `aria-labelledby`; the accessible name is the property, and it survived the change.) The same round collapsed the sheet's own header to one line for the same reason — an `APPLICATION SETTINGS` eyebrow over `Settings` is one word twice — via a `.sheet-header.is-title-only` modifier, because the shared `.sheet-header` reserves 92px for the eyebrow/title/description stack that the four `ConfigurationPanels` sheets all still carry. Together: 223px of sheet chrome plus setting for one 27px control, now 119px — the setting itself did not move (61px both ways), all 104px came off the chrome. `App.test.tsx` pins it — the sheet holds exactly one heading, the panel holds none, and no `.eyebrow` or `.form-heading` survives on the surface.

**When [#741](https://github.com/vfarcic/dot-agent-deck/issues/741) or [#802](https://github.com/vfarcic/dot-agent-deck/issues/802) adds a second section, that is the moment to decide whether section headings earn their row** — and it is an open question, not a settled one, so do not pre-build for it. The argument against them survives the second section: the section column returns the moment the registry holds two rows, and it will already name the active section in the list beside the panel, so a heading repeating that name inside the panel is the same redundancy one level up. The argument for them is that two sections make a panel's identity worth confirming where the user is looking. Decide it with both panels in front of you; whichever way it goes, it is one rule for every panel rather than a per-panel choice.

Two things to expect while doing it. `default_document_shape_is_pinned` asserts the exact serialised default document, so **a new field fails that test by design** — updating it is the deliberate act that puts the ownership question in front of a reviewer. (An `Option` section whose default is `None` fails only the JSON half of that pin, because TOML omits a `None` field and JSON renders it `null`. The question still gets asked; the answer is just that the TOML default is unchanged, which is the point of the `Option`.) And the settings sheet **drops its section column while there is only one section**, rendering that panel full width; your registry row is what brings the column back, with no layout work on your side. Both directions are pinned with stub sections in `desktop/src/components/SettingsSheet.test.tsx`.

### The credential boundary

Two different kinds of check watch PRD #803's credential rule, and **confusing them is the mistake [#827](https://github.com/vfarcic/dot-agent-deck/issues/827) was opened about.** Read this before storing anything a user would mind losing.

`no_settings_key_name_trips_the_credential_tripwire` in `settings.rs` is a **naming tripwire, not a security boundary**. It fails the build on a key *name* containing `key`, `token`, `secret`, `password`, `credential`, `authorization`, `bearer` or `passphrase`, with an allowlist of full key **paths** (`voice.secret_backend`, not `secret_backend`, so an exception cannot travel to a same-named field in another section) — and since #827 each exemption also pins a **concrete type**, because an exemption granted for a boolean "is one stored" would otherwise keep covering that path after somebody changed the field to a `String` under the same name. What it still cannot see: a field called `endpoint` or `value` whose *value* is a token, and any value at all — it never looks at one. Read a pass as "nobody named a field like a credential", never as "a credential cannot get in here".

The checks that do look at values are these, and what they establish is narrower than an absolute — the narrow version is the one to quote:

| what it proves | where |
| --- | --- |
| **Every field in the Rust schema has a type from a pinned allowlist, and no field is free text.** Two strengths, and the difference matters: `u32`, `u16`, `AppearanceMode`, `ZoomLevel` and `Selection` cannot carry arbitrary text at all; the ssh-argument newtypes PRD #741 stores (`Hostname`, `SshUser`, `KeyPath`, `HostAlias`, `RemoteSocketPath`) and `EndpointId` **bound and charset-restrict** it, which is weaker — measured, a 45-byte `sk-…`-shaped token parses as a `Hostname`. What rules a credential out of *those* is where the value goes (each is handed to `ssh` as an argument and reaches no authentication surface), that key material and a passphrase are excluded by shape (`KeyPath` must start `/` or `~/`), and the policy that a credential goes behind `SecretStore`. A serde `skip`/`flatten` is refused too, because it hides a field from the document the sweep below walks | `desktop_settings_secrets::every_settings_field_has_a_type_that_cannot_carry_a_credential`, `settings::tests::an_ssh_argument_field_bounds_and_restricts_rather_than_forbidding_text` |
| **A uniquely-named sentinel reaches none of the sinks**: the `desktop_set_settings` echo, the `desktop_get_settings` snapshot, the parse diagnostic, both halves of `SettingsWriteError` including `safe_message` output, and both halves of `SettingsDocumentProblem` — which is the newest of them and the only one that reaches a **webview** as well as a log ([#1072](https://github.com/vfarcic/dot-agent-deck/issues/1072)). The **disk** sink is narrower than it was, and the old wording — *the document on disk across a load–modify–save round trip* — was true only because of the data loss #1072 fixed: the scrub was a side effect of publishing defaults over a document nobody had understood. A value that still lets the document load is overwritten by the round trip as before; one that makes it unreadable now stays in the user's own `0o600` file, which is the same answer already given for a key the schema does not own, and destroying a user's configuration is not a proportionate way to scrub a value they typed themselves. Both arms are exercised and the test fails if either stops being reached. Derived from the **default** document rather than a list, so a field #802 adds is covered the moment it appears *there* — which is the scope, not a formality: an optional field, or a field on a row in a list that defaults to empty, is outside this sweep because the default document does not contain it. PRD #741's endpoint fields are exactly that shape; the naming tripwire grew a populated `representative_document()` to reach them, and the value-side claim about them is made by `an_ssh_argument_field_bounds_and_restricts_rather_than_forbidding_text` instead. **What it reaches is the command *bodies*, not the commands** — a `#[tauri::command]` takes a `Webview`, which needs a running app, so `ensure_main_webview` and the framework's own argument decoding are outside every test in this crate ([#823](https://github.com/vfarcic/dot-agent-deck/issues/823) is the missing tier). `desktop_get_settings` is a one-line wrapper over `load_snapshot()`; `desktop_set_settings` wraps `save()` **plus** a `map_err` closure that logs `error.detail()` and returns `safe_message(error.public())`. `load_snapshot()` and `save()` are driven through the real path seam against a real file; the closure is reached by neither, and is covered instead by `no_settings_write_error_…`, which asserts both of the values it can emit are sentinel-free | `settings.rs`'s `a_credential_from_the_webview_…`, `a_credential_at_a_known_schema_leaf_…`, `the_settings_commands_own_bodies_…`, `a_parse_diagnostic_…`, `no_settings_write_error_…` |
| **The TypeScript DTO's shape is pinned field by field.** A frontend-only `apiKey` on `DesktopSettingsDto` used to be declared where nothing read it, because the Rust tripwire cannot see `bridge.ts` | `desktop_settings_secrets::the_typescript_settings_dtos_carry_exactly_the_pinned_fields` |
| **`normalizeDesktopSettings` constructs its result and never spreads its input**, so an undeclared field — a credential under any name — is dropped rather than carried into `localStorage` or into app state | `desktop_settings_secrets::the_settings_normaliser_…` (structural) plus `bridge.test.ts`'s three runtime tests |
| **The `localStorage` key set is pinned**, constant and literal, and `localStorage` may only be spelled `localStorage.<op>(...)` so an alias cannot name a key the scan is unable to read. Scope is `.ts`/`.tsx` under `desktop/src`: `sessionStorage`, IndexedDB, `document.cookie` and a `.js` file are outside it, and none of the four appears there today. Indifferent to [#824](https://github.com/vfarcic/dot-agent-deck/issues/824)'s question of which keys *belong* there | `desktop_settings_secrets::the_localstorage_key_set_is_pinned_and_holds_no_credential` |

**Three things none of that proves, stated because a boundary described too widely is worse than one described honestly.**

First, **a key this build's schema does not own keeps its value on disk.** The save merges rather than replaces, which is what stops an older build eating a newer one's section — so a credential a *user* or a *newer build* wrote into `desktop.toml` stays there. What it cannot do is reach anything else: the struct drops it at load, so it is absent from the IPC echo, from the settings snapshot and from every rendering of the document. `a_key_this_schema_does_not_own_keeps_its_value_and_reaches_nothing_else` measures exactly that rather than glossing it.

Second, **a refused IPC payload's serde message can quote the value back to the webview that sent it.** Nothing is disclosed to a party that did not already hold it, so this is a scope statement rather than a hole: the asserted claim is "no sink outside the sender".

Third, **the `SecretStore` now exists and "a credential can only enter through it" is still not assertable.** PRD #802 M4 built the seam #803 M5 named — `desktop/src-tauri/src/secrets.rs`, store/load/delete keyed by a [`SecretId`], over the OS keychain through `keyring` — and the field-type check is what forced it: adding the `[voice]` section turned that test red at four fields, and the resolution was closed enums for the choices and the keychain for the key. **Going red is how it said so, which is the mechanism working.** What is *not* assertable is the completeness claim: nothing stops a later feature storing a credential somewhere else entirely, and no check here would see it. What survives is the narrower claim PRD #741 M6 left behind — nothing in this schema is free text, every field is bounded and charset-restricted by a deserializer, and adding one that could hold a PEM block or a padded base64 blob means editing `ALLOWED_FIELD_TYPES` in a required gate.

Two properties of that store are worth knowing before reaching for it, because both are deliberate and both are easy to undo by accident. **There is no IPC command that reads a credential back** — the webview may ask whether one is stored, replace one and forget one, and `SecretStore::load` exists for the Rust side alone, where the backends make their network call anyway because the CSP forces every network hop there. A command returning a value to the webview would put it one `JSON.stringify` from the `localStorage` half of the rule. And **a failed store is an `Err`, never a status saying "nothing stored"**: the outcome the design is against is a user who thinks their key is saved and finds the feature broken tomorrow, so the three situations that produce a failure — no credential store on this machine, a locked one, and anything else the platform reports — each carry their own sentence naming what did not happen.

[`SecretId`]: ../../desktop/src-tauri/src/secrets.rs

One behaviour changed to close a sink rather than to name it. The load diagnostic used to print `toml::de::Error`'s own `Display`, which echoes the offending value **twice** — in a rendered source line and again in serde's `invalid type: string "…"` message — so a hand-edited document's bytes went straight into this process's stderr and the deck log, which is a file people paste into bug reports. `invalid_document_log` now prints a **locator** instead: the file and `line N, column M`, and nothing from the document. The trade is deliberate and it loses the `expected u32` half; the line is the line to open, where the offending text is in front of you anyway. That rule now governs two sinks rather than one — [#1072](https://github.com/vfarcic/dot-agent-deck/issues/1072) put the same locator in front of the **user**, so `parse_locator` is shared by the log line, the `SettingsDocumentProblem` the settings surface renders and the refusal `save_to` returns, and all three are a locator plus fixed prose.

Both scanners are in a **required** gate, and that placement is the point: they run under `cargo test-fast` (via `--workspace`) and in the CI `build` job, while `desktop-web` — where a vitest guard would run — is advisory and can be merged past. Same reasoning as the palette guards, and the same fail-closed discipline: every filesystem error fails the test rather than removing a file from the scan, and a symlink is refused rather than followed.

### Two persistence mechanisms, for now

The settings document is not the app's only storage. **Five keys** live in `window.localStorage`. Four are namespaced by `modeScopedKey` (`desktop/src/lib/bridge.ts`) with a `.live`/`.fixture` suffix — agent profiles, the prompt library, workflow role order, and the agent overview's column choice — and the fifth, `FIXTURE_SETTINGS_KEY`, is deliberately **unscoped**, because it holds the fixture preview's settings document and a theme choice is global. ([#824](https://github.com/vfarcic/dot-agent-deck/issues/824) and this document both said "four" until #827 pinned the set; the overview columns arrived with PRD #745 and the fixture settings key with #803, neither of which updated the count, and #819 M6 then deleted the project library key, taking the set from six to five. The whole set is pinned by `desktop_settings_secrets::the_localstorage_key_set_is_pinned_and_holds_no_credential`, so a drift now reddens a required check instead of leaving a stale sentence — that pin caught this very deletion on the merge, and its scope is `localStorage` in `.ts`/`.tsx` under `desktop/src`.) **Three of the five are per-project draft content** — agent profiles, the prompt library and workflow role order — which the boundary rule above puts daemon-side, so they were deliberately **not** migrated into `desktop.toml`: that store is per-installation too, and moving them there would entrench them on the same wrong side while looking resolved. [#824](https://github.com/vfarcic/dot-agent-deck/issues/824) tracks those three and [#819](https://github.com/vfarcic/dot-agent-deck/issues/819) is the plausible home; #819 M6 has already taken the worst of them, the project library, out of `localStorage` altogether. The other two are not in that argument: the overview column choice describes this display and the fixture settings key exists only so a browser preview survives a reload, so neither is waiting on #819. Whichever way #824 goes: **do not add a sixth `localStorage` key by pattern-matching.** Apply the criterion first — app preference goes in `desktop.toml`, project content waits for #819 — and note that since #827 the answer is no longer only a convention: the key set is pinned in a required gate, so a new key is a deliberate edit to `PINNED_STORAGE_KEYS` and a review conversation rather than a line nobody sees.

## Zoom and text size

`Cmd +` / `Cmd -` / `Cmd 0` on macOS, `Ctrl` equivalents elsewhere, scale the whole window — chrome and agent terminals alike — and the level persists in `[zoom]` in `desktop.toml`. The same level is visible and settable in the settings sheet's **Zoom** section, because a webview zoom has no indicator of its own and 110% against 100% is not a difference you can read off the screen. The ladder is ten levels from 75% to 300%, and 100% is what `Cmd 0` returns to. PRD #744 has the design.

### Webview zoom, not a CSS font scale

The mechanism is `webview.set_zoom()` — `webkit_web_view_set_zoom_level` on Linux, `WKWebView.setPageZoom` on macOS 11+, WebView2's zoom factor on Windows. **The alternative was not a real alternative**, and the measurement is worth keeping because it will come up again: `desktop/src/styles.css` contains **zero `rem` units** and 164 `font-size` declarations, every one of them in `px`, among 960 `px` literals. A root font-size is therefore not a scaling seam in this app — proposing one is proposing to rewrite those literals first — and it would still leave the terminals alone, because xterm renders to a canvas from its own `fontSize` option. Page zoom needs no stylesheet change and scales the panes with everything else.

Page zoom shrinks the **CSS-pixel** viewport, so the app's existing breakpoints (1450, 1260, 960, 680, 420) do the reflow: at 1440 × 1.5 it lays out at 960 CSS px and degrades exactly as it does when you narrow the window. That is also where the 300% ceiling comes from — `body` is `overflow-x: hidden`, so horizontal overflow is *clipped* rather than scrollable while vertical overflow merely scrolls, which makes width the binding constraint. `tauri.conf.json` declares `minWidth: 1024` and `styles.css` declares `min-width: 320px`; 1024 / 3.0 = 341 is clear of the floor and 1024 / 3.2 = 320 reaches it exactly. The 75% floor is the other end of the same argument: the agent footer is 6.8px, which renders at 5.1px there.

### Why `zoomHotkeysEnabled` is not used

Tauri ships a window option that looks like this feature in one boolean, and on macOS and Linux it injects a hotkey polyfill (`tauri/src/webview/scripts/zoom-hotkey.js`). Read it before reaching for it: it persists nothing (`let zoomLevel = 1`, so every launch is 100%), fires one un-coalesced IPC per keystroke, never re-fits the terminals, ranges 0.2–10.0 in additive 0.2 steps, calls no `preventDefault` in its keydown handler, and on **Windows** is not the polyfill at all — there the flag sets WebView2's `IsZoomControlEnabled` and `IsPinchZoomEnabled`, so the steps and range are the runtime's. It also needs `core:webview:allow-set-webview-zoom`, which we would otherwise not need at all.

### The call goes through Rust, and so does the launch apply

Two things follow from that permission. **`core:webview:default` does not include `allow-set-webview-zoom`** — its webview default set is `allow-get-all-webviews`, `allow-webview-position`, `allow-webview-size` and `allow-internal-toggle-devtools`, and this app's only capability is `["core:default"]`. So the frontend route (`getCurrentWebview().setZoom()`) would be denied, and widening the webview's own core surface to reach a call Rust can make itself is more privilege for no gain. The zoom is a `desktop_set_zoom` app command instead, behind `ensure_main_webview` like every other one, reached through the `DeckBridge` seam so fixture mode's no-op is structural.

**The stored level is applied Rust-side at launch**, in `run()`'s `setup` hook, before the webview has run any of our JavaScript — otherwise a user stored at 150% watches the app paint at 100% and jump. The frontend applies only on *change*. Note this is a deliberate **departure** from the appearance arrangement above, whose one effect applies on load *and* on change precisely so there is no second path to disagree with: zoom takes the split because it has a Rust-side option that removes a visible flash, and pays for it with the failure that split invites — if the Rust leg regresses, the window comes up at 100% while the Zoom row reads the stored level. `useZoom`'s applier effect deliberately records its first value without applying it, which is what stops the frontend fighting the launch apply. If the flash turns out to be imperceptible, collapsing to the appearance shape is the better design.

### The zoom keys are claimed in the capture phase, and that is load-bearing

**Do not move this listener to the bubble phase, and do not fold it into `App.tsx`'s handler.** xterm binds its own `keydown` on `.xterm-helper-textarea` — focused whenever an agent pane has focus, which is most of the time — and its key evaluation maps `Ctrl` plus a single-character key with `keyCode >= 48` to that literal character, and `Ctrl _` to 0x1F (undo in readline and emacs). `-` is keyCode 189, `=` is 187 and `0` is 48. In the bubble phase a window-level listener runs *after* the target's own handlers, so every zoom keystroke would have zoomed **and** written a byte into the focused agent's PTY, and `preventDefault()` cannot retract a sent byte.

`useZoom` therefore registers on `window` with `capture: true` and calls both `preventDefault()` and `stopPropagation()`, so xterm never sees the event.

**Where it is called from is load-bearing too, for a different reason.** `useZoom` is called in `DeckShell`, above the view switch, because that is the only component mounted for every screen. It was first called inside the deck — which `DeckShell` renders *instead of* the overview, not around it — so the zoom keys were dead on the Overview screen while working everywhere else. Note the symptom this produced, because it is easy to misread: the *level already applied* stayed applied there, since a webview zoom is a property of the webview rather than of anything React renders. Only the keys stopped responding. A screen added later inherits zoom by sitting under `DeckShell`; one that renders its own settings state instead of taking `DeckShell`'s would re-create the bug. Two things make this worth a paragraph rather than a comment. First, the failure is **platform-split**: the macOS binding uses `metaKey`, which xterm's branch excludes, so a Mac reviewer sees nothing wrong and only Linux and Windows type into the terminal. Second, `App.tsx`'s handler returns early on `editing`, which is true for `.xterm-helper-textarea` — so folding zoom into it would *also* break zoom in the app's normal state. The guard is `claims the keys before a descendant listener can see them` in `desktop/src/hooks/useZoom.test.ts`; if that test goes red, the zoom keys are typing into somebody's agent.

### Three rates, and the one that was already right

A zoom change does three things and each is rate-limited differently, because each costs something different.

| What | Rate | Why |
| --- | --- | --- |
| `set_zoom` on the webview | every change, unthrottled | it is what the user is waiting to see |
| `fit()` on every mounted pane | one pass per animation frame | `fit()` reads layout, so a held key would force one reflow per pane per key repeat |
| the write to `desktop.toml` | trailing flush every 400ms | each write is a temp-file-plus-rename |

The **daemon** resize is not in that table, and that is the point: each `fit()` reports through `TauriDeckBridge.resizeTerminal`, which already coalesces per agent on its own frame with a single-in-flight gate. Nothing about zoom touches it, and nothing should — a second layer there would reinvent something that works.

The persist is a *trailing flush* rather than a debounce, so a key held for ten seconds writes once every 400ms instead of writing nothing until it is released; a debounce loses the level entirely if the app dies mid-burst. `useZoom` also flushes a pending level on unmount, so the last change of a session survives.

### The re-fit is explicit, because relying on the observer is a trap

Each pane already re-fits from a `ResizeObserver`, and under page zoom that observer does fire — but **only because of the pane's width**, which descends from the viewport. Its height does not follow: `.agent-panel` is `min-height: 320px; height: 42vh; max-height: 560px`, and at the declared default window height of 920 the `42vh` term is 386px at 100% but 309px at 125%, so from 125% upward it is pinned at its floor and stops moving — six of the ten ladder steps. So "the box shrinks with the viewport" is half true, nothing in the code says the terminals depend on it, and jsdom has no layout engine to observe it either way. `TerminalViewport` therefore registers its `fit` with `terminalRegistry`, and a zoom change calls `refitAllTerminals()`; the observer stays as the backstop that catches the real post-layout geometry.

### What none of this proves

jsdom runs no layout engine, paints nothing and computes no geometry, and there is no driver-level or browser-level tier ([#823](https://github.com/vfarcic/dot-agent-deck/issues/823), [#836](https://github.com/vfarcic/dot-agent-deck/issues/836)). The tests cover the ladder, the key matching, the capture-phase interception, all three coalescers, the persistence round-trip and that a re-fit was requested. **They cannot show that anything got bigger.** Five claims rest on the manual check below and nothing else: that the webview actually scales, that `fit()` yields the right rows/cols afterwards, that the layout holds at 300% and 75%, that the terminal canvas re-renders crisply at the new device pixel ratio, and that the Rust launch apply happens at all.

## Appearance and theming

The app follows the OS light/dark appearance by default, with an in-app override — Light, Dark or System — in the settings sheet's Appearance section. `System` is the default and it **removes** the `data-theme` attribute rather than writing a value; the two are not interchangeable, because there is no `[data-theme="system"]` block and an absent attribute is what lets the media query decide, live and in both directions. The override is `desktop/src/lib/appearance.ts`, the panel is `desktop/src/components/AppearancePanel.tsx`, and the value persists in `[appearance]` in `desktop.toml`. PRD #743 has the design.

### The choice is on the first painted frame

An explicit choice is applied **before the webview parses the document**, not by the React effect that keeps it applied afterwards. `desktop/src-tauri/src/appearance.rs` reads the stored mode with `settings::load_snapshot()` — which is synchronous, so the answer exists before the window does — and registers it as a Tauri initialization script through a plugin's `js_init_script`. The script sets `data-theme` on the document root, and Tauri runs it after the global object is created and before the document is parsed, so the palette is already right when the page first paints.

**This is an ordering fix, and the two halves only work together** ([#845](https://github.com/vfarcic/dot-agent-deck/issues/845)). Without the injector the frontend cannot possibly be early enough: the document arrives over `invoke`, which is async, and until it does the root carries no attribute — which `styles.css` reads as "follow the OS", so a user who chose Dark on a light machine watched a light window turn dark. Without the frontend half the injector is silently undone: `App.tsx`'s appearance effect used to run on mount with the hook's unread placeholder, mode `system`, and applying System is a `removeAttribute` — indistinguishable from doing nothing on a fresh document, but a clobber on one the injector had already seeded.

The gate on that effect is `chosen`, not `loaded`, and the difference is load-bearing. `loaded` means the disk has replied; `chosen` means the mode is one somebody actually chose, which is **also** true of a click made while the initial read is still in flight — and nothing gates the appearance radios on `loaded`, so that click is reachable. A bare `if (!loaded) return` passes the clobber tests and breaks PRD #743's no-restart requirement; `desktop/src/App.test.tsx` pins both directions, and reverting the gate to `loaded` turns the control red.

A plugin rather than `WebviewWindowBuilder::initialization_script` is deliberate: the builder route needs `"create": false` in `tauri.conf.json` and window creation moved into `setup()`, and a window that fails to build is an app with **no window** — which nothing in CI would catch. `js_init_script` reaches every webview the app creates without changing how any of them are declared.

**What is verified and what is not.** The Rust tests assert the seam where the ordering is decided: that the plugin hands the webview the script for the stored mode, on the main frame, and that System yields no script at all. The vitest tests assert that mounting leaves a pre-seeded root alone until it has read a document that could replace it. Neither observes a paint — jsdom has no layout ([#836](https://github.com/vfarcic/dot-agent-deck/issues/836)) and no driver-level tier exists ([#823](https://github.com/vfarcic/dot-agent-deck/issues/823)) — so **that the first frame is actually correct is checked by the manual walk below and nowhere else.** Deleting the `.plugin(appearance::init())` line is caught, though not by a test: the module goes dead and `-D warnings` fails the build.

### Every colour is a token

`desktop/src/styles.css` opens with the palette on `:root` — 66 colour tokens plus three layout ones — and **no colour is written anywhere else**. That is not a style preference: a hex literal in a rule cannot follow a theme, so it stays light in dark mode, and the decay is invisible because nothing renders dark mode in CI. The families:

- **Light chrome** — surfaces (`--canvas`, `--paper`, `--paper-strong`, `--paper-sunken`, `--paper-sunken-strong`, `--skeleton`), text (`--ink`, `--ink-soft`, `--muted`, `--faint`, `--on-accent`), lines (`--line`, `--line-strong`, `--dialog-edge`).
- **Accents**, each a family at several depths: `--teal-*` (primary), `--green-*` (passed/done), `--amber-*` (waiting/caution), `--red-*` (failed/destructive), `--violet-*` (needs a human).
- **Live status** — `--status-ok`, `--status-warn`, `--status-error`. One family rather than two, because they are read against both the light chrome and the dark instrument chrome.
- **Instrument chrome** — `--terminal`, `--shell*`: the dark surfaces wrapped around the terminals (the rail, the tab strip, the agent footer, the output panels, the reader overlay). These stay dark in both appearances, so most of them keep their light value.
- **Channel forms** — `--scrim-rgb`, `--paper-rgb`, `--teal-rgb`, for colours also used at partial alpha. `rgb(var(--scrim-rgb) / .12)` keeps the alpha at the call site so one token serves every depth. `--paper-rgb` and `--teal-rgb` track the token they are named after; **`--scrim-rgb` tracks nothing** — its role is the nine scrims and drop shadows, and it goes to black in dark rather than following `--ink`, because a shadow cast in the near-white dark `--ink` would light the app up instead of darkening it. It was called `--ink-rgb` until that name invited exactly the wrong edit.

When you need a colour, use an existing token. When you genuinely need a new one, add it to `:root` **and** to both dark blocks; the guard below fails the build if you add it to one and not the others.

### The two dark blocks, and why both are needed

Light values stay on bare `:root`, so a document with no media-query support and no override renders the app exactly as it always did. The dark values are then declared **twice**, and neither copy is redundant:

- `@media (prefers-color-scheme: dark)` **under a `:root:not([data-theme="light"])` guard** — the OS default. The guard is what lets an explicit *Light* choice win on a dark machine; without it the media query would beat the attribute.
- `:root[data-theme="dark"]` — an explicit *Dark* choice, which has to win on a **light** machine, where the media query never fires at all.

Every colour token on `:root` is re-declared in both blocks, even where the dark value is deliberately identical to the light one — the terminals and the live-status colours are the worked examples. Re-stating them is what makes the dark block a complete list of decisions rather than a diff, and it is why the guard treats a missing token as an omission instead of an intentional carry-over.

`desktop/index.html` carries the other half: `<meta name="color-scheme" content="light dark">` is what makes the webview's **own** chrome — scrollbars, form controls, the default background behind the page — follow the appearance instead of staying light under a dark document, and `theme-color` needs one element per appearance, selected by a `media` attribute.

### The `theme-invariant:` opt-out

Any line carrying `theme-invariant: <reason>` in a comment is exempt from the colour guard, for that line only. Both qualifiers are enforced: the marker is read from the line's **comment text** and nowhere else, so a string literal quoting it (this documentation pasted into a component, say) exempts nothing, and a bare `theme-invariant:` with nothing after it is not an opt-out at all. It is not a way out of doing the work — it is for colour that is genuinely outside the theme. What qualifies today: the **xterm palette** in `.ts`/`.tsx`, because the terminals stay dark in both appearances (below), and colour arriving from another branch that has not been tokenised yet — PR #779 has light hexes in flight, so whichever of the two lands second needs a note rather than a wall. What does not qualify: "it's only a shadow", "it's only a border", "it's nearly the same in both themes". A shadow tuned for a light surface is invisible on a dark one, which makes it a theme colour in the way that matters. Always write the reason; the marker without one is a `TODO` wearing a disguise.

### The terminals stay dark in both appearances

`--terminal` and the `--shell*` family are not themed, and this is a decision rather than an omission. The panes are xterm.js fed raw PTY bytes: we control the background, the foreground and the 16 ANSI slots, but not what an agent emits into them. Agent CLIs pick colours for *their* user's terminal, and two of those choices break on a light background — dim greys tuned to read on black become near-invisible, and anything emitting truecolor SGR (`\e[38;2;r;g;b m`) bypasses the 16-slot palette entirely, so remapping cannot rescue it. The failure would land on the one surface people actually work in, and it is not one we can prevent from here. So the chrome around the panes themes normally and the pane stays a dark inset — which is what it already looks like in light mode. A "terminal follows the app appearance" setting was considered and deferred: it is cheap to add to a section that now exists, and shipping it means shipping a control whose other position nobody has measured against real agent output.

### The two guards

Both live in `xtask/linkage-check/src/desktop_palette.rs` and both run in `cargo test-fast` (via `--workspace`) and in the CI `build` job, which is one of the four **required** checks. That placement is deliberate: a vitest guard would run in `desktop-web`, which is advisory and can be merged past. Run them alone with `cargo test -p xtask-linkage-check desktop_palette`.

**The colour guard** scans every source file under `desktop/src` and fails on a hard-coded colour outside the palette: hex literals (`#rgb`, `#rgba`, `#rrggbb`, `#rrggbbaa`), the colour functions `rgb() rgba() hsl() hsla() hwb() lab() lch() oklab() oklch() color()` **unless** their arguments name a token, and the bare keywords `white` and `black`. `transparent`, `currentColor` and `inherit` are never flagged. Two things may hold a literal: a custom-property declaration inside a `:root` block of `desktop/src/styles.css` — that exact path, not any file merely *named* `styles.css`, because the palette is supposed to live in one place; the exemption is custom properties specifically, so `:root { color: #1d2522; }` still fails — and any line carrying the `theme-invariant:` marker in a comment, with a reason. **When it fires:** move the value onto a token (add one if no existing token has that role), or mark the line with a reason. Do not widen the guard.

**`check_dark_palette`** compares the three `:root` blocks and catches the ways the palette can go quietly wrong: the two dark blocks drifting apart (a token declared in one with a different value, or missing from the other), a light colour token with **no** dark counterpart, a **dark-only** token, which resolves to nothing in light mode, and either dark block sitting in the wrong place — the guarded one has to be **inside** the `@media (prefers-color-scheme: dark)` rule and the attribute one **outside** it. That last check is on byte ranges rather than on selector text, because a block dedented out of the `@media { … }` wrapper keeps its selector spelling and its values while applying them in *light* mode, and nothing textual can tell the difference. **When it fires:** it names each problem individually; the fix is always to make the three blocks agree, never to delete an assertion. Nothing else can see this — no test renders dark mode — so a token added to `:root` alone would otherwise ship as a colour that silently keeps its light value.

**Both guards fail closed**, which matters more than it sounds for a required check: every filesystem error — a directory that cannot be listed, an entry that cannot be typed, a file that cannot be read — fails the test rather than dropping those files from the scan, and a symlink under `desktop/src` is refused rather than followed (a directory link can leave the tree or loop back into it; a file link would be reported under a path it does not have). There is no symlink there today, so if you add one deliberately, `sources` has to be taught what it means.

**One false positive is worth knowing about in advance**, because the failure reads as a colour problem when it is a naming one: `#` followed by three hex digits inside a **string literal** is flagged, so `"…#803…"` and `"…PR #416…"` both trip the colour guard. Comments are masked before scanning; string contents deliberately are **not**, because `"#141817"` is exactly what the guard hunts. That is correct behaviour rather than a bug, and it will recur as more strings are written under `desktop/src`. The fix is to rewrite the string — `PRD 803`, no `#` — not to touch the guard and not to reach for the opt-out.

## Models and agent profiles

The fixture seeds an orchestrator and release profile using Claude and coder, reviewer, auditor, and tester profiles using `gpt-5.6-sol` with role-appropriate reasoning effort. Editing a profile stores a draft in the webview's local storage and **Confirm draft** does not execute anything or write `.dot-agent-deck.toml`; **Launch live loop** does execute each enabled profile's launch command through the daemon after the workflow validation described above. Treat launch commands as executable configuration, never store tokens in them, and review them before starting a live loop. **Reset defaults** clears the local draft.

For OpenAI, Anthropic, and OpenCode profiles, the launch command is generated from the current provider, CLI, model, reasoning-effort, and permission fields. The generated command is a read-only preview; on macOS and Linux, values are POSIX-quoted as individual shell words and invalid, NUL-containing, blank, or oversized commands block launch. An **advanced custom command override** is available for unusual CLIs, but it is explicitly labeled as an exact shell command that bypasses the structured fields. Its permissions are unmanaged by the profile UI, may be arbitrary, and must be encoded and reviewed in the command itself. Custom roles are excluded from structured full-access counts, and a custom override does not bypass the Windows platform guard. The launch confirmation distinguishes custom-command risk from permission claims made only for generated roles. The submitted command overrides the matching project role for that launch only; format-preserving profile write-back is not implemented.

Live sessions continue to use the commands and models that created them through the daemon. The current daemon snapshot exposes agent type but not a reliable model identifier, so the deck's live tiles still label model, cost, token and lease as unavailable instead of guessing. Two of that set have since moved: the branch chip and the attempt readouts are **gone** rather than labelled, because a fabricated `ATT 01` reads as data in a way an explicit "unavailable" does not (see [What it shows, and why not more](#what-it-shows-and-why-not-more)), and the write lease is genuinely reported on the [agent overview](#the-agent-overview), which reads it from `SessionSnapshot` rather than from the deck's fixture-shaped model.

## Voice control: the pipeline, the table, and what a row costs

PRD [#802](https://github.com/vfarcic/dot-agent-deck/issues/802) built voice control for this app, and its parts sit in four places on purpose: the **pipeline** and the **command table** are Rust-side under `desktop/src-tauri/src/voice/`, **dispatch** is frontend in `desktop/src/lib/voiceActions.ts` ([the next section](#the-voice-action-registry-and-what-its-guard-does-and-does-not-see)), the **surface** is `desktop/src/components/VoiceControlPanel.tsx` ([the one after that](#the-voice-surface-and-the-four-decisions-in-it-worth-knowing-before-you-edit-it)), and the **settings** are a `[voice]` section plus, for the backends that need one, a key in the OS keychain.

This section is what you need in order to **add a voice command without reading the PRD**. The short version: implement the feature as normal, add a row to `commands.toml`, flip its registry entry to `voice: true`, and update the tests that pin the shipped row set by value.

**Why voice is in the desktop app and not the TUI.** It needs a microphone, OS integration and a persistent local process — awkward through `ssh -t`, natural in a native app. Together with the fleet view it is what gives this app a job the TUI structurally cannot do.

### The pipeline, end to end

Six stages. The fifth is the one that keeps voice from becoming a second control surface.

| # | stage | where | what happens |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| 1 | capture | `voice/capture.rs` | the microphone, Rust-side, segmented into utterances by `Vad` |
| 2 | transcribe | `voice/transcribe.rs` | PCM in, a `Transcript` out, over the `Transcriber` seam |
| 3 | resolve | `voice/remote.rs`, `voice/openai.rs` | **one** model call carrying the whole command table, the live agent list and the transcript, over the `IntentResolver` seam |
| 4 | validate | `voice/outcome.rs`, `voice/table.rs` | the action exists, is callable on the declared screen, and every param resolves against live state |
| 5 | execute | `desktop/src/lib/voiceActions.ts` | the resolved action is handed to the handler a **click** uses |
| 6 | report | `voice/outcome.rs` | the app renders the sentence, from the table |

**Capture is Rust-side, and that decision propagates.** Of the three webviews this app ships on, `wry 0.55.1` grants microphone capture on exactly one: it answers WKWebView's permission callback with an unconditional `Grant` on macOS, leaves the kind entirely to WebView2's defaults on Windows, and on Linux **never sets `enable-media-stream` and connects no `permission-request` signal at all** — so webview-side `getUserMedia` there has no path to being granted without an upstream change. A capture path in the webview would therefore have worked on one platform and been dead on another, which is why the device is opened in Rust instead; `desktop_voice_stop` transcribes there and returns text. No audio buffer ever crosses into the webview. The CSP pushes the same way for every network hop: `connect-src` in `desktop/src-tauri/tauri.conf.json` names `ipc:` and `http://ipc.localhost` and nothing else, so **transcription, intent resolution and any future model download are Rust-side by construction**, not by preference.

**A device whose sample format this build cannot read is refused at OPEN, by name.** `cpal::SampleFormat` declares twelve PCM encodings plus a DSD trio, and PR #1163 found four of the twelve — `I24`, `U24`, `I64`, `U64` — falling through an empty match arm in the callback: the device opened, recorded, and delivered an empty buffer, so the user was told *the microphone heard nothing* with nothing to act on. 24-bit is ordinary on real interfaces. All twelve now convert (the two 24-bit ones through `DeviceSample` impls on `cpal`'s own newtypes, since there is no Rust primitive for a 24-bit sample and converting through a temporary `Vec` would allocate per callback). The DSD trio is **refused**: it is a 1-bit sigma-delta stream rather than PCM, and turning it into samples needs a decimation filter. `conversion_for` is one match with two consumers — `open_device` asks it before touching a device, `feed` asks it per callback — because two lists would drift, and the drift is silent in the one direction that matters. `SampleFormat` is `#[non_exhaustive]`, so a variant a later `cpal` adds is refused at open rather than dropped per callback: if you add a conversion, add it there and both halves move together.

**One open microphone becomes a sequence of utterances in `voice/capture.rs`, and that is what makes continuous voice control possible.** `Vad` is deliberately the simplest thing that can *end* an utterance: an RMS threshold (`SPEECH_FLOOR`, 600 out of `i16::MAX`, about -35 dBFS) measured over 20 ms frames, plus a quiet that has run for `SILENCE_HOLD` (800 ms). It is fed every output sample as `PcmSink` produces it, carries a partial frame between device callbacks, and latches once it fires so a late callback cannot un-end a finished utterance. `CaptureSession::status` then reports `CaptureState::Done` while the recording is still open and the audio still there, which is the boundary the webview polls for.

Three things it is not, each stated because the unqualified version would be wrong. It is **not** `Pcm16::is_silent`, which looks at a finished buffer and decides only whether a device delivered anything at all; this one runs on the data path and answers a question with a time in it. It is **not** a speech/noise classifier — a steady loud noise reads as speech and holds the utterance open, and what bounds *that* is `MAX_UTTERANCE` and the surface's discard. And it does **not** end an utterance nobody started: quiet before the first speech is ignored, so a microphone open in a quiet room ends at the cap rather than here. The threshold errs **high** on purpose, because the two directions do not cost the same — set too low it never accumulates a hold in a room with any noise in it, so every command runs into the cap and is lost, while set too high it can only end an utterance after 800 ms of below-threshold audio, which is a pause somebody took rather than a gap between words. Nine unit tests drive it over stubbed audio, as the rest of that module is driven; none opens a device.

**A segment is only transcribed if somebody actually spoke in it, and `Pcm16::is_silent` was never that test.** `MIN_SPEECH` (120 ms) inside a `SPEECH_WINDOW` (200 ms) is: the most speech-level audio `Pcm16::measure_speech` finds in any one sliding window of the finished buffer, measured with `SPEECH_FLOOR` and `VAD_FRAME` exactly as `Vad` measures the live signal, and `handle_audio` refuses anything under it before calling any backend. What made this necessary is that `Vad::heard_speech` flips on **one** frame over the floor and then ends the segment 800 ms later, so a keyboard tap in a quiet room produces a segment — and `is_silent`'s `all()` needs *every* sample under `SILENCE_FLOOR`, so that one tap made thirty seconds of room tone eligible to send. Whisper-family models are trained on captioned video and emit their training artefacts on near-silence, so PRD #802's product owner was shown `Heard: "Don't forget to subscribe"` about words nobody had said. **Density over a window** is the discriminating half: a 20 ms RMS window dilutes an impulse to one frame, so a typist at 100 ms between keystrokes gets two frames — 40 ms — into any window, while a word reaches 120 ms even when a stop closure splits it. The threshold errs **low** on purpose, the opposite direction from `SPEECH_FLOOR`'s, because these two fail differently — refusing a command somebody said is indistinguishable to the user from the feature being broken, while a junk sentence is recoverable in one glance. It is a threshold on the **audio**: nothing reads what a model returned, and no list of known hallucinated phrases exists anywhere in the module. `HttpTranscriber::run` asks the same question as a backstop for a caller that goes straight to `Transcriber::transcribe`.

**The window rung was 0 ms until [#802's real-microphone report](https://github.com/vfarcic/dot-agent-deck/issues/802), and the gate is what refused speech.** The rule used to be an *unbroken* run of `MIN_SPEECH`, which reads as reasonable and is the wrong axis: what separates typing from speech is **density**, not run length, and **speech contains real silence inside single words**. A voiceless stop is a closure — *"next"* is an 80 ms vowel, a 40 ms silence and a 60 ms /st/ cluster — so it holds 140 ms of speech-level audio and never 120 ms of it consecutively. Measured against this module's own detector before the fix, three ordinary shapes were refused: that *"next"*, a quiet speaker's vowel with a 20 ms dip through it, and a *"back"* with both its closures. So an utterance armed by one frame and closed by `SILENCE_HOLD` was then classified as silence by a rule five times stricter, and the user was told *"Nothing was said"* about a word they had said. The two numbers no longer differ by 6× for no stated reason: there is **one** question — *is this 20 ms frame at speech level?*, which is `SPEECH_FLOOR` — and a ladder of how much quiet each larger thing tolerates. Inside a **word**, 80 ms (`SPEECH_WINDOW − MIN_SPEECH`), which is a stop closure. Inside an **utterance**, 800 ms (`SILENCE_HOLD`), which is a pause somebody took. Beyond that, `MAX_UTTERANCE`, which is a cap and not a judgement. `Vad::heard_speech`'s single frame is deliberately on no rung of that ladder and is **not** a claim that anybody spoke: it exists to start the silence clock, and it is the most permissive test in the module because arming on a stricter rule would hold the microphone open to `MAX_UTTERANCE` through a segment of nothing but typing instead of closing it in a second. `Vad::speaking` and `Pcm16::has_speech` are the **same** predicate — same floor, same frame alignment, same window span — one incremental and one a pass over the buffer, and `voice_capture_the_live_and_finished_rules_are_one_predicate` is what holds the two implementations together.

**The refusal is its own outcome and it now carries a measurement.** `TranscriptionOutcome::Silent` is a fourth kind beside `heard`, `not_configured` and `failed`, and it used to carry no `detail` "because there is nothing to diagnose" — which was true of the quiet-room case it was written for and false of the one that mattered. A user who *did* speak reads that row, so it has to separate the three things it can mean, and it branches on `SpeechMeasure`'s two fields: if `peak_rms` never reached `SPEECH_FLOOR`, nothing in the buffer counted as speech at any length — *Too quiet to transcribe — the loudest moment reached 410 where 600 counts as speech. Move closer or turn the input up; still listening.* If it did and `voiced` is still short, the audio was speech and there was not enough of it — *I did not hear enough to transcribe — 80 ms of speech inside the loudest 200 ms, where 120 ms is needed. Say that again; still listening.* Neither reads as a failure and neither blames the device, which is the rule that has not changed; what changed is that the sentence names a number the reader can act on instead of telling somebody who spoke that they had not. **That measurement is also the field diagnostic**: a single refusal now says whether the microphone is quiet, the floor is too high for it, or the gate is too strict — a question that otherwise costs a round trip to the person holding the microphone, because `SPEECH_FLOOR` is the one constant here that no tier in this repository can calibrate.

**Releasing the device is blocking work and is done off the lock.** `AudioStream::drop` joins the device thread, so it is as slow as the platform's teardown. All three paths that release one — `stop`, `cancel` and the length cap's substitution — move the stream out from under the session mutex and drop it after the guard, and all three Tauri commands hand the call to `spawn_blocking`, the treatment the device *open* already had. Holding the mutex across a driver teardown parked every concurrent `status`, `cancel` and `start` behind it, and doing it on a runtime task parked a worker as well. The same rule covers the keychain, which is why `secrets::load_off_runtime` exists: `SecretStore::load` may put an unlock prompt on screen, and both voice backends read it from inside an `async fn`.

**Steps 5 and 6 are the load-bearing pair.** Voice gets no execution path of its own — a `VoiceOutcome::Dispatch` names an `invoke` target in the frontend registry and the webview dispatches it exactly where a click does — and the **app** renders every user-facing sentence, from `report` and `unavailable_hint` in the table plus fixed prose in `outcome.rs`. The model writes no prose a user reads. The one deliberate exception is the **transcript**, which is shown verbatim on a no-match: seeing exactly what was heard is what turns a mis-transcription into a correction, and `outcome.rs` asserts the exception alongside the rule so a later sweep cannot tidy it away. Every *other* foreign string in a sentence — a model-supplied param, a daemon-supplied agent label, a backend's failure detail — goes through `safe_message`.

**One tool with an `action` enum, and the full list every time.** Every command is sent on every request, each annotated `callable: true|false` computed from its `screens` against the current screen, rather than the list being filtered. Filtering produces a bad failure: asking to open an agent from a screen where that is impossible would yield "I don't know how to do that", which is false. The truthful answer names the prerequisite, which is what `unavailable_hint` is for. The trade, because it is easy to forget: **filtering prevents wrong calls; flagging explains them.** Flagging relies entirely on stage 4 to refuse an action the model called anyway — the flag improves the message, not the misfire rate.

### The command table, and its three consumers

`desktop/src-tauri/src/voice/commands.toml`, embedded with `include_str!` so it ships inside the binary and cannot drift from the build that compiled it. It is in the Rust crate because two of its three consumers are Rust-side and the third reaches it through an existing IPC reply rather than through a second copy.

1. **Schema generation** (`voice/schema.rs`, `voice/prompt.rs`, `voice/remote.rs`) — `id`, `description` and `params` become the model's tool definition.
2. **Validation** (`voice/table.rs`, `voice/outcome.rs`) — `screens` and each param `kind` check the model's answer before anything runs.
3. **Dispatch** (`desktop/src/lib/voiceActions.ts`) — `invoke` names an existing registry entry, and `report` is the sentence shown afterwards.

**`description` is a PROMPT, not documentation.** The model picks on it. Write it for a model and review it as an interface — it is the only thing separating one command from another at the moment of choice, and no user ever reads it. Two rows in the shipped table (`open_deck` and `close_agent_view`) are both asked for by a bare "go back", and **listing the phrase on both was measured to be not enough**: with each description merely naming "go back" among the utterances it serves, a real-model run answered `none` on the overview rather than falling through to the row that was callable. Each row now states what a bare "go back" means **under its own precondition** — "on the agent overview", "while an agent's pane is open" — and the word *bare* is load-bearing, because "go back to settings" has to keep reaching `open_settings`. That still leans on `screens`: no envelope carries a screen name, only the per-row `callable` flag, so the model learns which precondition holds by reading which row is callable. The prose tells it what to do with that signal; the signal is still `screens`, which means **a change to either row's `screens` column is a change to how those two are disambiguated**.

**`report` is the success sentence and is named `report` on purpose.** Without it, "the app renders every sentence" would be lost — refusals have `unavailable_hint` and fixed prose to draw on and success had nowhere, so the frontend would have had to invent success wording. A `{param}` placeholder is replaced with what that param resolved to, and a placeholder naming no declared param is refused at parse time. Do not rename it to `confirmation`: [D5](../../prds/802-desktop-voice-control.md#milestones) is *destructive commands behind confirmation*, so rows in this same file will later carry a genuine confirm-before-acting flag, and the two are close to opposite — a report describes a completed action, a confirmation blocks one before it happens.

**The shipped slice WAS navigation-only and is not any more**, which is worth saying in the sentence that used to claim it rather than leaving the claim to rot. `voice_off` stops the microphone, `list_commands` opens an overlay, and `dictate_to_agent` aims the microphone at one agent and types what it hears into that agent's terminal. What still holds is the narrower property the old sentence was protecting: **no row starts an agent, stops one, or answers a permission prompt**, which is why none of them needs the confirm-before-acting step [D5](#milestones) is about. `close_agent_view` closes a **view** and not a terminal pane — `closeAgent` in `App.tsx`, which is `setView({ kind: from })` and nothing else — and its `description` says so in as many words, because the destructive operation is one word away from it in speech.

**Three rows carry no `screens` column, which means callable everywhere, and that is a requirement rather than laziness.** Stopping must never be unavailable; neither must the phrase that lists what can be said; and aiming the microphone at an agent must work from the screen where the user is already watching one. Such a row's `unavailable_hint` is the one column nothing renders — `Unavailable` is produced only by a row the current screen cannot run — and `voice_table_rows_callable_everywhere_are_the_deliberate_set` pins which rows are in that position so a fourth is a decision somebody made.

### What adding a row costs, measured rather than asserted

PRD #802's acceptance criterion is that **adding a command changes no implementation** — one row and one classification flip — **plus the test assertions that pin the shipped row set by value**.

It was originally written as "a ONE-FILE change". That count was wrong. PRD #802's M8 added `open_settings` and measured **seven** files:

| # | file | what changed | implementation? |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| 1 | `desktop/src-tauri/src/voice/commands.toml` | the row | the row itself |
| 2 | `desktop/src/lib/voiceActions.ts` | `no_voice` → `voice: true` | the classification flip |
| 3 | `desktop/src-tauri/src/voice/table.rs` | 2 pinned-row test assertions | no |
| 4 | `desktop/src-tauri/src/voice/schema.rs` | 3 pinned-row test assertions | no |
| 5 | `desktop/src-tauri/src/voice/prompt.rs` | 3 pinned-row test assertions | no |
| 6 | `desktop/src-tauri/src/voice/remote.rs` | 1 pinned-row test assertion | no |
| 7 | `xtask/linkage-check/src/voice_command_registry.rs` | 1 guard-test plant re-pointed | no |

**Five of the seven are test-only, and every Rust hunk is inside a `mod tests`.** Nine unit tests pin the shipped row set **by value** — the action enum in the generated schema, the CLI prompt, the remote request body, the per-screen `callable` flags, and the table's own row list. That is a deliberate choice `table.rs` documents: the shape of the shipped table is pinned rather than described, because a test that merely described the shape would pass while the table said something else. So the real cost is **one edit per place the row set is written down**, and that is what those tests are *for*.

Two practical consequences when you add your own row:

- **Expect `cargo test-fast` to go red in five files with value mismatches, and read that as the pinning working.** Each failure names the expected and actual row set; add your row to each. None of it is plumbing left undone and none of it surfaces at runtime.
- **The seventh file is conditional and usually will not apply to you.** Rule 13's `overtaken` planted-bad-input test names a specific registry entry that has *no* row; give that entry a row and the plant has to move to another unspoken entry. It only bites when the entry you are voice-enabling happens to be the one the plant names.

**Scope the promise correctly.** It covers *commands*, which is the case that grows per feature. Activation modes, modal dictation and the destructive-confirmation flow are plumbing — built once, not extended per feature — so "one more costs a row and no implementation" is true for commands and not for new *interaction modes*, which should be rare.

#### Four more rows, measured the same way, and what they cost

M8's `open_settings` was a *navigation* row whose behaviour already existed behind a rail button. The four added after it are the cases that measure the claim where it is under strain, and the answer is not one number.

| row | files beyond the seven M8 measured | why |
| --- | --- | --- |
| `voice_off` | **+2** — `App.tsx`, `VoiceControlPanel.tsx` | the behaviour belongs to the **voice surface**, which is not a screen, so there was nowhere for it to be published from |
