| `list_commands` | **+6** — a Tauri command, `bridge.ts`, `types.ts`, `useDeckRuntime.ts`, the panel, `styles.css` | the overlay needs the annotated table in the **webview**, and nothing sent it there |
| `dictate_to_agent` | **+3 beyond `voice_off`'s** — the panel's whole dictation mode, plus a `speech` flag through `voice/capture.rs` and the status DTO | it is an interaction **mode**, not a command |
| the empty report row | not a row at all | static text in the panel |

**Read the first column as the honest measure, and read the pattern rather than the totals.** A row exposing behaviour a click already reaches still costs the row, the flip and the pinned assertions — M8's number held. A row exposing behaviour that belongs to the *voice surface itself* costs a publishing seam, once: `VoicePanelChannel` is `DeckSurface`'s `voiceChannel` mechanism pointed the other way round the screen switch, and the second and third such rows reused it for one line each. A row that needs data the webview does not have costs an IPC verb. And a row that changes what an utterance *means* is not a command at all.

**So the sentence to carry forward is narrower than M8's and truer:** adding a *command* changes no implementation; adding a *capability the voice surface owns* costs a seam the first time; adding an *interaction mode* is a feature. PRD #802 said the third of these in advance and it was right to.

#### Dictation, which is the one row that is a mode

`dictate_to_agent` aims the microphone at one agent. While aimed, an utterance is **not resolved against the table at all** — it is typed into that agent's prompt through `sendTerminalInput`, the same path a keystroke takes, rather than `submit_text`, which types *and* submits in one call and would make the words invisible until they were already gone.

Four properties, each of which is the reason a piece of it exists:

- **The words go somewhere VISIBLE.** The row opens the agent's pane as well as aiming at it, because on the overview no terminal is mounted at all and a command that typed into a terminal nobody could see would be the hidden buffer this feature is defined against.
- **It never submits silently.** After `VOICE_DICTATION_SEND_MS` with nobody speaking it presses Enter — behind a countdown that is on screen for every one of those seconds and is cancelled by any speech.
- **What cancels it is `CaptureStatus::speech`**, a flag added for this: the capture session's latched answer to *has anybody spoken since this recording opened*, computed on the device thread by the same detector that finds utterance boundaries. Nothing else on that status could answer it — `captured_ms` counts audio and grows in a silent room, and `Done` arrives only after `SILENCE_HOLD` past the **end** of a sentence, which for a long one is well after a five-second countdown would have fired.
- **Two phrases are matched in the panel rather than by the model**, and this is the one place in the product where spoken words are not owned by the command table. Check 13 is what keeps that second place from drifting: every phrase in `VOICE_OFF_PHRASES` must appear in the `voice_off` row's `description` — a phrase the panel matches and the model was never told about would stop dictation and *not* be a command outside it — and the two lists must stay **disjoint**, because the precedence below says the order decides nothing *because* they are. The check runs one way only: a phrasing in the description that the panel does not match is a real inconsistency too, but the description is a prompt rather than a list and there is nothing to enumerate it from. The exit phrase and `voice off` are compared for exact equality against the whole normalised transcript — never as a substring, so *"we should stop dictation of the log"* is typed rather than obeyed. Running the resolver over every dictated sentence would cost a model call per sentence and would reintroduce the false positive the phrase's distinctiveness exists to remove: a model asked *"was that the exit?"* can be wrong, where `===` cannot. **`voice off` is checked first**, deliberately: the two lists share no phrase, so the order decides nothing today, but the only reading that cannot leave a live microphone after a user asked for it to stop is the bigger stop.

**A known residual, stated rather than discovered.** Nothing checks *before* aiming that the target agent's pane accepts input. An agent holding a read lease, with no live target, or already finished is unwritable (`terminalInput.ts`), and dictating at one fails on the first utterance: the write rejects, the report shows the rejection and dictation ends. Loud rather than silent, but later than it could be — the check would need the agent snapshot, which the panel does not take.

**Why the `invoke` column is stringly typed rather than a Rust enum the compiler checks.** A typed enum would mean editing the table *and* a variant *and* a match arm, and the last two are implementation — exactly what the criterion forbids. Reference-plus-guard buys the same safety at commit time, which is the trade `tests/CATALOG.md` already makes. Check 13 is what makes it a trade rather than a hope.

### The two intent backends, the one transcription backend, and the settings that choose them

The `[voice]` section has **three keys, two of which are tables**. `activation` is still one closed token; `transcription` and `intent` each became `{ backend, endpoint, model }` in PRD #802's provider work, because a user cannot know which key to paste when the endpoint is a secret of the build's. The backend token is still a closed enum with a folding deserializer in the `AppearanceMode` idiom — an unknown token folds to the default rather than failing the document, because a file written by a newer build may name a backend this one has never heard of, while an **over-length** token is a malformed document and is an error. A document written before the tables existed supplies a bare string where a table is expected, and `StageSpec` reads both shapes so the whole document is not lost over a stale voice token.

| field | backend tokens | default | what it is |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| `transcription` | `local`, `remote` | **`local`** | `local` is a speech container on loopback at `127.0.0.1:18000` (`Systran/faster-whisper-tiny.en`), which sends no credential at all; `remote` is hosted `whisper-1` at `api.openai.com`, and needs an OpenAI key |
| `intent` | `anthropic`, `openai_compatible` | **`openai_compatible`** | which **wire protocol** the one HTTPS call speaks. `openai_compatible` is `/v1/chat/completions` with a nested `json_schema`, preset to `api.openai.com` on `gpt-5-mini` with `reasoning_effort: minimal`; `anthropic` is Messages with tool-use at `api.anthropic.com` on `claude-haiku-4-5` |
| `intent.max_tokens` | an integer, 64–32768 | **`4096`** | how much answer one command may cost, **reasoning included**. `max_tokens` on the Anthropic wire and `max_completion_tokens` on the OpenAI one — one knob, two spellings. It is a ceiling and not a reservation: it costs nothing unless it is used |
| `activation` | `toggle` | `toggle` | press once to start, press once to stop. The only mode today |

**Speech needs no key and Commands does, and the asymmetry is a measurement rather than an oversight.** A keyless speech container answers in a **0.653 s median** warm, so the honest default for that stage is one that works on the day the app is installed with the audio never leaving the machine. Local *intent* was measured twice against the same phrase fixtures and is **not** viable today: a 1.5B chat model scored 20/24 — turning *"what time is it?"* into `open_agent`, which is the refusal that is most of voice's safety — and a 34 MB embedding classifier held two false positives no threshold repairs (*"start the tester"* at 0.668, above several valid commands). So Commands asks for a key, deliberately, and what the provider work buys is that the user chooses whose.

**ONE key runs the whole feature, and that is why Commands defaults to OpenAI.** Speech's hosted option is OpenAI's `whisper-1`, so while Commands defaulted to Anthropic a user who wanted voice hosted end to end needed two vendors, two accounts and two keys — for a feature whose pitch is that it works the moment you turn it on. The three shapes now available are: **one OpenAI key** for both stages; **an Anthropic key** for Commands if that is the provider they have, with Speech either keyless or on an OpenAI key; or **no key for Speech at all**, running the local container, which is still the default for that stage. Both hosted presets being one vendor is pinned by a test rather than left to the two constants drifting apart.

**The switch cost nothing measurable, which is the only reason it was available.** `gpt-5-mini` through the shipped builder and parser: 24/24 on the 24-fixture table at an **818 ms** median against `claude-haiku-4-5`'s 780 ms, at $0.0073 for the sweep. `reasoning_effort: minimal` is what buys that — provider-default reasoning also scored 24/24 but took 1,778 ms and $0.0161, spending 4,416 reasoning tokens for no score. Re-measured afterwards against the current **34**-fixture table, in the shipping configuration — which includes the tie-break sentence below, without which the OpenAI preset does **not** reach these numbers. Six runs on the OpenAI default: **34, 34, 32, 34, 34, 34**. Three on the Anthropic preset for comparison: **33, 34, 33**, both failures being `open-agent-by-state`, its own long-recorded wobble. Read the two as level rather than as one beating the other; a single fixture's coin-flip is what separates them, and it is a different fixture for each.

**An earlier sweep scored 18/24 and that was OUR defect, not the model's.** Six fixtures had spent exactly 256 completion tokens — the whole of the hardwired ceiling, all of it reasoning — and returned `finish_reason: "length"` with nothing written. That is what made `intent.max_tokens` a field; see the row above, and note the defect was never specific to this model or this provider. It made the generic `openai_compatible` path unusable with **any** reasoning model whose reasoning is not suppressed.

**`reasoning_effort` is sent with the preset and with nothing else.** It is an OpenAI-family parameter, not part of the `/v1/chat/completions` shape every server implementing that path accepts — a different one may refuse an unknown field outright (the `llama.cpp` server probed for PRD #802 wanted `chat_template_kwargs` instead), and even `api.openai.com` refuses it for models that do not reason. `IntentSettings::reasoning_effort` is the gate and answers `Some` only when the endpoint **and** the model are both still this build's preset, so every edit a user can make — another provider, a gateway in front of OpenAI, a server on loopback, a different model at the same endpoint — takes it off and gets the provider's own default reasoning instead. That is the safe direction to be wrong in *because* the ceiling is 4096; at 256 it was not.

**One sentence in `TOOL_INSTRUCTIONS` is load-bearing for the switch and was added for it.** A bare *"go back"* fits `close_agent_view` on the agent screen and `open_deck` on the overview, and the prompt conveys which screen you are on only through each row's `callable` flag — while also saying that picking a `callable: false` action is right when that is what was asked for. `claude-haiku-4-5` read the descriptions' screen qualifiers; `gpt-5-mini` did not, and answered `open_deck` on the agent screen in **seven of eight** measured runs — one fixture, failing systematically, which is not what the 24-fixture table above could have shown. The fix is a tie-break rather than a rewrite: *when the words fit more than one action and only one is `callable: true`, pick that one*. Editing the two rows' descriptions to point at each other was tried first and merely moved the failure to the other fixture, which is the same outcome the 2026-09-20 prompt-variant sweep recorded.

**The agent-CLI intent backend is GONE, and both of `intent`'s tokens name a protocol because of it.** It shipped as the default — `claude -p`, spawning the pre-authenticated CLI already on the box, so the app needed no key and nothing downloaded — and PRD #802's provider work removed it. Two reasons. First, the product one: once Commands needs a credential at all, the app cannot assume everyone uses the provider it picked, and a *subprocess* has no endpoint, model or key to choose. Second, it was the most security-expensive code in the feature: the prompt is built partly from untrusted input, so containing a general-purpose coding agent took seven CLI flags, absolute-path resolution, an app-owned working directory, an allowlisted environment, process-group teardown and a `Drop` guard — all of it tracking another program's flag set. `opencode` had already been withdrawn on that ground, for having no equivalent flags. A user's stale `intent = "claude"` costs one re-pick: it folds to the default and the rest of the document survives, as does `"opencode"`. **The pre-provider-work `"remote"` is mapped explicitly to `anthropic` rather than folded**, and that stopped being a nicety when the default moved: it named the Anthropic API, so its user's stored `SecretId::VoiceIntent` is an Anthropic key, and folding it to today's default would put that key in an `Authorization` header addressed to `api.openai.com`.

**The command token names a PROTOCOL, not a transport and not a vendor.** Both go over the same client to whatever `ServiceUrl` the user named; what differs is the request body and where the answer is read from. `voice/remote.rs` owns the transport and the Anthropic dialect, `voice/openai.rs` the OpenAI-compatible one, and `Protocol` selects between them. The labels name the provider because that is the word on the key a user has to paste.

**One thing about the OpenAI-compatible envelope is a trap and is written into the code.** `llama.cpp` also accepts `response_format: { type: "json_schema", schema: {…} }` — the schema hoisted a level, no wrapper and no `strict` — and that form returns **HTTP 200 and is silently not enforced**. Only the nested `{ type: "json_schema", json_schema: { name, strict, schema } }` is. `voice_openai_request_uses_the_nested_json_schema_envelope` asserts the schema is *not* at the hoisted key, rather than merely asserting one is present.

**That protocol's schema differs from the tool-use one in a way that reaches `IntentAnswer`.** OpenAI's strict structured outputs refuse a declared property that is not in `required`, so `params` cannot be left optional: it enumerates every param the table declares, requires all of them, and types each as `["string", "null"]`. The model is therefore obliged to name a param the chosen action does not take. `IntentAnswer`'s field deserializer drops those nulls, so it arrives downstream as absent and becomes `ParamMissing` exactly as the other protocol's answer does.

**A local model is reachable and is not recommended.** With Commands on HTTP, pointing `endpoint` at a server on this machine is the same code path, and a **loopback endpoint asks for no key at all** — `RemoteResolver::run` never consults the keychain for one, which is the same rule Speech has reached from the other side. There is no preset for it and no label offering it, for the measurements above.

**The latency is reported rather than hidden.** Every `VoiceResult` carries the milliseconds it cost beside the backend's name, because the mitigation for *slow enough to feel broken* is to show the number; the keyed backend's median is ~0.9 s against the deleted agent CLI's 3.1–4.7 s. Both protocols constrain the answer to the table's enum — Anthropic through tool-use with `strict: true`, OpenAI-compatible through a nested `json_schema` with `strict: true` — so an action outside the set is unrepresentable rather than merely unlikely. A **new** strict schema pays a one-time ~0.9 s compilation cached for 24 hours, so the user-visible shape is one slower utterance after you edit `commands.toml`.

### The credential, and the `SecretStore` seam

**No credential is in `desktop.toml` and none is in `localStorage`.** PRD #803 named the `SecretStore` seam and deliberately did not build it; #802 M4 built it, in `desktop/src-tauri/src/secrets.rs` — store, load, delete, keyed by a `SecretId`, over the OS keychain through `keyring`. Two ids exist: `voice-intent` and `voice-transcription`. Those tokens are **stable**; changing one strands a key a user already stored under the old name, where nothing reads it and nothing offers to delete it.

**The guard is what forced it, and going red is the mechanism working.** `xtask/linkage-check/src/desktop_settings_secrets.rs` pins every settings field's type to `ALLOWED_FIELD_TYPES`, and `String` is deliberately not on that list. Adding the `[voice]` section turned that check red at four fields; the resolution was closed enums for the three choices and the keychain for the key, which is why the document holds no field for a credential at all. It holds no *boolean* either, although the rule would allow one — the panel asks the store, so the answer cannot go stale against the keychain.

**The value is never read back.** There is no `loadSecret` on the settings bridge and there must not be: a stored key arriving in the webview is one `JSON.stringify` from the `localStorage` half of the rule. The input starts empty every time and the panel says *A key is stored* rather than showing a masked preview, because a preview is an information channel out of the keychain whose only consumer is reassurance. A failed save **rejects** rather than resolving with `stored: false`, and the panel renders that sentence and keeps what was typed — the outcome being designed against is a user who thinks their key is stored and finds voice broken tomorrow.

**A Linux box with no Secret Service is a documented, non-silent failure.** `keyring` has nowhere to put the key there, and the panel says so rather than reporting a save that did not happen.

### What is NOT verified, on any platform

Green checks cover none of the following, and a reader should not infer otherwise. There is no `tauri-driver` and no WebDriver session in this repository ([#953](https://github.com/vfarcic/dot-agent-deck/issues/953)), so **nothing automated drives the real Tauri window**, and the Rust unit tests run capture against a stubbed device.

1. **That the OS actually grants the microphone, on any platform.** The permission dialogs are reached by nothing automated. The macOS `NSMicrophoneUsageDescription` entry lives in the bundle overlay (`desktop/src-tauri/tauri.bundle.conf.json`) and `Info.plist`, and `build-macos` is the only job that will ever touch either. Whether a `tauri dev` window needs it to record, or only a packaged `.app`, is [PRD #802's Open Question 2](../../prds/802-desktop-voice-control.md#open-questions) and needs a macOS host.
2. **That a real utterance transcribes end to end.** Every transcription test is against a stub or a recorded fixture.
3. ~~**That the `openai_compatible` command protocol works against OpenAI itself.**~~ **VERIFIED 2026-09-20**, and it had to be: that protocol on `api.openai.com` with `gpt-5-mini` is now the shipping default, so the phrase fixtures run against it on every credentialed run. Six runs in the shipping configuration on the day of the switch, 32/34 to 34/34, plus a dozen more while the tie-break sentence was being settled. The shape was already verified against two other servers — a local `llama.cpp` in PRD #802's reconnaissance, and Anthropic's own OpenAI-compatible endpoint, where a probe confirmed the nested `json_schema` envelope is genuinely grammar-enforced: an `action` enum holding one bogus token came back holding that token, over the answer the model plainly wanted to give. What stays unverified about it is what stays unverified about every entry in this list: no CI job runs it, so it is verified exactly as often as a developer runs it.

   (Two different third items have stood here before — *"that the agent-CLI intent backend resolves from an app launched with a Finder-shaped PATH"*, whose backend is gone, and the unverified-preset note this one replaces.)

The phrase fixtures (`desktop/src-tauri/src/voice/phrase_fixtures.toml`, driven by `desktop/src-tauri/tests/voice_phrase_fixtures.rs`) map utterances to expected actions against a real backend. They are opt-in, skipped when no backend is configured, and **run in no CI job** — CLAUDE.md rule 5's lane 2 in the desktop crate's own idiom. Run them yourself when you change the table, the prompt or a backend, because nothing else will.

### Adding a voice command: the checklist

1. **Implement the capability as normal**, reachable by click, and make sure it dispatches through `VOICE_ACTIONS` rather than a bare `onClick` — [the next section](#the-voice-action-registry-and-what-its-guard-does-and-does-not-see) is why, and what the guard cannot see.
2. **Add the row** to `commands.toml`: `id`, a `description` written for a model, `invoke` naming the registry entry, `screens` listing only the screens where **a click can already reach it**, an `unavailable_hint` naming the prerequisite, a `report` sentence, and a `[[commands.params]]` block per param.
3. **Flip the registry entry** from `no_voice: "<reason>"` to `voice: true`, and declare the context members its `run` needs in `needs`.
4. **Run `cargo test-fast`** and add your row to each pinned-row assertion that fails. Expect about five.
5. **Run `cargo xtask linkage-check`** — check 13 is what catches a typo in `invoke`, an unknown screen, an unknown param `kind`, or an entry nobody classified.
6. **Add a phrase fixture** and run it against a real backend yourself.

**If the behaviour belongs to the voice surface rather than to a screen** — the microphone, the report row, anything the panel owns — publish it through `VoicePanelChannel` instead of `deckVoiceContext`. `VoiceScreenContext` is `Omit<VoiceActionContext, keyof VoicePanelContext>`, so the two halves are complements by construction: a member cannot end up served twice or not at all, and putting one on the wrong side is a type error rather than a runtime refusal.

**Do not widen `screens` beyond what a click can reach.** M8's row is the worked example: `open_settings` is `["deck"]` and not `["deck", "overview"]` because the Settings overlay is reachable only from the deck rail, and listing `overview` would let voice open a surface no click can reach from that screen. That is voice becoming a second control surface with capabilities the first lacks, which is the one thing this design forbids — **a row does not define behaviour, it exposes behaviour that already exists**.

## The voice action registry, and what its guard does and does not see

PRD #802 is building voice control for this app, and the piece to understand first is **`desktop/src/lib/voiceActions.ts`**, because it is what the command table's `invoke` column points at. The surface that drives it — the panel, the microphone toggle, the report — is the next section. The credentialed phrase fixtures belong to that PRD's M10 and are not documented here yet.

**`VOICE_ACTIONS` is the app's dispatch seam for the rail and the command palette, not a list beside it.** Every rail button, every palette entry, the agent tile's open/close pair and the overview's row open all run through `VOICE_ACTIONS[id].run(context, params)`. That is deliberate and it is the whole point: the voice command table's `invoke` column names an entry here rather than a `#[tauri::command]`, because none of the registered Tauri commands performs a navigation — navigation in this app is React state. If a control dispatched its own `setState` beside the registry, `invoke` would be pointing at a list rather than at the app.

**Voice dispatches through `dispatchVoiceAction`, in the same file, and there is deliberately no `switch` over action ids anywhere.** It looks the outcome's `invoke` up in `VOICE_ACTIONS` and calls the same `run`, with one target object every entry reads the members it declared from. A `switch` would type-check and would be a second list of the ids — and it would be *implementation* that every new command has to edit, which is what PRD #802's acceptance criterion forbids. [What adding a row actually costs](#what-adding-a-row-costs-measured-rather-than-asserted) is measured below and it is not one file. It returns `false` for an `invoke` naming no entry — unreachable, since check 13 fails the build on one — so that the surface can say the command did not run instead of reporting a navigation nothing performed.

**Each entry is classified.** Either `voice: true`, which means a row in `desktop/src-tauri/src/voice/commands.toml` names it, or a written `no_voice` reason saying what about that capability makes it a poor fit for a spoken command. A reason is read by a person, so "not yet" is not one — write what a later reader would need in order to disagree with you.

**Check 13 in `cargo xtask linkage-check` is what keeps the two files honest.** It asserts that every `invoke` names a registry key, that every `screens` entry is a `kind` of `DeckView` in `desktop/src/types.ts`, that every param `kind` is a `ParamKind` from `voice/table.rs`, that every entry is classified, and that the registry literal stays statically readable — a spread or a computed key among its entries is refused, since the keys are read as text. `xtask/linkage-check/src/voice_command_registry.rs` holds the rule and its planted-bad-input tests, one per assertion, so "no findings" means something.

**What it does NOT see, said here as well as at the failure itself.** The check proves every registry **entry** is classified. It does **not** prove the registry is **complete**. A control wired with a bare `onClick` that never reaches `VOICE_ACTIONS` is invisible to it, and there were 80 `onClick=` sites in non-test `.tsx` under `desktop/src` when PRD #802 was written, against 7 static palette entries and 7 rail buttons. Most of the ones sampled are chrome — dismiss a toast, close a sheet — and the check could not tell a capability from a close button anyway, so the residue is not claimed to be harmless. Read a green check 13 as "the table and the registry agree", never as "nobody forgot a capability". The one mechanical approximation to the second claim is pinning a count of interactive controls, which that PRD's Open Question 3 recommends against for now with its churn cost named.


### The voice surface, and the four decisions in it worth knowing before you edit it

PRD #802 M6 added `desktop/src/components/VoiceControlPanel.tsx`: a fixed **Voice** button, and the transient report beside it. There is no dialog and no text box. Pressing the button turns continuous voice control **on**; pressing it again turns it off; in between, each utterance is segmented by the voice-activity detection in `voice/capture.rs`, transcribed, resolved, run and reported, and the microphone opens again for the next one without another press.

**The typed path was withdrawn at the product owner's direction, and the withdrawal is the reason the rest of this section reads as it does.** The shipped M6 opened a dialog with a `Command` text box and a *Run command* button, and offered the microphone only once transcription was configured — a build-time decision rather than anything issue #802 asked for. The requirement it was replaced with is: *"Only voice. When I click the Voice button, it should activate voice control and it should keep being controlled by voice until the button is clicked again. The button should indicate whether voice is currently on or off. If voice control does not work, it should show instructions how to enable it instead."* That is closer to what the PRD's own default activation mode already said, and it is why the VAD deferred to D4 came forward into M6.

**The button carries its state twice, and both are required.** `Voice on` / `Voice off` as visible text, and a matching `aria-pressed`, because the two audiences read different things and a toggle that showed one without the other would be on for only one of them. `desktop/e2e/voice-control.spec.ts` pins both against the real app in two browsers.

**The button is the ONLY indication the microphone is open, which is what makes a wrong one a privacy defect rather than a cosmetic one** — and PR #1163's security audit found a state in which it lied. A fresh panel initialises its toggle to *off*, and a mount is not always the first one: a hard webview reload, a crashed web-content process, or a webview the platform destroyed and recreated all produce a panel with no memory in front of a `CaptureSession` that may still be recording. So the panel had nothing to reconcile against, and rendered `Voice off` over a live device — with no way out, because the press that looks like it turns voice on calls `desktop_voice_start`, which `CaptureState::accepts_start` refuses while a recording is running. Past the 30-second cap it is worse: `cap_reached` releases the device on purpose but keeps the audio, so up to 960 KB of captured speech sat in the session with nothing able to reach it. Two halves fix it, and the first is the one that matters.

**The RELEASE is Rust's, at four teardown triggers, because a React passive effect making an asynchronous IPC call is not a lifetime.** A webview that is going away is not guaranteed to run the cleanup, let alone to let the call finish. `release_microphone` in `desktop/src-tauri/src/lib.rs` is called from: the main window's `CloseRequested` and `Destroyed` (`window_ends_capture` — both, because a vetoed close never reaches the second and a real one reaches it after the window is unusable); `on_page_load` with `PageLoadEvent::Started`, which is the document being replaced without the window going anywhere; `on_web_content_process_terminate`, **on macOS and iOS only** — tauri exposes no WebKitGTK equivalent, so a Linux web-process crash is released by whichever other trigger arrives first; and `release_on_exit`, where it runs **before** the terminal detach and the tunnel close, since those are bounded at 250 ms *per session* and a microphone should not stay open through a teardown that has nothing to do with it. `cancel` is idempotent and never refused, so every one of them is unconditional. It is also synchronous on the calling thread — `CpalStream::drop` joins the device thread — and that is deliberate: a release that is *scheduled* rather than done is a release that may not happen before the process does.

**The panel's half is a mount RECONCILE, and a third button state to go with it.** On mount it asks `voiceStatus` once and cancels anything Rust is still holding (`recording` or `transcribing` — the two states `accepts_start` refuses) before it claims the device is closed. Until that answer arrives the button reads `Voice…` with `aria-pressed="mixed"`, ARIA's own value for a toggle whose state is not settled, because `false` there is the same claim the reconcile exists to stop making. A press during the round trip supersedes the reconcile and does the same reconciliation itself, so the user is never waiting on it. A `pagehide` listener cancels too, and is explicitly defence in depth rather than the mechanism — it is the same asynchronous IPC on a document that is going away.

**A reconcile whose RELEASE is refused does not go on to claim the device is closed**, which is the same audit's finding on the same surface and was the one place the paragraph below did not hold. The reconcile swallowed the rejection and settled the button to `Voice off` with a crossed-out microphone over a session Rust had just declined to let go of — on the surface with the least information of any of them, a fresh panel with no press to report against. It now takes the same route `turnOff` takes: re-query, and on anything other than *the device is genuinely gone*, render the not-released presentation and put `VOICE_RELEASE_REFUSED` in the report. `turnOn`'s own reconcile did the same thing one step less obviously — it fell through to `desktop_voice_start`, which `accepts_start` then refused, so the press ended at `off` with an error sentence beside it — and takes the same route now. **The press is the retry on both**: the click handler routes `unreleased` to the release rather than to the start, whether or not the toggle was ever on.

**Turning voice off AWAITS the release, which is the same audit's should-fix and the same defect at a shorter timescale.** `turnOff` used to call `setOn(false)` and then fire `voiceCancel` without awaiting it, swallowing any rejection — so the button read `Voice off` before Rust had acknowledged anything, and permanently if the call failed. That is not a scheduling instant: `desktop_voice_cancel` runs the teardown on a blocking thread and `CpalStream::drop` joins the device thread, which is exactly why the three concurrency regression tests in `voice/capture.rs` drive a stub stream whose `Drop` blocks. So there is now a fourth button state: while the release is in flight it reads `Voice stopping…` with `aria-busy`, and a press meanwhile is **serialised** — ignored rather than racing the answer or starting a recording over a device Rust has not let go of. A press in any other state is live, `unreleased` included, because that press is the retry.

**A rejected release never reads as `off`, at any of the three places a release is attempted** — `turnOff`, `turnOn`'s reconcile and the mount reconcile, which all go through `releasedAfterRefusal`. The panel re-queries `voiceStatus`: if Rust reports the session idle, the call failed but the device is genuinely gone, so `off` is truthful and the rejection is still reported. Anything else — a held session, a status read that also failed, a runtime with no `voiceStatus` at all — keeps the button saying the microphone may be open and puts `VOICE_RELEASE_REFUSED` in the report beside the rejection's own sentence, naming the retry. **Every unknown about a RELEASE resolves to *not released***, because this is the one place where guessing in the direction that suits the button would be the whole defect.

That sentence used to be written one quantifier wider — *every unknown resolves to not released* — and PR #1163's review was right that it was false of two paths (both now closed, above). The narrower version is the one that is actually upheld, and the residual it leaves out is worth naming rather than re-absorbing: a **mount reconcile whose `voiceStatus` read itself fails** attempted no release and has no evidence a session exists, and it settles the button to `off` rather than leaving it at `Voice…` for ever. That is a guess, in the direction this surface is otherwise careful about — and the alternative is a permanent `aria-busy` `Voice…` claiming a round trip that is not in flight, which is its own false statement to a screen reader. `turnOn` reconciles again at the next press, which is what bounds it. If that trade is ever revisited, the thing to change is the *indicator*, not the reconcile: a fourth word for *nobody has been able to ask* would let the panel stop guessing without claiming to be busy.

**A press with no usable transcription backend shows instructions rather than failing quietly**, and the button is neither hidden nor disabled — a greyed-out control shows nothing and reads as a fault. The press reports `VOICE_UNAVAILABLE`, which names *Settings → Voice* rather than advising the user to "configure a backend". (This paragraph used to open with *"`off` is the default and a product statement"*, which the keyless-speech work made false twice over: the default is `local`, and the `off` token is gone. What a user can still meet is a `local` endpoint with no container behind it, which `voice::transcribe`'s unreachable sentence answers by naming the `docker run` rather than a setting.) Availability is read **at the press**, not cached from mount, so choosing a backend and pressing again works without a restart.

**It renders sentences; it never composes one.** Every situation an utterance can end in — a dispatch, a command the screen cannot run, a no-match, a param that resolved to nothing or to more than one thing, a backend that failed — arrives from Rust carrying its own `sentence`, rendered from the command table. The panel reads `outcome.kind` for exactly one decision, whether to dispatch, and prints `outcome.sentence` for everything else. It never builds wording out of `action`, `param`, `matches` or `detail`. **Six** sentences are written frontend-side (it said *two*, and was already three short before this list grew). `NOTHING_DISPATCHED` and `SCREEN_MOVED_ON` are for situations Rust structurally cannot see; `VOICE_UNAVAILABLE`, `VOICE_CAP_DISCARDED`, `VOICE_RELEASE_REFUSED` and `VOICE_EMPTY_STATE` are about the surface's own state — a microphone it has nothing to open, an utterance it threw away, a release it cannot vouch for, a row with nothing in it yet — rather than about an utterance. The dictation countdown is a seventh and the only one built around a value from elsewhere: nothing Rust-side knows a send is pending, so no table column could carry it, and a countdown that did not name whose prompt it was about to submit to would leave the one question a user needs answered before it reaches zero. `NOTHING_DISPATCHED` is for a dispatch whose `invoke` names no registry entry — the entry lives in TypeScript, and check 13 already fails the build on one that resolves to nothing. `SCREEN_MOVED_ON` is for an outcome that arrives after the user has walked to another screen: the classification was made against the screen declared with the utterance, so running it on the screen they are standing on now would act with the wrong screen's permission (see *The screen is declared* below). Every string does cross `displayText` on the way to the DOM, at the `message` budget: a sentence embeds a transcript, and a transcript is model- or microphone-supplied text with control and bidi codepoints still in it. That sanitises and bounds; it does not reword.

**It is mounted ABOVE the deck/overview screen switch, and that is architectural.** `DeckShell` returns one fragment on both paths — the screen at index 0, the panel at index 1 — so React keeps the panel's fiber while the screen below it is replaced. The report describes a navigation that has just happened and the Undo beside it reverses one, so both have to outlive the screen change they are about; mounted inside either screen, a successful `open_overview` would unmount the panel that was explaining it. If you restructure that return, keep the panel at a stable position: a fragment on one path and a bare `DeckSurface` on the other is a different root type and remounts everything under it.

**The browser fixture is what replaced the typed path's coverage argument.** The typed box was defended as the path every user has with no credential, and therefore as the only one anybody could test. That does not survive its own premise: a path nobody speaks through exercises the resolver and never the surface. `FixtureDeckBridge` now simulates a microphone the same way it simulates a daemon — with a transcription backend chosen in its settings, a start is accepted, the next status poll reports the utterance over, and a stop returns one canned transcript from `FIXTURE_VOICE_COMMANDS`. So the whole segment → transcribe → resolve → execute → listen-again cycle is driven in Playwright with no device and no key anywhere near it. It speaks **once per activation** and is then quiet, latched by `voiceCancel`: a stand-in that spoke every quarter second would overwrite its own report before anyone could read it, and a person who says one command and stops is the behaviour being previewed anyway.

**The screen is declared, not passed.** `resolveVoice(utterance)` takes the utterance and nothing else; the mounted screen reaches the bridge through `declareVoiceScreen(screen)`, called synchronously immediately before each resolve. The reason is that an utterance is resolved against live state read where that state already lives — the agent list Rust-side from the selected deck's own snapshot, the command table from the binary it is embedded in — and the mounted screen is the one piece that exists *only* in the webview, as a `useState<DeckView>`. Declaring it from an effect instead would let it lag a navigation and validate the next utterance against the screen the user just left.

**And the screen is re-checked when the answer comes back, which is a second thing and not the same one.** Declaring it at submit time fixes what the utterance is judged against; it does nothing about the user moving while the answer is being worked out — PRD #802 measured 0.653 s for speech and 0.62–1.03 s for commands, and 4.3–6.3 s for the whole pipeline before the agent-CLI intent backend was withdrawn. An outcome resolved against the overview and dispatched on the deck runs a command the overview authorised on a screen that never did, and PR #1163 found it doing exactly that: *"open settings"* spoken on the overview opened the Settings sheet on the deck. The surface compares the screen at submit with the screen at answer and renders `SCREEN_MOVED_ON` rather than dispatching. **The same reservation covers voice being turned off mid-flight**, which is the sharper half of one finding: abandoning used to invalidate nothing, so the continuation still called `onDispatch` and the app navigated seconds after the user stopped Voice. A `request` counter is claimed by each in-flight cycle and bumped by `turnOff` and by unmount, and every continuation asks whether it is still current before it writes state or dispatches. The claim is taken in `takeUtterance` rather than in `resolveOne` — transcription is the *first* await, so a cycle abandoned during it would otherwise reach the resolve through a step that claimed the surface afresh, which is an abandoned request laundering itself into a live one.

**The 30-second cap DISCARDS the segment, and does not transcribe it.** With `voice::Vad` ending an utterance after `voice::SILENCE_HOLD` of quiet, reaching `voice::MAX_UTTERANCE` means no boundary was ever found — continuous speech, a conversation in the room, a call, steady noise. In a navigation vocabulary where every command is one to four words, that is *definitionally* not a command, so transcribing it would spend a paid transcription call and then a paid intent call to arrive at a no-match, once every thirty seconds for as long as the microphone is open. The surface calls `voiceCancel`, reports `VOICE_CAP_DISCARDED`, and keeps listening. The earlier design offered **Send the recording** or **Discard it**; those buttons lived on a panel that no longer exists, and auto-transcribing was the wrong thing to replace them with.

**The webview learns an utterance ended by polling, and the interval was chosen with the latency in hand.** `voice::Vad` decides the speaking has stopped on the device thread, and `CaptureSession::status` reports it as `state: "done"` while the audio is still there to be taken; a poll that comes back `done` is the boundary the stop → transcribe → resolve → start cycle hangs off, and `capped` is checked first because a capped segment is both and the two answers differ. A Tauri event emitted from the capture side would carry the boundary with no wait at all — it would also be a new IPC verb in each of the three bridges plus a listener seam, for a saving bounded by the interval, so `VOICE_STATUS_POLL_MS` was cut from one second to **250 ms** instead. That puts the polling tax at 125 ms of mean added latency. **The pipeline it was weighed against has since got much faster** — that 4.3–6.3 s was the agent-CLI intent backend, which PRD #802's provider work withdrew, and the measurements left are 0.653 s for speech and 0.62–1.03 s for commands — so the tax is nearer a tenth of the wait than the under-3% it was, and about a sixth of `SILENCE_HOLD`'s 800 ms, which remains the term that dominates the end of an utterance. The cost is four state reads a second while the microphone is open, each an in-memory read of the capture session plus the small settings document, touching no device. The event path stays available if the hold ever gets short enough for this to matter.

**The cycle is serial, and that is a stated trade rather than an oversight.** The microphone is shut while the backends are working rather than recording over them. Overlapping would let a second command resolve against a screen the first is still changing, and the `SCREEN_MOVED_ON` guard would then be refusing the user's own sentences. The cost is that speech during those seconds is not captured, which is why the report says what it is doing — *Turning that into text…*, then *Working out what that means…* — instead of continuing to claim it is listening.

**A dispatch the host cannot serve is refused, not attempted.** `DeckShell` is the only component mounted for every screen, so the voice dispatch is built there; but five registry entries reach `useState` booleans that live inside `DeckSurface`. `DeckSurface` publishes the context it can serve upward through a `VoiceContextChannel` — a mutable slot the shell holds, republished on every commit because the context closes over that render's setters, and cleared on unmount — and the shell merges it in, putting its own `navigate` and `closeAgentView` last so those two mean the same thing whether or not a deck is mounted. `dispatchVoiceAction` then checks each entry's declared `needs` **before** calling it: an entry the host cannot serve answers `false`, which the surface reports as `NOTHING_DISPATCHED`. Checking rather than catching is the point — M6 did neither, and an overlay dispatch from the overview threw `TypeError: context.openOverlay is not a function`, which the report then rendered at the user. A caught message would break the "the app renders every sentence" property exactly as the thrown one did. `needs` is read at runtime and the `Pick<VoiceActionContext, …>` on `run` is not, so a `NeedsCoversRun` mapped type proves at compile time that the list covers what `run` requires; it deliberately tolerates an over-declared list, which only makes dispatch stricter.

**The Undo window is ten seconds**, `VOICE_UNDO_WINDOW_MS`. Long enough to react to a navigation you did not mean, short enough that it never becomes a second permanent navigation control in the report — the deck's own controls are what "go back in general" means, and this affordance is only about undoing *that* command. The destination is read off the view at dispatch time rather than popped from a stack, which is the same choice `closeAgent` makes and for the same reason: there is no history to be wrong about. It is **offered** only where a view actually moved: `open_settings` puts an overlay over the deck and leaves the view where it was, so the shell wraps the two view-moving context members and offers the Undo only if one was called. An object with no `undo` is the existing contract for *it ran and there is nothing to reverse*, which `onDispatch` already distinguishes from `undefined`.

**The voice surface is a PEER of the agent pane, not background, and it took an exemption in the modal fence to make that true.** M6 shipped with the opposite: while a pane is open `useInertBackground` marked every element that is not one of its ancestors `inert`, and the voice surface is the screen's sibling, so it was marked with the rest of the background. `inert` removes an element from hit testing as well as from the tab order, so the trigger was not merely untabbable but unclickable — measured in the browser tier, where the click was intercepted by the pane's own footer — and `close_agent_view`, the only row whose `screens` column is `["agent"]`, could not be spoken in a shipped build. The second of the two fixes named here was taken: the hook now skips elements carrying `VOICE_PEER_PROPS`, which the trigger and its report spread, and nothing else. It is an allow-list of one marker rather than a rule about roles or dialogs, so a second peer has to be exempted there deliberately. **The exempt elements are no longer a dialog and the exemption did not widen when they stopped being one** — nor when the surface became a reserved row and the count went from two marked elements to **one**. That is the exemption narrowing rather than moving: the row is one marked sibling holding the trigger and the report, so the walk skips the same subtree it skipped before, and the Undo inside the report is still exempt because it is a control. What the fence still guarantees is unchanged for everything else, the `DeckSelector` above all, and `AgentPaneDeckIdentity.test.tsx` states it as an equality against that one trigger rather than as an emptiness — so any *other* control escaping an open pane is still a failure.

**The surface is ONE RESERVED ROW at the bottom of the window, and that is a layout decision the product owner made after driving the real app.** Until PR #1163's review round the trigger and the report were two independently fixed boxes at z-index 90, the report stacked above the button and growing upward as it filled — so it was drawn over whatever was beneath it, including over the enlarged agent pane, which is the one place the user is actually working. Moving the boxes buys a different collision rather than none: a floating box's height is its content's, and no other element can set room aside for something whose size it cannot know. A row can. `--voice-row-height` (40px) is the contract, and every surface that would otherwise reach the window's bottom edge subtracts it — `.deck-main` on both screens, `.agent-pane-overlay`, `.reader-overlay`, `.evidence-drawer` and `.toast`, each marked *voice row* at its own rule. The row is z-index **95**: above the agent pane (90) and the reader overlay (80), below `.dialog-backdrop` (100). Two exclusions are deliberate rather than missed. A **modal dialog or sheet still covers the row**, which is correct — the voice surface is not exempted from `useInertBackground` for those the way it is for the agent pane, and shrinking their backdrops would leave a control clickable underneath a window-covering modal. And the **rail** is not on the list because it never overlaps: at desktop widths the row starts at the rail's right edge, and at `max-width: 680px`, where the rail becomes a 54px bottom bar, `--bottom-rail-height` lifts the row onto it. The agent pane keeps `inset: 0` — a modal scrim should cover the window — and it is its *content* box that stops above the row, via `padding-bottom`. The row's last report **persists** until the next utterance replaces it, which is now a decision rather than something inherited from the floating card: the space is reserved whether or not it holds anything, the pipeline goes straight back to listening, and a sentence that faded on a timer would be gone before a user who glanced away came back. `voice-control.spec.ts` asserts the geometry in both engines, including `elementFromPoint` at the row's own centre — a box comparison proves the pane's content stops above the row, and only a hit test proves a click there reaches the row rather than the scrim.

**The `aria-modal` question went away with the dialog rather than being answered.** The exemption had made the voice dialog's own `aria-modal="true"` a false claim — a dialog that deliberately coexists with another interactive dialog, and that inerts nothing itself, is not modal in the ARIA sense — and the correction at the time was to state `aria-modal="false"` and trap no focus. There is now no dialog at all: a button and a `role="status"` live region, which claim no modality, take no focus and trap none, so nothing has to be stated. The live region is mounted before any sentence arrives, which is what makes the announcement reliable, and the card inside it is what appears and disappears. The agent pane's own `aria-modal="true"` is untouched: it genuinely inerts everything but one named peer, which is the ordinary shape of a modal with a status region beside it.

### What the voice feature does and does not persist

PRD #802's Open Question 5 asked whether any part of an utterance is stored. The answer is in **three parts**, and the unqualified one-part version was written down and was false, which is why all three are spelled out here. Quote all three or none: the first on its own is the claim that was false, and the first two without the third read as covering a hosted endpoint that this app cannot speak for.

**This app persists nothing.** No transcript, utterance or audio buffer is written to a log or to disk by any code in `desktop/src-tauri/src/voice/`. `Transcript` and `Pcm16` both carry hand-written `Debug` impls that print a length and no content, so a derived `{:?}` on a container that holds one prints neither — which closes the careless route; the deliberate route is a rule this crate keeps. The IPC seam carries it one step earlier: `desktop_voice_stop` transcribes Rust-side and returns text, so no audio buffer ever crosses into the webview.

**A child process the app hands the prompt to was a different question, and this is where the first answer was wrong.** The `claude` intent backend passed the whole prompt — the utterance, and the agent labels from the daemon with it — to another application, and that application has storage of its own. It was writing a resumable session containing all of it; a redacted `Debug` impl in Rust does nothing about that. The backend was given `--no-session-persistence` along with the rest of its containment flags, and the `opencode` backend was withdrawn in the same change for having no equivalent one. **PRD #802's provider work then removed the agent-CLI backend outright**, so today no stage of this feature hands a prompt to a child process at all and the unqualified claim is true again. The two-part wording is kept as the rule a future backend inherits rather than has to rediscover — the last one cost an audit round to notice.

**A hosted backend's own retention is neither of those halves and is not something this app can assert at all.** The keyed remote intent and transcription backends upload to an endpoint; what that endpoint keeps is its policy.

## Current milestone limits

- The Tauri crate directly reuses the root library; the standalone protocol-crate extraction remains pending.
- **A remote deck is now the app's own feature, it HAS run against a second machine, and the half still missing is what the client rendered.** PRD #741 replaced the hand-rolled forwarded socket with a configured deck: an `Endpoint` whose kind is in the type, stored in `desktop.toml`, reached over an app-managed `ssh -N -L` child, chosen from the **Deck selector**, with a **Test connection** button that classifies twelve outcomes — [Decks](#decks-which-ones-the-app-talks-to-and-how-it-reaches-another-machine) is the whole of it. The thing that made remote *observation-only* is gone too: project resolution moved daemon-side in #819 M6, so the desktop sends back only a path the daemon supplied or the user typed. **The run on a second machine has now happened**, recorded in `prds/741-desktop-connect-any-daemon.md`'s Work Log for 2026-09-13: a desktop on a Mac drove a workflow launch against a deck on the Linux dev box over `ssh -L`, that daemon resolved a project there, published `orchestrator-context.md` there, and five roles started under it with real agents rather than stand-ins (`ClaudeCode` on two panes, `Codex` on two more). **What stayed unticked is the display half** — nobody looked at the connected window to confirm that the project header and the agent panes name `/home/vfarcic/…` and never `/Users/viktorfarcic/…`, which is the exact symptom the 2026-08-29 measurement reported. A daemon log proves what the daemon did and says nothing about what the client drew. So the mechanism is verified and the rendering is not; PRD #742's own two-deck run is what discharges the remainder. A loopback `ssh` is not a substitute for the **rendering** half — nothing local can say what the connected window drew — but it is no longer disqualified from the other two: PRD #742 M10 added `fleet/observe/002` for the transport and `project/resolve/002` for "whichever side resolved it", both in lane 1 and both explained under [Configuring a remote deck by hand](#configuring-a-remote-deck-by-hand).
- **The fleet view has never been seen with two real decks**, and four things about it are unverified in named ways rather than merely untested. **No automated tier reaches a real second daemon** — there is no `tauri-driver` ([#953](https://github.com/vfarcic/dot-agent-deck/issues/953)), so every fleet assertion in the repository is against fixtures. **That each watcher stamps its own endpoint on the emit is unguarded**: the payload *shape* is pinned by a key-set difference proving the stamp adds exactly `deck` and moves nothing, but attribution needs an `AppHandle`, which needs a running Tauri app — making the emit chain generic over `R: Runtime` is the lever that would close it. **`EndpointTunnels::acquire`'s lock has no test** and was fixed from the code, because the risk test injects its stall at `hello()`, which sits after `acquire`, and a local endpoint returns from `acquire` promptly. And **a watcher-teardown ordering residual**: `retain_watchers` aborts, so a watcher parked in its `select!` stops before its next emit while one already inside `snapshot_with` may have an emit in flight. Until the two-deck run exists and is recorded, read the fleet view as designed-for-N and measured-at-one.
- The deterministic loop, transition evidence, diffs, checks, artifacts, spend, and profile/workflow editors are preview data or local-only UI where the daemon does not expose structured data yet.
- **Voice control ships navigation-only, in one activation mode, and three of its properties are verified by nothing.** The five shipped rows open an agent, the overview, the deck, the settings sheet and close an agent view; nothing that starts, stops or approves is a row, and that is [D5](../../prds/802-desktop-voice-control.md#milestones) behind a confirmation rather than an oversight. Activation is press-on/press-off only, with voice-activity detection segmenting the utterances in between; hold-to-talk is [D4](../../prds/802-desktop-voice-control.md#milestones). Transcription needs **no key**, and its default is a speech container on loopback (`ghcr.io/speaches-ai/speaches`, `Systran/faster-whisper-tiny.en`, measured at a 0.653 s median warm) rather than the `off` this shipped with — **and since M6 was rewritten to voice only there is no typed fallback behind it**, so the prerequisite a user can still miss is the container, not a credential, and an endpoint nobody answers at says which `docker run` to type. Commands, by contrast, needs a key on both its presets — the agent-CLI backend that made it keyless by default is gone with PRD #802's provider work, because a stage that spends a credential has to let the user choose whose, and local intent was measured twice and is not viable; the only keyless Commands configuration left is an endpoint on loopback, which `RemoteResolver::run` never consults the keychain for. **That key is an OpenAI one by default**, which is Speech's hosted provider too, so going hosted end to end is one account rather than two. What no tier here proves is that the OS grants the microphone on any platform, or that a real utterance transcribes: see [what is NOT verified](#what-is-not-verified-on-any-platform), and read a green `desktop-web` or `desktop-browser` run as covering neither. (A third item stood here — that the `openai_compatible` protocol works against OpenAI itself — and the credentialed fixtures now run against exactly that, because it is the default.)
- Live orchestration graph events (`delegate`, `work-done`, and `dispatch`) and the graph view remain pending.
- OS-native notifications, signing, notarization, auto-update, remote/web hosting, and mobile layouts are out of the current spike. Packaging is no longer — see **Release bundles** above — but the bundles it produces are unsigned, so every OS still warns on first launch.
- Native Windows workflow-command construction is pending; the webview and Rust bridge both block desktop workflow launch on Windows rather than forwarding POSIX-quoted commands to `cmd.exe`.
- Pi coordinator launch is blocked in the desktop preview pending an acknowledged native seed-delivery protocol; Pi may still be used for non-start worker roles.
- Daemon startup is an explicit user action; visual rename and standalone start-agent flows are not exposed by the current frontend.
- Multi-pane throughput still needs the explicit M1.3 stress qualification, and a real-agent terminal session still needs the PRD's pre-release end-to-end gate.
- **The agent overview is not the landing screen, and has one drill-in rather than none.** The deck is still what the app opens on. PRD #1105 built #745's iteration-3 **single-agent view** — [the agent pane overlay](#the-agent-pane-overlay), reachable from an overview row and from a deck tile — so the overview's exits are now the deck and one agent's pane. The **group view** (the deck filtered to one tab bucket) and the **promotion to landing screen** are both untaken, and #745's iteration 3 is therefore still open. Promoting the overview with only one of its two destinations built would still land users somewhere they can barely leave.
- **The real Tauri window is still driven by nothing.** There is no `tauri-driver` and no WebDriver session anywhere in this repository, so real IPC, real xterm over a real PTY, and the distribution's own WebKitGTK or WKWebView build are exercised by the manual smoke check below and by nothing else. PRD #1105's PTY resize is the newest thing that lands on the wrong side of this line: the automated tiers observe the client's *proposed* geometry and stop there — see [What the automated tiers prove about the resize](#what-the-automated-tiers-prove-about-the-resize-and-what-they-do-not). What DOES run automatically, since issues [#823](https://github.com/vfarcic/dot-agent-deck/issues/823) and [#836](https://github.com/vfarcic/dot-agent-deck/issues/836), is a browser tier one rung below that: Playwright drives the production `pnpm build` bundle — served by `vite preview`, loaded through the PRD #745 fixture query strings, so no daemon and no credential are involved — in two engines, Chromium and Playwright's own WebKit build. See [The browser tier](#the-browser-tier) for what it asserts and what it does not.

## The browser tier

Issues [#823](https://github.com/vfarcic/dot-agent-deck/issues/823) and [#836](https://github.com/vfarcic/dot-agent-deck/issues/836) closed the same absence from two directions: nothing in this repository ran a layout engine over the desktop app. The vitest suite runs under `environment: "jsdom"`, and jsdom is a DOM implementation rather than a browser — it computes no geometry, performs no layout and paints nothing — so those tests can assert what React renders into a tree and nothing about what a reader sees. The tier under `desktop/e2e` is what closes that.

**What it drives.** Playwright's `webServer` runs `vite build` and then serves the resulting `desktop/dist` with `vite preview`, so every spec loads the same production bundle `pnpm build` produces: minified, built for the `safari15` target, with the real stylesheet. Not the dev server, and not the hand-built runtime the vitest tests render into. Each spec reaches its screen through the PRD #745 fixture query strings (`?fixture=1&state=crowded` and friends), so there is no daemon, no credential and no real agent anywhere in it.

**Which engines.** Two Playwright projects, `chromium` and `webkit`, both run on every invocation. The second is the point. Before this tier, every automated check of this app ran in Chromium or in jsdom while the app ships on WebKit — WebKitGTK under Tauri on Linux, WKWebView on macOS. Playwright's `webkit` is its own build from the WebKit source tree; the binary it launches on Linux is `minibrowser-gtk`, WebKit's GTK port. So it answers WebCore and JavaScriptCore questions the way WebKit answers them, which is a different thing from answering them the way *this machine's* WebKitGTK or *this Mac's* WKWebView would.

**What it asserts today.** **Sixteen** files. Every one of them makes at least one assertion jsdom structurally cannot, and the bullets say which parts of each are geometry, real event dispatch or real storage and which are ordinary DOM-shape checks that happen to live here because the screen they are about is only reachable in a browser. (This said *eight* and listed eight until PRD #742 M7 recounted it; `deck-selector.spec.ts` and `decks.spec.ts` arrived with PRD #741 and are the two it was missing. It then said *ten* while twelve existed. A count in a sentence goes stale every time a spec lands, so check it with `ls desktop/e2e/*.spec.ts | wc -l` rather than trusting this number; the enumeration below is the part that has to stay complete.)

- `overview-scroll.spec.ts` — with every column ticked at a 900px viewport, the overview's table really overflows; every group card resolves the shared grid template to the identical used track widths; each cell sits inside its own track; and scrolling `.overview-table-region` moves every card *and* the legend by the same amount. A card scrolling on its own is the failure this layout exists to prevent, and it only becomes reachable once the chosen column set is wide enough to overflow.
- `overview-rows.spec.ts` — every row is the same height whether its prompt is long or blank, and injecting the longest string the app can ever put in a prompt cell (`DISPLAY_LIMITS.prompt` plus the elision marker) changes neither the row's height nor its width. This is the assertion #836 records as unmakeable: an audit found a vitest test whose comment claimed to prove it, which jsdom cannot, and the comment was corrected rather than the test strengthened.
- `page-overflow.spec.ts` — at 1280x800 and at 900x800, on the deck and on the overview, nothing extends past the page body. `body { overflow-x: hidden }` is what makes getting this wrong invisible to the eye: content past the right edge is clipped rather than reachable, so a reader sees a truncated screen and no scrollbar saying why. `scrollWidth` still reports it.
- `appearance.spec.ts` — the FIRST of the two behaviours #823 names as the ones Chromium cannot answer for WebKit, and only the first. `<meta name="color-scheme" content="light dark">` actually puts the document's used colour scheme into dark under a dark preference, checked through the `Canvas` system colour with `only light` / `only dark` probes as controls, and the canvas repaints between the two appearances — both engine-side measurements. The `theme-color` half is **not** a measurement of engine behaviour, and the file says so at length: what an engine resolved for `theme-color` is not observable from inside the page, so that test asserts only that `index.html` carries one correctly `media`-scoped meta per appearance, that the engine's own `matchMedia` separates the two `media` strings in both directions, and that the dark meta's value still equals the dark `--canvas` it was copied from (a drift guard, read as a composited colour). Whether an engine honours `media` on `theme-color` is answered by nothing automated, and by no named step of the manual walk either — see [Both appearances, and the settings surface](#both-appearances-and-the-settings-surface) below for exactly what that leaves, and [#953](https://github.com/vfarcic/dot-agent-deck/issues/953) for the rung that could close it.
- `column-picker.spec.ts` — the picker driven the way a person drives it. Three of its six tests are here because no other tier can ask the question. The menu is measured: it lays out below its trigger, right-aligned to it, wholly inside the viewport, and extending past the top bar's own bottom edge — which is only possible because it is out of flow — and a hit test at its own centre lands inside it rather than on the fleet underneath, which is what `z-index: 30` buys. A real pointer press on an element elsewhere on the screen dismisses it, and the test first measures that its target is genuinely clear of the menu's box, because an assertion that turned out to be clicking *inside* the menu would fail for a reason that has nothing to do with the behaviour. And a second press on the trigger closes the menu rather than closing and reopening it: the dismiss listener runs on `pointerdown` and the trigger on `click`, so one press fires both in the engine's own order, and a trigger outside the picker's root would look dead. The other three are end-state checks — Escape dismisses; the name column's checkbox is checked and `disabled`, and unticking everything else leaves a one-column screen that still paints every one of the fifteen names at a non-zero width; Restore defaults returns both the legend and the menu's own ticks to the four the screen opens on.
- `column-persistence.spec.ts` — the item jsdom cannot reach at all, because there is no navigation to survive: the vitest suite mounts a component into a tree it already owns and unmounts it again. Here a chosen five-column set is written to real per-origin storage, a real reload tears the document down, and the fresh mount comes back on the same five with the engine resolving five used grid tracks. Then the scoping, asserted against the two literal key names: a fixture visit writes only `…overview-columns.v1.fixture`; a live visit — which in a plain browser has no Tauri bridge, so no fleet ever arrives, but the screen still mounts and still writes — wakes up on the defaults under `…v1.live` instead of inheriting the fixture layout, and leaves the fixture key untouched. Last, a stored value that cannot be parsed leaves the screen on its opening four rather than white: `readStoredColumns` parses on mount, so an unguarded throw there takes the whole app down before anything renders, and only a real reload of a real stored value can observe that.
- `connection-states.spec.ts` — the single-deck states put a different screen each in front of the reader, and `empty` is the one that used to be got wrong (it fell through to the disconnected branch, so a healthy fresh install was told its daemon was missing). Which note renders, which do not, what the daemon message says and what the AGENTS instrument prints are DOM-shape checks. Two things are not. Each screen is measured — a non-empty box, below the sticky top bar, inside the viewport — because a screen clipped to nothing is indistinguishable in the DOM from the right one. And the daemon lamp is read as a **composited colour** across the four single-deck states: it resolves through `--status-ok`, `--shell-faint` and `--status-error` under the dark appearance, so the comparison is the engine's answer rather than a class name. Connected and empty must paint the *same* colour — an idle daemon is not a fault — while disconnected and error must differ from it and from each other. PRD #742 M4 added partial connectivity on top: at `?fixture=1&state=fleet`, one deck down among two up must read as **one deck's** problem rather than the screen's — the healthy decks keep their rows and their pips while the silent one carries the degraded chrome — and the header must count the decks that answered and never the fleet the silent one was last seen running. M14 added a fourth deck to that scenario, one that has not reported yet, and the pair of assertions that matter are the ones the DOM tier cannot make: its note must not be the disconnected one, and its lamp must paint as neither a healthy deck's nor a downed deck's. The header reads `2/4`, and a build that dropped an unreported deck would read `2/3` and look exactly as correct.
- `overview-grouping.spec.ts` — the fifteen-agent fleet at `?fixture=1&state=crowded`, which PRD #745 built to make this hard: its agents are declared out of role order and orchestration `dot-ai`'s start role is deliberately its third, so grouping, ordering and coordinator identification are three separate things a screen has to get right rather than one accident of declaration order. Which agent lands in which of the four cards, in what order, and which row carries the COORDINATOR badge are DOM-shape. What only an engine answers is whether the reading order is the *seen* order: every card and every row is measured, and the assertions are that the boxes descend the page in the sequence the DOM lists them and that no two overlap. A `flex-direction: column-reverse` leaves every DOM assertion in that file green and puts the fleet on screen upside down — which is exactly how it was mutation-tested. The badge is measured too: it has a box, that box sits within its own row, and it follows the agent name in the cell rather than sitting on top of it. PRD #742 M4 added the fleet to the same file: at `?fixture=1&state=fleet` it renders one section per deck, in order, each holding only its own deck's rows — which the fixture makes a real question rather than a formality by giving the remote decks' agents the **same agent ids** as the local deck's, since ids are per-daemon monotonic values and a fixture that avoided the collision would pass every `(daemonId, agentId)` assertion for the wrong reason. It also states how many decks answered and counts only those, and stacks the whole fleet down a phone-width screen without sliding the page sideways.
- `deck-selector.spec.ts` — the Deck selector driven on **both shells**, the deck and the overview, since it sits in the top bar of each and a regression on one would be invisible from the other. DOM-shape: every configured deck is listed, All Decks leads, the local deck is the default, and switching names the chosen deck and survives a reload. Engine-only: the menu opens under its trigger, floats over the screen and toggles rather than closing-and-reopening on a second press — the same `pointerdown`/`click` ordering the column picker depends on — it stays wholly on screen at 900px with the page not sliding sideways open or closed, and it groups its options with a `<span>` rather than a `<legend>`, which is [the WebKit layout divergence](#adding-a-setting) that made the fieldset form unusable (issue #1032).
- `agent-pane-overlay.spec.ts` — PRD #1105's agent pane, from both origins, in both engines. Geometry: the pane's box really occupies the viewport, measured against `window.innerWidth`/`innerHeight` rather than merely being bigger than a tile. Engine-only beyond that: the screen underneath is still the **same DOM node** and still `isConnected` while the pane is up — a `.agent-grid` that was torn down and rebuilt would look identical to every DOM-shape query and would have cost five scrollback replays — and `Escape` returns to the screen the pane was opened from rather than to the app's default. The dialog's `role`, `aria-modal` and accessible name are ordinary DOM shape.
- `agent-pane-modal.spec.ts` — the pane's `aria-modal="true"` made true, and the only **automated** tier that can tell (the manual walk's step 8 is the other). The base screen stays mounted under the pane by design, so until PRD #1105's security audit every control on it — the rail, the tiles, and the `DeckSelector` that retargets the whole app — kept its place in the tab order behind a full-window dialog. The fix marks everything that is not an ancestor of the pane `inert`; the vitest tier asserts the attribute, and only a real engine's tab order can assert what the attribute DOES — measured, not assumed: in this repo's jsdom, `"inert" in HTMLElement.prototype` is `false` and `focus()` on a button under an inert ancestor still makes it `document.activeElement`. So this presses Tab twenty times and requires focus never to leave the pane **except onto the one peer the fence exempts, the Voice trigger** — matched by identity, so the deck selector, the rail and the other tiles all still fail it — checks the trigger is outside every `inert` while the selector is under one, and checks the selector is clickable again after `Escape`. PRD #802 narrowed that claim by exactly that one control: an exempt control is in the tab order as well as clickable, which is the accessible half of "reachable" rather than something to take back with a `tabindex`. Both engines, because `inert` is a comparatively recent platform feature and WebKit is the half of this pair that ships in the packaged app. It was mutation-tested by removing the hook: both engines go red.
- `agent-pane-narrow.spec.ts` — the phone-width trap, and the only spec below 900px. At 400x780 the pane keeps a visible terminal and a visible tab strip, both of which the tile's own `@media (max-width: 680px)` rules hide. jsdom evaluates no media query, so this claim is not assertable in the vitest tier at any effort. It drives the overview origin because the deck's `.agent-tile:not(.is-selected) { display: none }` leaves no Open control on screen at that width.
- `overview-row-open.spec.ts` — a click on an overview row's text, away from its maximise control, opens that agent's pane, and a drag across the row's text does not. Engine-only: a real mouse drag across a prompt leaves a non-empty selection **and** still delivers a `click` to the row — counted on the row itself, so a pass cannot come from an engine that never sent one — and no pane opens; a plain click on the same row then does. The hover is the other half jsdom cannot see, since it never matches `:hover`: the row and its text show a pointer cursor, and hovering the row's text gives its open control the same computed colour, background and border as hovering the control itself, measured on a **failed** row, whose red background already hides the ordinary row hover. The row's own rules (the control opens once rather than twice, other controls keep their clicks, a selection elsewhere does not block a row) are vitest's, against selections it builds itself.
- `decks.spec.ts` — the **Settings → Decks** panel, and the only browser spec of the settings sheet. Geometry: a deck's fields lay out as the house two-column settings row. Engine-only behaviour beyond that: a bidi override planted in a settings-supplied host cannot reverse the deck label on screen, which is a question about what the engine draws and not about what the DOM holds; **Test connection** in the browser preview says what it cannot do here rather than inventing a verdict; and, since PRD #742 M6, a stored `all` selection paints as a choice rather than as nothing — the panel's chooser is built from `deckChoices` now, so the fleet token is checked, the missing detail form is replaced by a sentence saying why, and the button is disabled instead of live for a token no probe can answer.

**What it does not cover.** Enumerated rather than summarised, because this paragraph will be read later as a safety property:

- **The real Tauri window.** No `tauri-driver`, no WebDriver session, no native window. Real IPC, the `desktop://` event stream, xterm over a real PTY, window chrome, zoom and the sidecar are outside it. Closing that rung — `tauri-driver` with `WebKitWebDriver` against a native window under xvfb — is tracked as [#953](https://github.com/vfarcic/dot-agent-deck/issues/953).
- **The engines that actually ship.** Playwright's WebKit is not the WebKitGTK your distribution installs and not WKWebView, and their versions move independently. A WebKit-family bug this tier catches is very likely real for the app; a WebKit-family bug it *misses* is not evidence of absence on either shipping engine.
