- **Everything live.** Fixture data only: no daemon handshake, no snapshot stream, no attach protocol, no real agent.
- **The deck.** Most of these files are about the overview. The split is deliberately not kept here as a fraction: this bullet read "six of the ten files" while eleven existed, so count them with `ls desktop/e2e/*.spec.ts` rather than trusting a number in a sentence. The deck screen is reached by six files. Three reach it for a narrow question — `page-overflow.spec.ts` checks that it does not push the page sideways, `appearance.spec.ts` reads its canvas colour, and `deck-selector.spec.ts` drives the selector in its top bar. Three are about what is on it: `agent-terminal-input.spec.ts` (issue #1042) pins that xterm's own textarea is each tile's ONLY text input — there is no second composer beside it — and that a pane the daemon reports as history-only or as having no live target renders its terminal disabled, marked with its verdict, and carrying a readable reason; `agent-pane-overlay.spec.ts` (PRD #1105) opens a tile's agent as a full-window pane and checks the grid beneath survives it; `agent-pane-modal.spec.ts` (the same PRD's security audit) tabs around that pane and checks the deck behind it is genuinely out of reach. The tile's remaining surfaces, the Reader overlay, the handoff rail, the zoom panel and the workflow panels have no browser spec at all. The settings sheet has two, not the one this bullet used to claim: `decks.spec.ts` covers its **Decks** panel and nothing else in it, and `settings-rows.spec.ts` measures the two-column row in every panel the registry offers and asserts nothing else about any of them.
- **Keyboard reach, and the narrow breakpoint almost everywhere.** One file tabs through a screen, and it is not about that screen: `agent-pane-modal.spec.ts` presses Tab to prove the agent pane CONTAINS focus, so what it enumerates is the pane's own tab order and not the deck's. No spec walks a screen's controls in order, checks that every one is reachable, or checks that any of them carries a visible focus ring. Every spec but one stays at or above 900px — deliberately, since below 680px the padding and the status pips change. The exception is `agent-pane-narrow.spec.ts` at 400x780, and it asserts two things about the agent pane at that width and nothing about any other surface there.
- **Non-Linux runners.** The CI job runs on `ubuntu-latest` only, so nothing here has ever run against a macOS or Windows browser build.

**Running it locally.** From `desktop/`:

```sh
pnpm exec playwright install --with-deps chromium webkit   # one-time; `--with-deps` needs sudo and is for WebKit
pnpm test:browser                                          # both engines
pnpm test:browser --project=chromium                       # one engine
pnpm test:browser overview-scroll                          # one file
pnpm test:browser:types                                    # type-check the specs; Playwright itself does not
```

The install downloads 399 MiB and unpacks to about 927 MB under `~/.cache/ms-playwright`. `--with-deps` runs `apt-get` for WebKit's GTK4 and GStreamer runtime — ~130 packages, measured locally on Ubuntu 26.04 rather than on the `ubuntu-latest` image CI uses, so the exact set depends on what the image already carries — and Chromium needs almost none of it. `@playwright/test` is pinned to an exact version in `package.json` rather than carrying a caret, because a Playwright version selects the browser revisions it downloads: the pin is what makes "which engine ran" a fact you can read off the lockfile. Renovate still bumps it; a bump moves the engines with it, which is the point.

There is no `pnpm build` to run first: the Playwright `webServer` builds and serves in one command, so the bundle under test is always current. A failing run writes a trace and a screenshot under `desktop/test-results/`; open one with `pnpm exec playwright show-trace <path>`.

**One devbox gotcha.** Inside a `devbox shell` or `devbox run`, nix's `glibc-bin` puts its own `ldconfig` ahead of the system one on `PATH`, and that `ldconfig` reads a cache file that does not exist. Playwright's host-requirement check then reports every shared library as missing — `libglib-2.0.so.0` included, on a machine that plainly has it — and refuses to launch WebKit. The libraries are fine; the lookup is not. Put the system one first for the run:

```sh
env PATH="/usr/bin:/bin:$PATH" pnpm test:browser
```

`PLAYWRIGHT_SKIP_VALIDATE_HOST_REQUIREMENTS=1` also works, but it suppresses the check rather than fixing it, so a genuinely missing library then surfaces as a browser crash instead of a clear message.

**Where it runs.** The `desktop-browser` job in `.github/workflows/ci.yml`, on every PR, under the same `changes` gate as `desktop-web`. It is **advisory**: the ruleset requires exactly `build`, `build-macos`, `build-windows` and `security` (`CLAUDE.md` rule 8), and this job is new enough that its stability on a GitHub runner is unmeasured — the same reason `e2e-deterministic` was added advisory. Making it required needs a run history plus a deliberate `scripts/apply-branch-protection.sh` change, which is a repository-administration act rather than something a PR does.

**Its relationship to the palette guards.** They are not redundant and neither replaces the other. `xtask/linkage-check/src/desktop_palette.rs` reads the stylesheet as text — no hard-coded colour outside the palette, the three `:root` blocks agreeing — and runs in the **required** `build` job. This tier resolves colours in an engine and runs in an advisory one. PRD #743's 399-declaration comparison in `chrome-headless-shell` was a one-off measurement rather than a checked-in gate, and nothing here reimplements it.

## Verification commands

Run frontend tests and the production web build from `desktop/`:

```sh
pnpm test
pnpm build
```

Run the browser tier from `desktop/` — the jsdom suite above answers no question about layout, scroll or painted colour, so this is a separate obligation rather than a nicer way to run the same tests. It needs a one-time browser install; [The browser tier](#the-browser-tier) has that, the devbox `ldconfig` gotcha, and what it does and does not cover:

```sh
pnpm test:browser         # Chromium and WebKit, against the production bundle
pnpm test:browser:types   # type-check the specs, which Playwright does not
```

Run the desktop Rust bridge tests and lints from the repository root:

```sh
cargo test --locked -p dot-agent-deck-desktop
cargo fmt --check
cargo clippy -p dot-agent-deck-desktop --all-targets -- -D warnings
```

Run the repository-wide required fast gates from the repository root. Both flags on the clippy line and `--workspace` on the test alias are load-bearing — `CLAUDE.md` rules 2 and 5 explain each of them:

```sh
cargo fmt --check
cargo clippy --workspace --all-targets --features e2e,e2e-live -- -D warnings
cargo test-fast
```

There is no full-tier obligation before a PR — CI runs lane 1 on every PR (CLAUDE.md rule 5, [`e2e-lanes.md`](e2e-lanes.md)). What you do owe is the tests covering what you changed, so where a desktop change touches a PTY or real-agent path, run those by filter:

```sh
cargo test-e2e <filter>        # lane 1, no credentials needed
cargo test-e2e-live <filter>   # lane 2, needs your own agent credentials — and runs in no CI job
```

### A real agent, end to end

The live manual smoke check is: build the matching CLI, launch `pnpm tauri dev`, use **Start deck** if needed, launch the configured live loop against a disposable project/worktree, confirm every role hydrates under one orchestration, interact with a real agent in an embedded terminal, resize the window and terminal, reconnect without duplicated output, and stop only a disposable agent through the confirmation dialog. Do not treat the fixture as proof that a real agent or daemon path works.

Then, with that same live daemon still owning those agents, extend it over the overview. This half is the only check that exercises the attach model against real sockets, since no automated harness drives the real window:

1. **Land on the overview.** Click **Overview** in the rail. Confirm the fleet renders grouped — standalone panes first, then the orchestration you launched as one card with its roles in role order and its coordinator badged — and that the counts in the header are real numbers rather than `—`. The deck card's header should read `Local deck` with no socket filename and no `Deck responding` beside it; hover the name and the full socket path is there. If your desktop and daemon builds differ, the two stamps are on that same hover.
2. **Confirm zero terminals stay attached while it is up.** The app has no readout for this, so observe it from outside: the desktop process's open connections to the attach socket (`DOT_AGENT_DECK_ATTACH_SOCKET`, via `lsof -p <desktop pid>`) should fall to zero. Arriving here from a deck full of tiles is the case that matters — the warm set flushes to zero rather than down to `MAX_WARM_TERMINALS`, so a lingering three would be a regression rather than by design. Give the fire-and-forget declaration a moment to land before concluding anything.
3. **Read the honest columns.** Tick every column in the **Columns** picker first — the screen opens on four, and this step is about the other five. Confirm the **CLI** column names a binary you could type — `claude`, not `claude_code` — for every agent, and check the picker dismisses when you press the pointer down outside it and closes rather than reopening when you click its own button. Press **Escape** with it open too, and on macOS that one is the point of the step: dismissal is a React `onKeyDown` on the picker root rather than a document listener, so it depends on the click having moved focus inside the picker — measured to hold in Chromium and in Playwright's WebKit, while Safari is documented not to focus a `<button>` on click and WKWebView is an engine no tier runs. [#957](https://github.com/vfarcic/dot-agent-deck/issues/957) records that as **unverified on WKWebView** rather than broken, and this walk is what would settle it. **Restore defaults** should put the four back. Active tool and tool count should move as a real agent works. **Last activity** should read `just now` for the agent you are driving and a real age for one you left alone — never `just now` for all of them at once. A blank cell means the daemon reported no live session for that agent at all (`AgentRecord.live` absent), which is expected for one that has not yet emitted a hook event and is *not* the same thing as an agent that has been quiet. **Uptime** should be populated for *every* agent the daemon spawned, including one whose Last activity is blank — that pairing is the whole reason it comes off the registry record rather than off the session. Drive a `clear = true` delegate and watch that worker's uptime reset while the orchestrator's keeps climbing.
4. **Choose columns and leave.** Untick a column and confirm it disappears from the legend, every group card's header row and every row at once — one template, one scroll region. (Narrowing until the table overflows and checking that it scrolls **as a whole**, with the cards and the legend in step, used to be here too. `overview-scroll.spec.ts` in [the browser tier](#the-browser-tier) now asserts it in both engines on every PR, so it has been removed rather than duplicated — a checklist that repeats what a test already does is a checklist people stop running.) Then quit the app, reopen it and return to the overview: the choice is still there. Check the fixture and live modes do not share it — `?fixture=1`'s columns are stored under a different key. (Both halves are now asserted in both engines by `column-persistence.spec.ts`, but this step **stays**, unlike the scroll one above. That spec survives a page reload inside one browser process; what you are checking here is that the choice survives the packaged app being quit and relaunched, in the webview the app actually ships with. Those are different questions, and the tier answers only the first.)
5. **Drill back to the deck with terminals still working.** Click **Open deck**, confirm every tile re-attaches and streams, and type into one to see it reach the agent. Coming back from the overview costs a replay **by design** — the empty declaration flushed the warm set to zero — so do not read that as a regression. The warm set is exercised without leaving the deck: switch one tile off its terminal tab and back, and it must return with no scrollback replay; do that for more tiles than `MAX_WARM_TERMINALS` and the least-recently-left one pays a replay when it is evicted.
6. **Stop the daemon under a live app** (`dot-agent-deck daemon stop`, from a shell — the app exposes no such action). The overview must switch to the disconnected note with the list blank rather than stale, and the header instruments must read `—` rather than `0` — the app keeps the previous snapshot's agents, so a `0` there is a regression. Reconnect and confirm the fleet comes back. A daemon's agents are its own children and it persists no session state, so a *restarted* daemon owns nothing and correctly lands on the zero-agent first-run screen rather than on a fleet with blank activity cells.

### Voice control, which no automated tier reaches at all

The three tiers between them drive the surface in jsdom, the surface in two real browser engines over the fixture bridge, and every Rust stage over stubs. **None of them opens a device, reaches a model, or runs inside the real Tauri window.** So this walk is the only thing that exercises the feature as a user meets it, and every step below the first is one no test anywhere can replace. **Since M6 was rewritten to voice only there is no typed path to fall back on**, so step 1 is now the gate on all the rest: if the microphone does not open, nothing further in this walk is reachable.

1. **The unavailable path, with nothing usable configured.** `transcription = "off"` is gone and the default is `local`, so reach this state by leaving the speech container **not running**. Press **Voice** and confirm the report names the `docker run` to type rather than reporting an outage. This is what a fresh install meets first if it has not started the container, so a regression here is the whole feature reading as broken. Then start the container and press Voice again **without restarting the app** — availability is read at the press, and the point of the step is that it takes effect immediately.
2. **The microphone, on whatever platform you have.** With a working speech backend — the local container, or `remote` with a key stored in Settings → Voice — press **Voice**. **Confirm the OS permission prompt appears the first time** — this is [unverified on every platform](#what-is-not-verified-on-any-platform) and is the reason this step exists. On macOS do it in a **packaged** build as well as under `tauri dev` if you can: whether a dev window needs `NSMicrophoneUsageDescription` too is PRD #802's Open Question 2, a denial there is silent, and `build-macos` is the only job that will ever touch that file.
3. **Continuous operation, which is the thing the rewrite is about.** With Voice on, say `show me the overview`, wait, and confirm the app navigates and renders `Opening the agent overview.` Then — **without pressing anything** — say `go back` and land on the deck. Confirm the button still reads *Voice on* throughout. Check the reported latency is a real number rather than a spinner that never resolves; around a second is either command preset behaving normally — measured medians are 818 ms on the OpenAI default and 780 ms on the Anthropic one. **This is where a VAD misjudgement shows up**, and it is the one thing the stubbed unit tests cannot tell you: if commands are being cut off mid-word, `SPEECH_FLOOR` is too high for your microphone; if nothing ever resolves and every utterance ends at the cap, it is too low for your room. Step 3a is how you tell which, with a number rather than by feel.
3a. **Read the refusal's measurement, which is the only calibration of `SPEECH_FLOOR` this repository has.** Speak one very short syllable — *"hm"* — and let the utterance close. The row must refuse it, and the refusal names which of the two causes it was. If it says **too quiet** — *the loudest moment reached 410 where 600 counts as speech* — then nothing you say at that level will ever be transcribed at any length, and the number is the evidence: either raise the input (move closer, turn the device's level up) or `SPEECH_FLOOR` is wrong for this microphone and wants changing in `voice/capture.rs`. If it says **I did not hear enough** — *80 ms of speech inside the loudest 200 ms, where 120 ms is needed* — the level is fine and only the length was short, which is the expected answer for one syllable. Now say a real command and confirm it is **not** refused. Report both numbers if you file anything: PRD #802's original report was *"it says Nothing was said when it does not understand"* with no measurement attached, and separating the gate's defect from the floor's cost a round trip that one refusal would now answer. `SPEECH_FLOOR` is deliberately not calibrated by any test here — [no tier in this repository opens a device](#voice-control-which-no-automated-tier-reaches-at-all) — so this step is the whole of it.

4. **The refusal paths, which are the point of the design.** From the **deck**, ask for something that exists but is not callable there — `close this agent` — and confirm you get the table's own `unavailable_hint` naming the prerequisite, not "I don't know how to do that". Then ask for something outside the closed set — `what time is it` — and confirm the no-match shows **your transcript verbatim**. Both sentences are rendered by the app from the table; if either ever reads like a model wrote it, something has crossed the stage-6 line.
5. **The 30-second cap, which discards.** With Voice on, talk continuously — read a paragraph aloud with no pause in it. At 30 s the report must say the segment was thrown away and Voice must stay **on**. Confirm no transcription happened: nothing appears in the report about what you said, because nothing was sent. If you see a transcript or a no-match sentence here, the cap is transcribing and the paid-call-per-accident problem is back.
6. **Turning it off releases the device.** Press **Voice** while it is listening and confirm the label returns to *Voice off*, `aria-pressed` is false, and your OS stops showing the microphone as in use. Nothing you were part-way through saying should be transcribed — the toggle cancels, it does not stop-and-send. Watch the order rather than only the end state: the label must pass through *Voice stopping…* and reach *Voice off* **after** the OS indicator goes out, never before. On a fast machine that transition is a few milliseconds and easy to miss; what you are checking is that it is never the other way round.
7. **Losing the webview under a live microphone**, which is what the audit blocker was about and what nothing automated can reach: no tier here runs inside the real Tauri window. With Voice **on** and your OS showing the microphone in use, force a hard reload of the webview (in `tauri dev`, the devtools' reload). Confirm the OS indicator goes out — that is `on_page_load(Started)` calling `release_microphone` — and that the panel that comes back reads `Voice off` rather than `Voice on` over nothing. Then press **Voice**: it must open a microphone rather than report *a recording is already running*, which is what a session nobody cancelled leaves behind. Do the same for the window: with Voice on, quit the app and confirm the indicator goes out immediately rather than after the terminals have finished detaching.
8. **Undo.** Speak a navigation, then press **Undo** inside ten seconds and confirm it puts the screen back. Then say `open settings` — which opens an overlay and moves no view — and confirm **no Undo is offered**, because there is nothing to reverse.
9. **The peer exemption, which is the one that has regressed once already.** Open an agent's pane so it is modal, then click the **Voice** button: it must be clickable *and* tabbable while every other background control is neither, and an Undo in the report beside it must be clickable too. Say `close this` and confirm the view closes. `close_agent_view` is the only row whose `screens` is `["agent"]`, so if the modal fence ever marks the trigger `inert` again, that row is unreachable in a shipped build and nothing automated will say so.
10. **Stopping by voice, and the row that tells you how.** Press **Voice** and, before saying anything, read the report row: it must name the phrase that lists everything **and** both ways to stop. Then say `voice off` and confirm the microphone closes exactly as the button closes it — your OS indicator goes out and the label reaches *Voice off*. Do it once from the **agent** screen as well, which is the screen that serves the fewest context members: `voice_off` is published by the voice surface rather than by a screen, and this is the step that would catch that publishing seam going missing.
11. **Discovery.** Say `what can I say?` and confirm the overlay lists commands in two groups — the ones this screen can run and the ones it cannot — and that the list matches what `commands.toml` actually holds, since it is generated from it. Close it with its own button and again with **Escape**. Open it once with an agent's pane enlarged: it must be clickable there, which is step 9's exemption covering a second element. Note that what you are reading is each row's `description`, written for a model; that is deliberate, and if it reads badly the decision to fix is about that column.
12. **Dictation, which is the one thing here that types into somebody's agent.** Pick an agent whose pane **accepts input** — not one that has finished, and not one the app shows as read-only — and say `type to the <agent>`. Confirm its pane opens and the row says *Typing to …*. Then say a sentence and watch four things: the words appear **in that agent's prompt where you can see them**, nothing is submitted, the countdown appears and runs down a second at a time, and **speaking again starts it over**. Let one countdown reach zero and confirm the prompt is submitted then and not before. Say `stop dictation` and confirm typing stops, **nothing is submitted**, what you had typed is still sitting in the prompt, and voice is still on for commands. Then aim again and say `voice off` instead, and confirm everything stops including the microphone. Finally the escape that matters most: aim, say a sentence, and press the **Voice button** during the countdown — the send must be called off with the text left in the prompt. That is the way out of a *missed* exit, where the phrase was misheard and went into the prompt instead, and it is the one step of this walk that checks the feature is recoverable rather than merely working.
13. **Then run the phrase fixtures yourself**, because no CI job does and an ordinary `cargo test-fast` never reaches a model either:

   ```sh
   DOT_AGENT_DECK_REQUIRE_REAL_E2E=1 \
     cargo test --locked -p dot-agent-deck-desktop --test voice_phrase_fixtures
   ```

   They are authoritative only for the shipping default backend on this build's own preset — `test_support::api_preset()`, currently OpenAI chat-completions on `gpt-5-mini` — and deliberately offer no backend switch, because a green run against another model would prove less than it appears to. `api_resolver` builds through `resolver_for(&IntentSettings::default())`, the same call production makes, so it follows the default wherever it goes rather than naming a protocol of its own. They need `OPENAI_API_KEY` in the environment: a developer's own key on a developer's own machine, CLAUDE.md rule 5's lane 2, and nothing registered on the repository. **Set that variable** — and keep it matched to the default if that ever moves again, because the preflight only knows whether the variable it was told to read is set. Without it a missing credential is a green runtime skip, which is the failure mode that lets a broken table sit unnoticed for weeks.

### The agent pane, and the PTY resize nobody else checks

**This is the compensating control for the biggest gap in PRD #1105's coverage, so run it as a procedure rather than as a glance.** Nothing drives the real Tauri window ([#953](https://github.com/vfarcic/dot-agent-deck/issues/953)), and what the automated tiers do assert about the resize is narrower than it sounds: they observe the **client's proposed geometry** handed to the runtime — `runtime.resizeTerminal(agentId, cols, rows)` — and nothing past it. They do not show that a real PTY moved, that a native WebKitGTK or WKWebView window measured the same cells, that the daemon's focus-driven arbitration produced the size that was asked for, or that another client attached to the same agent reflowed. [What the automated tiers prove about the resize](#what-the-automated-tiers-prove-about-the-resize-and-what-they-do-not) enumerates that boundary; the steps below are the only place any of it is checked.

Run it with `pnpm tauri dev` or a bundled build, against the same live daemon and the same real agents as the walk above — a **real agent** in the pane is load-bearing for steps 2, 3 and 6, because a pane with no live process reflows nothing. `task run-all` sets up step 6's two clients — a TUI and the desktop on one daemon — against an isolated sandbox rather than your real deck ([local-run.md](local-run.md#task-run-all-the-tui-and-the-desktop-against-one-sandbox-daemon)); read its rule 16 caution before running it from a worktree anyone is editing.

1. **Open from the deck, and confirm the grid did not go away.** Press the maximise control in a tile's header — a real `<button>` carrying the accessible name `Open <role> agent`, rather than a click handler on the tile body, so that a deck of nine reads as nine distinct controls. Nothing automated tabs through a screen in this repository, so this walk is also the place to check that the keyboard reaches it and that the name is the one announced. The pane must cover the window. Then confirm the cost of it: the other tiles' terminals must still be streaming when you come back, with **no scrollback replay** on any of them, and no burst of reconnect activity while the pane is up (`lsof -p <desktop pid>` against the attach socket should show the same count of connections before, during and after). A replay on close means something re-declared the shown set — the deck path is supposed to declare nothing at all.
2. **Watch the agent's own TUI reflow, in the pane.** The pane is the whole window rather than a ninth of it, so the agent's output should **re-lay-out to the new column count**, not stay at the old one and sit in the corner. Box drawing, status lines and wrapped paragraphs are what make this visible; a pane whose content is the right *size* but the wrong *shape* means the daemon was never told, which reads as the agent misbehaving rather than as a resize bug. Type into it and confirm the keystrokes reach the agent — the pane is a working surface, not a viewer.
3. **Resize the window while the pane is open.** Drag the window wider and narrower, and step the zoom with the keys. Each time, the agent's grid should follow within a moment. Take the window down under 680px wide too: the pane must keep its terminal and its tab strip, since the tile's own phone rules hide both and the pane overrides them. (That last one has a browser spec, `agent-pane-narrow.spec.ts`, so you are confirming it in the shipping engine rather than discovering it.)
4. **Close back to the deck.** `Esc`, and again with the close control in the pane's header. Both must land on the deck with the tile you came from in its original cell, on the **same panel tab** it was on, its scroll position and the deck's selection intact, and — again — no replay. Confirm the agent's TUI reflows *back* to tile width.
5. **Open from the overview, and close back to the overview.** Click **Overview** in the rail, then the maximise control on an agent's row. Open it a second time by clicking the row itself, away from the control, and then drag across that row's prompt or working directory: the drag must leave the text selected and open nothing. Two things to confirm that the deck path cannot tell you: the overview is still mounted underneath (`Esc` returns to it, not to the deck, and the scroll position and column choice are as you left them), and this open costs **exactly one attach** where the deck path costs none — one new connection to the attach socket while the pane is up, gone again on close. Use an agent on the deck you are on; step 7 below is the one that covers an agent on another deck, which costs no attach at all. Arriving here from a full deck is the case worth using, because the warm set was flushed to zero on the way to the overview.
6. **The reflow another client sees, which needs two clients on one agent.** This is the step the automated tiers cannot approach at all.

   - In a terminal, attach a TUI to the **same daemon** — `dot-agent-deck` with the same `DOT_AGENT_DECK_ATTACH_SOCKET`, which is the socket a TUI attaches through (`task run-all` does this for you) — and bring up the same agent's embedded pane so it is live and has reported its geometry. A current TUI declares its dimensions at attach, so this needs no coaxing; what it does need is for the pane to actually be on screen.
   - Make that terminal window **narrow** — narrower than the desktop's maximised pane, though wide enough to read. The daemon sizes the agent by whichever client claimed focus last, so the size you see depends on which window you touched last, not on which pane is smaller.
   - **Click into the desktop window, then open the pane.** The agent should take the pane's size, and **the TUI's pane should reflow, live, with nothing on its own screen explaining why** — clipped at its right edge, because it is handed the desktop's wider grid. That is the feature working and a genuine cross-client side effect, chosen knowingly. Close the pane and the TUI should reflow again to the tile's size. Two reflows per open/close cycle is the expected cost to the other viewer.
   - **Switch focus to the TUI without resizing anything:** open the pane again, then click into the terminal window or press a key there. The agent should reflow to the TUI pane's size, the TUI should now fit, and the still-open desktop pane should render at the TUI's grid with the remainder of its box unused. That is `applyAppliedGrid` working as designed — the client proposes and the daemon disposes — and it is not a bug to file. **Click back into the desktop window** and the agent should return to the pane's size. Each switch is one reflow.
   - **Switch to a third application**, such as a browser, from either client. Nothing should reflow: the rule is the *last*-focused deck client, not the currently focused one.
   - If the terminal does not report focus changes — tmux without `set -g focus-events on` — switching to it claims nothing, and the TUI takes over only on the first key or click. Check that the switch still happens then, rather than never.
   - Repeat the switch at 300% zoom in the desktop, since zoom changes the desktop's proposal without changing the window.

7. **A second deck's agents, if you have one configured.** From the overview, find an agent belonging to a deck that is *not* the selected one. Its row must carry the **same maximise control** as every other row, and pressing it must open a pane with a **live terminal**: the header, status, assignment, footer, all five tabs, and output actually arriving from the other machine. Type into it and confirm the keystrokes land on **that** agent and not on the selected deck's same-id namesake — the cheapest check is two agents that share an id, with `echo` into one and nothing appearing in the other. Check what the pane does *not* do as carefully as what it does: the Deck selector must still read what it read before, and nothing may touch `desktop.toml` (`cp` it first and `diff` after, and expect the two files to be **identical bytes**). An earlier build of this feature switched the deck for you and wrote that file twice per open; [the section above](#an-agent-on-any-reachable-deck-opens-with-a-live-terminal) says why the switch was withdrawn, and a non-empty diff here means it has come back. Then move the selection to that deck and back again with the pane open: the terminal must stay live and the pane must not flash or reopen. Finally, take that deck away — stop its daemon, or drop the ssh tunnel — and the pane must replace its terminal with a sentence naming the deck and repeating its own failure, rather than leaving a black rectangle.

8. **The pane is a real modal, and a deck-origin pane is fenced on identity.** Open a pane from the deck and press Tab a dozen times: focus must stay inside it, and the deck selector behind it must not be clickable either. Then reach the selector *without* the pane — close it, switch the selected deck to one running an agent with the same id, and check the deck-origin pane you had is gone rather than wearing the new deck's namesake. There is no supported gesture that opens a deck-origin pane and then moves the selection, which is the point: the browser tier holds the focus half (`desktop/e2e/agent-pane-modal.spec.ts`) and the vitest tier holds the fence, and what this walk adds is the real engine in the packaged app.

### Zoom, at both ends of the ladder

**This is the check that reaches the visual half of PRD #744** — see *What none of this proves* above. It is no longer quite the only one: [the agent pane walk](#the-agent-pane-and-the-pty-resize-nobody-else-checks) above steps the zoom too, and its second bullet here is the overlapping half. Everything else on this list still runs nowhere but here. Run it with `pnpm tauri dev` or a bundled build, with a **real agent** running in a pane, because two of the five things it covers are about the PTY.

- **Step to 300% and back to 100%.** The chrome must stay laid out — nothing clipped at the right edge, no surface overlapping another, the rail and the evidence drawer still usable. The app's own breakpoints should fire on the way up, so it should look like a narrowed window rather than a broken one. Then step down to 75% and check the smallest type is still readable, since several declarations are 6px at 100%.
- **Confirm the agent's terminal re-fitted rather than just re-scaled.** At each level the running agent's own TUI must repaint at the new width — its box drawing and status line should reflow to fit the pane, not stay at the old column count and wrap or clip. A pane whose content is the right *size* but the wrong *shape* means the daemon was never told, which is the failure mode this feature is most likely to ship with, and it reads as the agent misbehaving rather than as a zoom bug.
- **Confirm nothing was typed into the PTY.** Focus a terminal pane, press each zoom key several times, and check the agent's prompt is still empty — no stray `-`, `=` or `0` characters, and no undo. This is the capture-phase guarantee, and on macOS it passes for the wrong reason (`metaKey` misses xterm's branch), so **run it on Linux or Windows** for it to mean anything.
- **Confirm the level survives a restart with no flash.** Set 150%, quit, relaunch, and watch the first paint: it must come up already at 150%. A brief 100% frame before it jumps means the Rust `setup` apply is not happening and the frontend is covering for it.
- **Confirm the Zoom row and the keys agree.** Change the level with the keys, open the settings sheet, and the Zoom row should read the level you are at; change it in the row and the window should follow. They are two writers on one value, so a disagreement here is the echo guard in `useZoom` failing.
- **Confirm the browser preview says it cannot.** Open `?fixture=1` in a browser: the Zoom row must be disabled and point at the browser's own zoom, and the keys must do nothing of ours.

### Both appearances, and the settings surface

**This is the only check that runs on the WebKit builds the app actually ships with** — WebKitGTK as your distribution packages it on Linux, WKWebView on macOS. Nothing automated launches either, because nothing drives the real Tauri window ([#823](https://github.com/vfarcic/dot-agent-deck/issues/823) closed the browser rung of that ladder, not this one). Two behaviours were the most likely to differ from Chromium and are the reason this paragraph was written: `color-scheme: light dark` on `<meta>`, which is what makes the webview's own scrollbars, form controls and default background follow the appearance, and `theme-color` with `media` attributes, where support for the `media` selector is the part that varies. **The first of those two is now asserted on every PR in a WebKit engine** by `appearance.spec.ts` in [the browser tier](#the-browser-tier) — Playwright's own WebKit build, which is neither of the two above, so it narrows the risk there without retiring the walk. **The second is not, and cannot be from that tier**: which `theme-color` an engine resolved is not observable from inside the page (measured in both engines — no `/theme/i` property on `window`, `document`, `navigator` or `screen`, not a CSS property, nothing on `HTMLMetaElement`, and Chromium's CDP `themeColor` is the web app manifest's, not the meta's). So of those two, `media`-on-`theme-color` is the one **nothing automated answers**: the browser tier checks the metas are well-formed, `media`-scoped and undrifted, and stops there. Be precise about what that leaves, because "the manual walk covers it" would be the easy thing to write and is not quite true — no bullet below names `theme-color`, and its effect is a tint on browser UI chrome that a Tauri window may not paint at all, since that window's chrome is native rather than the webview's. The honest position is that the first bullet's "walk every surface in both appearances" would notice a webview whose own chrome stayed light, which is the `color-scheme` failure; a `theme-color` `media` attribute the engine ignored would most likely be invisible here. Closing it properly needs the real-window rung, [#953](https://github.com/vfarcic/dot-agent-deck/issues/953). Everything else on this list still runs nowhere but here.

Do the whole list twice — once with the OS set to Light and once to Dark — with `pnpm tauri dev` or a bundled build:

- **Walk every surface in both appearances.** The deck itself (agent tiles, the rail, the tab strip, the agent footer, the evidence drawer, the output reader), all four config sheets (Projects, Workflows, Profiles, Prompts), the settings sheet, the command palette (⌘K), the shortcut dialog (`?`), a confirmation dialog, the connection banner, and the failure and empty states — the fixture preview's `?state=disconnected`, `?state=error` and `?state=empty` are the cheapest way to reach the last of those, and they are exactly the surfaces least likely to be opened during development and most likely to be wrong. Nothing should be light-on-dark or dark-on-light, and no surface should stop separating from its parent.
- **Confirm the override wins in both directions.** On a dark machine choose **Light** and get a light app; on a light machine choose **Dark** and get a dark app. Both directions matter and they exercise different CSS: the media query under its `:root:not([data-theme="light"])` guard, and the `:root[data-theme="dark"]` block. Then choose **System** and confirm it follows the OS **live** — change the OS appearance with the app open and the window should follow without a restart or a reload.
- **Confirm the terminals are unchanged.** The panes stay dark in both appearances by decision. With a real agent running, its output should look exactly as it does today in light mode; the chrome around the pane themes, the pane does not.
- **Confirm the choice is on the FIRST frame, not applied a moment later.** Set an override that disagrees with the OS — Dark on a light machine, or Light on a dark one — then quit and relaunch, watching the window as it opens. It must come up in the chosen palette with no flash of the other one and no blank frame in front of it. No automated check reaches this ([#845](https://github.com/vfarcic/dot-agent-deck/issues/845)) — the automated half stops at the seam where the ordering is decided, and a paint is past it. Relaunch a few times — a flash is a race, so one clean launch is weaker evidence than it looks.
- **Confirm the choice persists and the footer tells the truth.** Set an override, quit, relaunch, and it should still be applied — before the sheet is ever opened. The sheet's footer should name a path that exists on disk (`~/.config/dot-agent-deck/desktop.toml`) and the file should be readable and hand-editable; in the browser fixture preview the same footer must say there is **no** file rather than printing a plausible-looking path.
- **Confirm the store fails softly.** Delete `desktop.toml`, then corrupt it, then make it unreadable, launching the app each time: all three should start cleanly on defaults, with no crash and no error dialog.
